Solvable Descent and the Grunwald Problem for Solvable Groups
Julian L. Demeio

Abstract
. We prove a suitable fibration theorem over quasi-trivial tori that, through an approach developed by Harpaz and Wittenberg, implies so-called solvable descent . In particular, this gives a positive answer to the Grunwald problem for solvable groups up to the necessary Brauer–Manin obstruction, providing a generalizion of Shafarevich’s positive answer to the Inverse Galois Problem for solvable groups. This also provides an alternative proof of Shafarevich’s result that avoids his “shrinking procedure”. For the fibration theorem, we first adapt the starting ideas of Shafarevich for the creation of local lifts. To deal then with the Brauer–Manin obstruction (i.e. the relevant local-to-global obstruction), we compute its “triple variation” on grids of fibers. The resulting expression is a linear combination of Redéi symbols on the base. Customizing these and employing a combinatorial principle first noted by Alexander Smith in the context of Class and Selmer Groups, one infers the vanishing of the obstruction in at least one fiber.



1.
Introduction

We prove a fibration theorem (Theorem
1.4
) that combined with earlier work of Harpaz and Wittenberg
[
23
]
proves:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
X
be a homogeneous space under a linear algebraic semi-simple simply connected group over a number field
K
with finite solvable stabilizers. Then
X(K)
is dense in
X(K_{\Omega})^{\Br X}
.

This answers positively the Grunwald Problem for Solvable Groups up to the necessary Brauer–Manin obstruction, see Corollary
1.3
below. Moreover, by a result of Lucchini-Arteche
[
32
, Corollary 6.3]
on the unramified Brauer group of homogeneous spaces with finite stabilizers, Theorem
1.1
also implies a positive answer to the (tame) Grunwald problem for finite solvable group schemes:

Corollary 1.2
.

Let
G
be a solvable finite group, and
K
be a number field. Let
S
be a finite set of places of
K
not containing any finite place dividing the order of
G
, and let, for each
v\in S
,
L_{v}/K_{v}
be a Galois extension whose Galois group embeds in
G
. Then there exists a Galois extension
L/K
with group
G
such that for every
v\in S
the extension of
K_{v}
obtained by completing
L
at a place dividing
v
is isomorphic to
L_{v}/K_{v}
.

The Grunwald Problem.
Its history starts with the celebrated Grunwald–Wang theorem, i.e. Corollary
1.2
when
G
is abelian,
K=\mathbb{Q}
and
S
contains only the
2
-adic place. The important contribution of Wang
[
48
]
was to notice that the theorem does not hold with an empty
S
, by proving that no extension of
\mathbb{Q}
with group
G=\mathbb{Z}/8\mathbb{Z}
is inert over
2
. Inspired by their theorem, the Grunwald Problem asks the following question for a finite group
G
, and a number field
K
with local completions
K_{v},\ v\in M_{K}
:

(GP) Does the restriction map
\Hom(\Gamma_{K},G)/{\text{conj}}\rightarrow\prod_{v}\Hom(\Gamma_{v},G)/{\text{conj}}
have dense image?

Here
conj
stands for conjugation by elements of
G
,
\Gamma_{K}\coloneqq\Gal(\overline{K}/K)
and
\Gamma_{v}\coloneqq\Gal(\overline{K}_{v}/K_{v})
.

An interesting case where a positive answer to (GP) is known is when there is a positive answer to
Noether’s problem
for
G
. I.e. when, for one (equivalently all) embedding(s)
G\hookrightarrow GL_{n}(K)
, the quotient
\mathbb{A}^{n}/G
is stably rational. This is known to be the case for various
G
, notoriously including
G=S_{n},n\in\mathbb{N}
. See e.g.
[
50
]
for more on this topic. However, for general
G
, Noether’s problem has a negative answer
[
7
, p.223]
, and thus a different approach to (GP) is necessary.

When
K=\mathbb{Q}
and
G
is abelian, the counterexample of Wang shows that the general answer to (GP) is negative, but the Grunwald–Wang theorem indicates that the obstruction to a positive answer should be supported at finitely many places, i.e. that the closure of
\Hom(\Gamma_{K},G)/{\text{conj}}
in
\prod_{v}\Hom(\Gamma_{v},G)/{\text{conj}}
should be a product

(\star)\quad\mathcal{A}\times\prod_{v\notin S}\Hom(\Gamma_{v},G)/{\text{conj}},

where
\mathcal{A}
is a non-empty subset of
\prod_{v\in S}\Hom(\Gamma_{v},G)/{\text{conj}}
for some finite set of places
S
of
K
. The set
\mathcal{A}
was later conjecturally linked to the Brauer–Manin obstruction to weak approximation on the space
X=\SL_{n,K}/G
, for an(y) embedding
G\hookrightarrow SL_{n}(K)
. See Proposition 2.4 of
[
8
]
and the preceding discussion.
A celebrated conjecture of Colliot-Thélène and Sansuc would then imply a positive answer to the following refined version of the Grunwald Problem:

(GP
{}_{\text{BM}}
) Is the image of
\Hom(\Gamma_{K},G)/{\text{conj}}
dense in
\left(\prod_{v}\Hom(\Gamma_{v},G)/{\text{conj}}\right)^{BM}
?

We refer the reader to
[
39
, Section 3]
for a precise definition of the “Brauer–Manin set”
\left(\prod_{v}\Hom(\Gamma_{v},G)/{\text{conj}}\right)^{BM}
in the more general context of embedding problems, and we only point out here that it is a set of the form
(\star)
.

Some groups for which a positive answer to (GP) or (GP
{}_{\text{BM}}
) is known are:

(GP
{}_{\text{BM}}
)

abelian groups
G
by Wang
[
48
]
;

(GP
{}_{\text{BM}}
)

iterated semi-direct products
G=A_{1}\rtimes\left(A_{2}\rtimes\cdots\rtimes A_{r}\right)
of abelian groups by Harari
[
20
]
;

(GP)

solvable groups
G
with order prime to the number of roots of unity in
K
by Neukirch
[
37
]
;

(GP
{}_{\text{BM}}
)

supersolvable groups
G
by Harpaz and Wittenberg
[
23
]
.

Theorem
1.1
implies (GP
{}_{\text{BM}}
) for solvable groups, and the analogous statement for solvable group schemes (see e.g.
[
39
, Section 9]
for the implication):

Corollary 1.3
.

Let
G
be a finite solvable group scheme over a number field
K
. Then the restriction
H^{1}(\Gamma_{K},G)\rightarrow\left(\prod_{v}H^{1}(\Gamma_{v},G)\right)^{BM}
has dense image.

We refer again to
[
39
, Section 3]
for a precise definition of the “Brauer–Manin set”
\left(\prod_{v}H^{1}(\Gamma_{v},G)\right)^{BM}
. When
G
is a constant group scheme, then we have a natural identification
H^{1}(\Gamma_{K},G)=\Hom(\Gamma_{K},G)/{\text{conj}}
(and analog local identifications). Hence Corollary
1.3
gives, as a special case, a positive answer to (GP
{}_{\text{BM}}
) for solvable groups
G
.

Using the descent theory developed by Harpaz and Wittenberg (see
[
23
]
and Section
3
), Theorem
1.1
follows from the following fibration theorem, to whose proof the bulk of this paper is devoted:

Theorem 1.4

(Main Theorem)
.

Let
V\rightarrow Q
be a smooth morphism with rationally connected fibers over a quasi-trivial torus
Q
defined over a number field
K
. Assume there exists a
K
-torus
T
endowed with a group-epimorphism
T\rightarrow Q
, and an action on
V
such that the projection
V\rightarrow Q
is
T
-equivariant. Then:

V(K_{\Omega})_{\bullet}^{\Br_{ur}V}=\overline{\bigcup_{q\in Q(K)}V_{q}(K_{\Omega})_{\bullet}^{\Br_{ur}V_{q}}}.

A special case of Theorem
1.4
, when a certain additional splitting assumption is satisfied (see
[
23
, Théorème 4.2]
) was already proven by Harpaz and Wittenberg
[
23
]
. Harpaz and Wittenberg also proved Theorem
1.4
conditionally under their homonymous conjecture
[
22
, Conjecture 9.1]
.

Theorem
1.4
easily reduces to the case where
Q
is simple as a quasi-trivial torus, and in this case we actually prove a slightly stronger variant of the main theorem, Theorem
3.1
, with a higher degree of archimedean approximation.

The fibration method.
We follow the general pattern of the fibration method, working on a relative compactification of the fibration. Generally speaking, given a smooth proper morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
, the “fibration method” is a technique to prove that:

(
\star
)

X(K_{\Omega})^{\Br_{ur}X}=\overline{\bigcup_{y\in Y(K)}X_{y}(\mathbb{A}_{K})^{\Br X_{y}}},

although variants where the set of archimedean points is replaced by the set of their connected components are also common.

Identity (

⋆

‣
1
) is equivalent to saying that for any (arbitrarily large) finite set of places
S
of
K
, and collection of local points
(x_{v})_{v\in S}\in X(K_{S})^{\Br_{ur}X}
, there exists a
y\in Y(K)
and a Brauer–Manin unobstructed adelic point
(x^{\prime}_{v})_{v\in M_{K}}
of the fiber
X_{y}
, approximating
x_{v}
arbitrarily well for
v\in S
. One may think of this problem as being the combination of the following two parts:

i.

(
local solubility of the fibers
) finding a
y\in Y(K)
that approximates
f(x_{v}),v\in S
for which
X_{y}(\mathbb{A}_{K})\neq\emptyset
;

ii.

(
avoiding the Brauer–Manin obstruction
) ensuring that there is a
Brauer–

Manin unobstructed
adelic point
(x_{v}(y))_{v\in M_{K}}\in X_{y}(\mathbb{A}_{K})^{\Br X_{y}}
, with
y
as above, that approximates
x_{v}
for
v\in S
.

Outline of the paper and strategy of the proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Section
2
is reserved to settling notation and preliminaries.

In Section
3
, we reduce the general case of Theorem
1.4
to the case where
Q=R_{E/K}\mathbb{G}_{m}
with
E
a field and, in this case, we actually formulate a slightly stronger result, Theorem
3.1
, with a higher degree of archimedean approximation. This stronger result is what we prove in the rest of the paper.

In Section
4
, we also show how solvable descent follows from Theorem
1.4
via the work of Harpaz and Wittenberg
[
24
]
. We also use here a base-change result of theirs (see also Theorem
4.4
) to reduce Theorem
3.1
to the case where
E/K
is Galois. Sections
5
-
13
are dedicated to the proof of Theorem
3.1
in the Galois case.

In Section
5
we introduce two kinds of arithmetic symbols that play central roles in the paper: half-spin symbols and (generalized) Redéi symbols. Both arise in some fashion from the Brauer–Manin pairing on the fibers of
f
, as we later show.

In Section
6
we introduce, in a general setting, the
horizontal Brauer group

\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y):=H^{2}(Y,\tau_{\geq 1}Rf_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})

of a smooth proper morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
with geometrically integral fibers.
The main motivation behind this definition is that if
Y
is a
K
-algebraic variety and
H^{1}(X_{\bar{y}},\mu_{\infty})=H^{2}(X_{\bar{y}},\mathcal{O})=0
for geometric points
\bar{y}
of
Y
, then
\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)\cong\Br X_{y}/\im\Br K
for
y
in a Hilbertian subset
H\subset Y(K)
. I.e.,
\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)
parametrizes the Brauer groups of the fibers modulo constants in a Hilbertian set of parameters. The horizontal Brauer group sits in an exact sequence

\Br Y\xrightarrow{f^{*}}\Br X\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)\xrightarrow{\partial}H^{3}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m})\xrightarrow{f^{*}}H^{3}(X,\mathbb{G}_{m}).

The morphism
\Br X\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)
factors through
\Br X_{\eta}
, where
\eta
denotes here the generic point of
Y
, as
\Br X\subset\Br X_{\eta}\xrightarrow{r}\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)
. Finally, we also show
in this section that if
Y
is a form of a torus, the following is a complex:

(1.1)

\Br X_{\eta}\xrightarrow{r}\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)\xrightarrow{\bar{\partial}}H^{3}(Y\otimes_{K}\overline{K},\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\Gamma_{K}}.

In Section
7
we provide a general criterion for finding representatives for the quotient
\Br X_{\eta}/f^{*}\Br\eta
in
\Br X_{\eta}
with prescribed residues. We then apply this criterion to the fibration
f:X\rightarrow Q
of Theorem
3.1
(this is the relative compactification of the fibration
V\rightarrow Q
of Theorem
1.4
), in the special case that
Q=R_{E/K}\mathbb{G}_{m}
with
E
a field and
E/K
Galois.
We then compute, via functoriality of residues, the local Brauer pairing associated to the representatives just obtained, which reveals (the local components of) half-spin symbols arising, hence their treatment at the beginning of the paper.

In Section
8
we use the rational connectedness of the fibers of
f
, combined with the Theorem of Graber–Harris–Starr
[
16
]
, to produce a finite étale multisection of the shape:

s:Q^{L,m,(1)}\rightarrow X,\quad Q^{L,m,(1)}:=\Spec L[y^{\pm 1},(y^{\sigma})^{\pm\frac{1}{m}}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma\setminus\{\text{id}\}},

where
\Gamma=\Gal(E/K)
, and
\{y^{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in\Gamma}
is an equivariant basis of characters of
Q
, and
L
is a finite (field) extension of
E
. See the main text for details.

In Section
9
we reduce the general case of Theorem
3.1
to the case where some additional assumptions are satisfied:
E/K
is Galois, the complex (
1.1
) is exact, and the multisection produced in Section
8
satisfies, in addition, that
\mu_{m}\subset E^{*}
and
s^{*}B=0
for a finite subset
B\subset(\Br X)_{+}
generating the quotient
\Br X_{\eta}/f^{*}\Br\eta
. The reductions are obtained via the same base–change method of Harpaz and Wittenberg used in Section
4
.

In Section
10
, we follow the ideas of the paper
[
40
]
of Shafarevich (see also
[
36
, Theorem IX.9.3.2]
for an English version) to prove an arithmetic approximation result, which, when applied to our setting, produces parameters
q\in Q(K)
whose images in
Q(K_{v})
at large bad
v
lift to
K_{v}
-points of the multisection, and whose associated half-spin symbols vanish. We also produce such parameters
q
in the shape of grids

\mathcal{M}=q_{0}\cdot\{q_{1,1},\ldots,q_{1,M}\}\cdots\{q_{k,1},\ldots,q_{k,M}\}\subset E^{*}=Q(K),

with customazible “Redéi variations”. Our interest in these Redéi variations is that they appear in the expression for the triple variation of the Brauer–Manin pairing on the fibers associated to
\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)
, as shown later in Section
12
.

In Section
11
we use the approximation result of Section
10
and the multisection
s
to produce adelic points on the fibers above suitable grids
\mathcal{M}\subset Q(K)
.

In Section
12
we compute the triple variation on suitable grids
\mathcal{M}
of the Brauer–Manin pairing associated to an element
b\in\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)
, for the specific choice of adelic points on the fibers made in Section
11
. The resulting expression is a linear combination of the Redéi variations of the grid in the base, with linear weights depending on the class
\bar{\partial}(b)\in H^{3}(Q\otimes_{K}\overline{K},\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\Gamma_{K}}
. The computation is done using Čech cochains.

In Section
13
we conclude the proof of Theorem
3.1
under the additional assumptions to which we reduced in Section
9
. Using the results of Section
10
we produce grids above which one may construct adelic points on the respective fibers via the construction in Section
11
, and such that these adelic points are orthogonal to
\im\Br X_{\eta}
.
The key idea to avoid then the Brauer–Manin obstruction associated to
\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)/\im\Br X_{\eta}
is to employ the following baby-case of Smith’s combinatorics
[
42
, Section 7]
:

Lemma 1.5

(Smith)
.

Let
A
be a finite abelian group, and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. For every
M\in\mathbb{N}
larger than some constant
M_{0}(A)
, there exists a function
g:\{1,\ldots,M-1\}^{k}\rightarrow A
such that all functions
f:\{1,\ldots,M\}^{k}\rightarrow A
with
\Delta^{(k)}(f)=g
are surjective.

Here the
k
-th variation
operator
\Delta^{(k)}
denotes the composition
\partial_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\partial_{k}
, where
(\partial_{i}f)(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i},\ldots,a_{k})\coloneqq f(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i}+1,\ldots,a_{k})-f(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i},\ldots,a_{k})
.

We apply Smith’s lemma following this simple idea: given an “unkonwn” function
f:\{1,\ldots,M\}^{k}\rightarrow A
for which we can customize the
k
-th variation
\Delta^{(k)}
to be any function we want (for instance, the function
g
), then
f
can be forced to attain the value
0
(as it is surjective, it attains in particular the value
0
). In our case, we take for the “unknown” function the Brauer–Manin obstruction associated to
\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)/\im\Br X_{\eta}
on the fibers
X_{q}
, for
q
in a suitable grid
\mathcal{M}\cong\{1,\ldots,M\}^{3}
in
Q(K)
. Since, by the results of Section
12
, the triple variation of the Brauer–Manin obstruction is expressible as a combination of Redéi-variations of the grid
\mathcal{M}
, and these Redéi-variations are customizable by the result of Section
10
, this concludes the proof.

The paper contains three appendices. Appendix
A
is dedicated to proving a combination of Chebotarev and Hecke density Theorems, which plays an important role in giving enough archimedean approximation in Section
10
to ensure that the whole argument carries through. In Appendix
B
we give our definition of relative étale cohomology (a definition already appears in Friedlander’s book
[
12
]
, but this involves the theory of simplicial schemes, that we preferred to avoid). This is used in our computation of Section
12
to express the Brauer–Manin pairing on the fibers in terms of Čech cochains. In Appendix
C
we give a slight generalization of a Theorem of Nakaoka proving, under some technical hypothesis, that the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence of wreath products splits at the second page. This is used in some technical passages in Section
12
.

Finally, it feels important to emphasize that Smith’s combinatorics is vastly more general than Lemma
1.5
. As a small example, in the same context as the lemma one may infer that
f
attains all values in the group
A
with multiplicities that tend to be uniformly equidistributed as
M\rightarrow\infty
. See
[
42
, Section 7]
, or Section 3 of the work
[
6
]
on Class Groups by Chen, Koymans, Milovic and Pagano.

Part of the results of this paper had been previously obtained independently by Harpaz and Wittenberg in an unpublished note, as they told me in private communication. Namely, they had the results of Section
10
and applied them to create local lifts for fibration quasi-trivial tori, i.e. the content of Section
11
.

The author is thankful to Jean–Louis Colliot-Thélène for his very active interest in the paper, and several useful comments. He is also grateful to Carlo Pagano for his detailed exposition of his work with Koymans for a study group in Basel in the year 2022-2023, as the last part of this paper was inspired by the “variational” ideas appearing in this other branch of Number Theory.
Finally, he thanks Jessica Alessandrì, Elyes Boughattas, Martin Bright, Adam Morgan, Yonathan Harpaz, Peter Koymans, Jesse Pajwani, Happy Uppal, and Olivier Wittenberg for helpful and interesting private conversations.

This project was finalised while the author was a guest at the Lodha Mathematical Sciences Institute (LMSI) in Mumbai, to which he is grateful for their hospitality and for offering wonderful working conditions.

2.
Notation and terminology

2.1.
Arithmetic

Number fields.

For a number field
K
, we let
\mathcal{O}_{K}
be its ring of integers,
M_{K}
be its set of places, and
M_{K}^{\text{fin}}
(resp.
M_{K}^{\infty}
) be its set of finite (resp. infinite) places. We let
\mathbb{A}_{K}
and
\mathbb{I}_{K}
the topological groups of adéles
\mathbb{A}_{K}\coloneqq\prod^{\prime}_{v}K_{v}
and idéles
\mathbb{I}_{K}\coloneqq\prod^{\prime}_{v}K_{v}^{*}
, where the products are restricted with respect to
\mathcal{O}_{v}\subset K_{v}
and
\mathcal{O}_{v}^{*}\subset K_{v}^{*}
, respectively. We let
K_{\Omega}:=\prod_{v}K_{v}
,
K_{\text{fin}}:=\prod_{v\in M_{K}^{\text{fin}}}K_{v}
, and
K_{\infty}:=\prod_{v\in M_{K}^{\infty}}K_{v}=K\otimes\mathbb{R}
. We let
K_{\text{dir}}:=(K_{\infty}\setminus\{0\})/\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, where
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
acts by multiplication via the embedding
\mathbb{R}\subset K\otimes\mathbb{R}=K_{\infty}
.

For
v\in M_{K}^{\text{fin}}
, we let
\mathcal{P}_{v}
be the associated prime ideal in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
. For a prime ideal
\mathcal{P}
(resp. a finite place
v\in M_{K}^{\text{fin}}
) of
K
, we let
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{P}}
(resp.
\mathbb{F}_{v}
) be the residue field
\mathcal{O}_{K}/\mathcal{P}
(resp.
\mathcal{O}_{K}/\mathcal{P}_{v}
).

For a finite extension
E/K
, we let
\Delta_{E/K}
be its relative discriminant, meant as an ideal in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.
For a finite Galois extension
E/K
, and a prime
\mathcal{P}
of
E
not dividing
\Delta_{E/K}
, we denote by
\Frob_{\mathcal{P}}\in\Gal(E/K)
its Frobenius.

Hilbert symbols.

Let
n
be a natural number, and
K
be a number field containing
\mu_{n}
. For
v\in M_{K}
, we denote by:

(-,-)_{n,v}:K_{v}^{*}/(K_{v}^{*})^{n}\times K_{v}^{*}/(K_{v}^{*})^{n}\rightarrow\mu_{n}

the local Hilbert symbol of order
n
. Recall that
(a,b)_{n,v}
is defined as the image of
(a,b)
under the cup product:

K_{v}^{*}/(K_{v}^{*})^{n}\times K_{v}^{*}/(K_{v}^{*})^{n}\xrightarrow{\cup}H^{2}(K_{v},\mu_{n})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mu_{n}\xrightarrow{\inv_{v}}\mu_{n}.

Power residues.

For a prime
\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{O}_{K}
and
\alpha\in(\mathcal{O}_{K}/\mathcal{P})^{*}
, we let

\left(\frac{\alpha}{\mathcal{P}}\right)_{n}\coloneqq\left(\alpha^{\frac{N\mathcal{P}-1}{n}}\bmod\mathcal{P}\right)\in\mu_{n}

be the
n
-th power residue symbol. For
a\in K^{*}
such that
v_{\mathcal{P}}(a)=0
, we let
\left(\frac{a}{\mathcal{P}}\right)_{n}:=\left(\frac{a\bmod\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{P}}\right)_{n}
. Recall that
(\pi,a)_{n,\mathcal{P}}=\left(\frac{a}{\mathcal{P}}\right)
for any uniformizer
\pi
of
\mathcal{P}
. The
n
-th power residue symbol extends multiplicatively: for an ideal
\mathcal{I}=\prod_{i}\mathcal{P}_{i}^{e_{i}}
of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, we define

\left(\frac{\alpha}{\mathcal{I}}\right)_{n}\coloneqq\prod_{i}\left(\frac{\alpha}{\mathcal{P}_{i}}\right)^{e_{i}}.

We define
\left[\frac{\alpha}{\mathcal{P}}\right]_{n}
as the image of
\left(\frac{\alpha}{\mathcal{P}}\right)_{n}
under the natural isomorphism
\mu_{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}(1)
(and analogously for multiplicative extensions,
S
-variants, ...).
1
1

1

We shall use this for the sole purpose of having additive instead of multiplicative notation when more convenient.

Class Field Theory.

For a number field
K
, let
C_{K}:=\mathbb{I}_{K}/K^{*}
, where
K^{*}
is embedded in
\mathbb{I}_{K}
diagonally (recall that this embedding is closed and discrete).

We follow
[
21
, Section 15.4]
.
Let
\mathfrak{m}=(I_{\mathfrak{m}},S_{\mathfrak{m}})
be a cycle of
K
: i.e.
I_{\mathfrak{m}}\subset O_{K}
is an ideal and
S_{\mathfrak{m}}
is a subset of the real places of
K
. The
ray class group

\Cl_{\mathfrak{m}}O_{K}
is (with Chevalley’s adelic notation, see e.g.
[
21
, Sec. 15.4]
) the quotient:

K^{*}\backslash\mathbb{I}_{K}/U_{\mathfrak{m}},

where
U_{\mathfrak{m}}:=\prod_{v\in M_{K}}U_{v}
,
U_{v}\coloneqq\mathcal{O}_{v}^{*}
for
v\nmid I_{\mathfrak{m}},v\notin S_{\mathfrak{m}}
,
U_{v}\coloneqq\{a\in\mathcal{O}_{v}^{*}:a\equiv 1\bmod m_{v}^{k}\}
for
v\mid I_{\mathfrak{m}}
, where
m_{v}^{k}
is the maximum power of
m_{v}
dividing
I_{\mathfrak{m}}
, and
U_{v}\coloneqq\{a\in K_{v}^{*}:a>0\}
for
v\in S_{\mathfrak{m}}
. By Class Field Theory, there is a unique abelian extension
H_{\mathfrak{m}}/K
with Galois group
\Cl_{\mathfrak{m}}O_{K}

such that, for any prime
\mathcal{P}
coprime with
I_{\mathfrak{m}}
,
\mathcal{P}
is unramified in
H_{\mathfrak{m}}
and the Frobenius
\Frob(\mathcal{P})\in\Gal(H_{\mathfrak{m}}/K)=\Cl_{\mathfrak{m}}O_{K}
is the equivalence class of the element
\left(1,\ldots,1,\pi_{\mathcal{P}},1,\ldots\right)\in\mathbb{I}_{K}
, where
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}
is a uniformizer of the maximal ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}}
.

For a place
v
, an element
x\in K_{v}^{*}
, and a cycle
\mathfrak{m}
, we use the notation

x\equiv 1\bmod\mathfrak{m}

to mean that
x\in U_{v}
.

2.2.
Geometry and arithmetic geometry

Brauer–Manin pairing.

For a scheme
X
, we denote by
\Br X\coloneqq H^{2}(X,\mathbb{G}_{m})
its (cohomological) Brauer group. Recall that, if
X
is a variety defined over a number field
K
, the
Brauer–Manin pairing

(-,-)_{BM}:X(\mathbb{A}_{K})\times\Br X\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}

is defined as
((P_{v})_{v\in M_{K}},B)_{BM}\coloneqq\sum_{v}\inv_{v}(B(P_{v}))
, where
\inv_{v}:H^{2}(K_{v},\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
denotes the usual invariant map
[
21
, Thm 8.9]
. Whenever
B\in\im\Br K
or
(P_{v})\in X(K)
(diagonally embedded in
X(\mathbb{A}_{K})
),
((P_{v}),B)_{BM}=0
by the Albert-Brauer-Hasse-Noether theorem
[
41
, Sec. 5]
. It follows that
X(K)\subseteq X(\mathbb{A}_{K})^{\Br X}
, where, for
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\Br X
:

X(\mathbb{A}_{K})^{\mathcal{B}}:=\{(P_{v})\in X(\mathbb{A}_{K})\mid((P_{v}),B)_{BM}=0\text{ for all }B\in\mathcal{B}\}.

When
X
is a smooth
K
-variety, we denote by
\Br_{ur}X
its
unramified Brauer group
. This is the subgroups of elements of
\Br X
that are unramified at all places of the function field
K(X)
. (See
[
7
, Section 6.2]
for a definition.) Equivalently,
\Br_{ur}X
is the Brauer group of a(ny) smooth compactification of
X
. The Brauer–Manin pairing on a(ny) smooth compactification of
X
induces a
continuous
unramified Brauer–Manin pairing:

X(K_{\Omega})\times\Br_{ur}X\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z},

defined via
((P_{v})_{v\in M_{K}},B)_{BM}\coloneqq\sum_{v}\inv_{v}(B(P_{v}))
. As above,
X(K)
is a subset of
X(K_{\Omega})^{\Br_{ur}X}
, where, for
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\Br_{ur}X
:

X(K_{\Omega})^{\mathcal{B}}:=\{(P_{v})\in X(K_{\Omega})\mid((P_{v},B))_{BM}=0\text{ for all }B\in\mathcal{B}\}.

Quasi-trivial tori.

A
quasi-trivial torus
over a field
k
of characteristic
0
is a
k
-torus
Q
that possesses a Galois-equivariant basis
\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{d}\}
of characters. Such a basis induces an identification:

Q=\Spec\left(\overline{k}[y_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,y_{d}^{\pm 1}]\right)^{\Gamma_{k}}.

To the
\Gamma_{k}
-set
\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{d}\}
corresponds a unique étale algebra
E/k
of degree
d
. The torus
Q
is then isomorphic to the Weil-restriction
R_{E/k}\mathbb{G}_{m}
, and we use the notation
R_{E/k}\mathbb{G}_{m}
throughout the paper to denote a quasi-trivial torus whose corresponding étale algebra is
E/k
.

Quasi-trivial tori over number fields

When
Q
is as in the paragraph above, and
k=K
is a number field, the Weil-restriction
R_{\mathcal{O}_{E}/\mathcal{O}_{K}}\mathbb{G}_{m}
, where
\mathcal{O}_{E}
denotes the ring of integers in the étale algebra
E
, provides a canonical arithmetic model for
Q
over
\mathcal{O}_{K}
. We denote this model by
\mathcal{Q}
.

When
E
is a field, we also let
Q(K_{\infty})_{\text{dir}}
be the quotient of
Q(K_{\infty})=E_{\infty}^{*}=\prod_{w\in M_{E}^{\infty}}E_{w}^{*}
by the image of
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
under the diagonal embedding
\mathbb{R}_{>0}\hookrightarrow\prod_{w\in M_{E}^{\infty}}E_{w}^{*}
. For a finite set of places
S
of
K
containing the archimedean ones, we let
Q(K_{S})_{\text{dir}}=\prod_{v\in S\cap M_{K}^{\text{fin}}}Q(K_{v})\times Q(K_{\infty})_{\text{dir}}
.

Galois quasi-trivial tori.

A quasi-trivial torus
Q
is
Galois quasi-trivial
if
E/k
is a Galois field extension. Equivalently, if
\Gamma_{k}
acts transitively on
\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{d}\}
with normal stabilizers. We call
\Gamma=\Gal(E/k)
the
Galois group
of the torus
Q
. In the Galois case, we frequently denote the Galois-equivariant basis of characters by
\{y^{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in\Gamma}
and the corresponding torus as
Q=\Spec\left(\overline{k}[(y^{\sigma})^{\pm 1}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma}\right)^{\Gamma_{k}}.

Hilbert sets and Hilbertian sets.

Following
[
29
, Sec. 9.5]
and
[
22
, Section 1]
, given a variety
X
defined over a field
k
, we say that a subset
H\subset X
is
Hilbert
if there exists a dense open
U\subset X
, and finitely many finite étale irreducible covers
\rho_{i}:V_{i}\rightarrow U
such that
H
is the set of points
x
of
X
where all the fibers
\rho_{i}^{-1}(x)
are connected. We stress that here
H
is not a subset of
X(k)
, as it contains, for instance, the generic point of
X
. We say that a subset
H\subset X(k)
is
Hilbertian
if it is the intersection of a Hilbert set with
X(k)
.

Picard functor.

For a base scheme
S
, and an
S
-scheme
f:X\rightarrow S
, we indicate by
\Pic_{X/S}
the
relative Picard sheaf
on
S_{\acute{E}t}
, i.e. the sheafification of the presheaf
T\mapsto\Pic(X\times_{S}T)/f_{T}^{*}\Pic T

[
27
]
. This sheaf is equal to the derived pushforward
R^{1}f^{\text{big}}_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}
, where
f^{\text{big}}_{*}:X_{\acute{E}t}\rightarrow S_{\acute{E}t}
denotes the pushforward on the big étale site.

Recall that, when
X\rightarrow S
is flat projective with geometrically integral fibers and
S
is locally noetherian, this sheaf is represented by an
S
-scheme locally of finite type
[
18
, p. 232-06, Theorem 3.1]
, which we also denote, with a slight abuse of notation, by
\Pic_{X/S}
.

2.3.
Derived categories and cohomologies

Homological algebra.

We follow
[
45
, Ch. III]
. We denote by
\Ch(\mathcal{A})
(resp.
K(\mathcal{A}),

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}),

\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathcal{A})
) the category of chain complexes in an abelian category
\mathcal{A}
(resp. its homotopy category, its derived category, and its bounded below subcategory). For a double complex
(C^{\bullet,\bullet},d_{1},d_{2})
, we denote by
\Tot^{\bullet}=\Tot^{\bullet}(C^{\bullet,\bullet})
its totalizing complex defined by
\Tot^{n}=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}C^{p,q},d=\sum_{p+q=n}d_{1}+(-1)^{p}d_{2}
.

For
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we denote by
A[n]\in\Ch(\mathcal{A})
the complex that is
0
in position different from
n
and
A
in the
n
-th position.

For
C^{\bullet}\in\Ch(\mathcal{A})
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we let
\tau_{\geq n}C^{\bullet}
be the corresponding
truncation
of
C^{\bullet}
, defined by

\left(\tau_{\geq n}C\right)^{i}:=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }i<n\\
\Coker(C^{n-1}\rightarrow C^{n})&\text{ if }\mathrm{i}=n\\
C^{i}&\text{ if }i>n.\end{cases}

We let also
\tau_{<n}C^{\bullet}:=C^{\bullet}/\left(\tau_{\geq n}C^{\bullet}\right)
, and
\tau_{[n,m]}C^{\bullet}:=\tau_{\leq m}\tau_{\geq n}C^{\bullet}
for
n\leq m
.

Sites and cohomology.

For a scheme
S
, we denote by
S_{\acute{e}t}
its small étale site. The notation
H^{\bullet}(S,F)
will always indicate
étale cohomology
of the étale sheaf
F/S
.

Coverings and Čech cochains.

A
covering

\mathcal{U}
of a scheme
S
will always mean an étale covering, i.e. a collection
\{\varphi_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow S\}_{i\in I}
where each
U_{i}\rightarrow S
is étale and
\bigcup_{i}\varphi_{i}(U_{i})=S
. A
refinement

\psi:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
of coverings
\mathcal{V}=\{V_{j}\rightarrow S\}_{j\in J},\,\mathcal{U}=\{\varphi_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow S\}_{i\in I}
is the collection of a map
\tau:J\rightarrow I
, and
S
-morphisms
\psi_{j}:V_{j}\rightarrow U_{\tau(j)}
for all
j
. The
category of coverings of
S
is the category whose objects are coverings and whose morphisms are refinements.

The
Čech comple
x
\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{U}/S,F)
of an étale sheaf
F/S
with respect to a covering
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\rightarrow S\}_{i\in I}
is defined by

\check{C}^{p}(\mathcal{U}/S,F):=\prod_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{p}}F(U_{i_{0}\cdots i_{p}}),\ \ d(s)_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{p+1}}:=\sum_{j=0}^{p+1}s_{i_{0},\ldots,\widehat{i_{j}},\ldots,i_{p+1}}|_{U_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{p+1}}},

where
U_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{p}}:=U_{i_{0}}\times_{S}\cdots\times_{S}U_{i_{p}}
.

Given two refinements
\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}:\mathcal{U}_{1}\rightrightarrows\mathcal{U}_{2}
, their associated refinement maps
\varphi^{*},(\varphi^{\prime})^{*}:C^{\bullet}(\mathcal{U}_{2}/S,P)\rightarrow C^{\bullet}(\mathcal{U}_{1}/S,P)
are homotopic. A (natural) homotopy is
[
34
, p.96]
:

\varphi^{*}-(\varphi^{\prime})^{*}=\d K_{\varphi_{1},\varphi^{\prime}}+K_{\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}}\d,\quad K_{\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}}:C^{\bullet}(\mathcal{U}_{2}/S,P)\rightarrow C^{\bullet-1}(\mathcal{U}_{1}/S,P),

(2.1)

(K_{\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}}s)_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{p-1}}=\sum_{0\leq r\leq p-1}\,(-1)^{r}s_{\tau(i_{0}),\ldots,\tau(i_{r}),\tau^{\prime}(i_{r}),\ldots,\tau^{\prime}(i_{p-1})}|_{U_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{p-1}}},

where the restriction denotes pullback along
(\varphi_{i_{0}},\ldots,\varphi_{i_{r}},\varphi^{\prime}_{i_{r}},\ldots,\varphi_{i_{p-1}})
. When
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi
,
K_{\varphi,\varphi}
is
2
-homotopically trivially. I.e. one has

(2.2)

\d K^{(2)}_{\varphi}+K^{(2)}_{\varphi}\d=K_{\varphi,\varphi},

for a
2
-homotopy
K^{(2)}_{\varphi}:C^{\bullet}(\mathcal{U}_{2}/S,P)\rightarrow C^{\bullet-2}(\mathcal{U}_{1}/S,P)
. Explicitly, one may take

(K^{(2)}_{\varphi}s)_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{p-2}}=\sum_{0\leq r\leq p-2}\,(-1)^{r}s_{\tau(i_{0}),\ldots,\tau(i_{r}),\tau(i_{r}),\tau(i_{r}),\ldots,\tau(i_{p-1})}|_{U_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{p-1}}},

where the restriction denotes pullback along
(\varphi_{i_{0}},\ldots,\varphi_{i_{r}},\varphi_{i_{r}},\varphi_{i_{r}},\ldots,\varphi_{i_{p-2}})
.
(We leave the straightforward computation verifying (
2.2
) to the interested reader).

Galois coverings.

When
\mathcal{U}
consists of a single Galois finite étale cover
S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
, there is a natural identification
[
34
, Example III.2.6]
:

(2.3)

\check{C}^{\bullet}(S^{\prime}/S,F)=\check{C}^{\bullet}(G,F(S^{\prime})),\,G:=\Gal(S^{\prime}/S).

We stress that this is really an identification of chain complexes and not just of the associated cohomology groups!

2.4.
Topology

Closure.

For a subset
A
of a topological space, we denote by
\overline{A}
its closure in said space.

Direction.

The
direction
of a vector
v\neq 0
in a real vector space
V
is its image in
(V\setminus\{0\})/\mathbb{R}_{>0},
where
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
acts by scalar multiplication.

Factorization into relative connected components.

For a proper continuous map
f:X\rightarrow Y
between locally compact Hausdorff topological spaces, we let
X\rightarrow\pi_{0,rel}(X/Y)

\rightarrow Y
be its factorization obtained by collapsing the connected components of the fibers, i.e. such that
X\rightarrow\pi_{0,rel}(X/Y)
has connected fibers and
\pi_{0,rel}(X/Y)\rightarrow Y
has totally disconnected fibers. (This is also known as the
monotone-light
factorization. See e.g.
[
2
]
for a proof of its existence, or
[
47
, Theorem 1]
for a statement in English.)

Covering spaces.

A continuous map
f:X\rightarrow Y
between topological spaces is a
covering space
if, locally on
Y
, it decomposes as a direct product
Y\times A
with
A
discrete.

3.
Main Theorem, stronger variant

We actually prove the following stronger version of Theorem
1.4
in the case where
E
is a field. The only improvement is that the theorem below gives a higher degree of archimedean approximation.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
E/K
be a finite extension of number fields, and
Q=R_{E/K}\mathbb{G}_{m}
the corresponding quasi-trivial torus. Let
f:X\rightarrow Q
be a smooth proper morphism with rationally connected fibers. Assume there exists a torus
T
endowed with a group-epimorphism
T\rightarrow Q
, and an action on
X
such that
f
is
T
-equivariant. Then:

X(K_{\Omega})_{dir}^{\Br_{ur}X}=\overline{\bigcup_{q\in Q(K)}X_{q}(\mathbb{A}_{K})_{\bullet}^{\Br X_{q}}}.

Here
X(K_{\infty})_{dir}:={\pi_{0,rel}}(X(K_{\infty})/Q(K_{\infty}))/\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, where the implied
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-action on
{\pi_{0,rel}}(X(K_{\infty})/Q(K_{\infty}))
is the unique one lifting the diagonal
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-action on
\prod_{w\mid\infty}E_{w}^{*}=Q(K_{\infty})
. This lift does exist and it is unique because
X(K_{\infty})\rightarrow Q(K_{\infty})
is a topological fiber bundle with compact smooth fibers by Ehresmann’s theorem
[
46
, Theorem 9.3]
(which applies because
f
is smooth and proper with a smooth base) and thus
{\pi_{0,rel}}(X(K_{\infty})/Q(K_{\infty}))\rightarrow Q(K_{\infty})
is a finite covering space.

Proposition 3.2
.

Theorem
3.1
implies Theorem
1.4
. More specifically:

(1)

If Theorem
3.1
holds, then Theorem
1.4
holds when
E
is a field.

(2)

If Theorem
1.4
holds when
E
is a field, then Theorem
1.4
holds always.

Proof of (1).

By Hironaka’s strong resolution of singularities
[
10
]
, we may find a relative compactification
f:X\rightarrow Q
where the
T
-action extends to
X
and
f
is
T
-equivariant.
Take now
((x_{v})_{v\in M_{K}^{\text{fin}}},x_{\infty})\in(V(K_{\text{fin}})\times V(K_{\infty}))^{\Br_{ur}V}=V(K_{\Omega})^{\Br_{ur}V}
. Theorem
3.1
gives

X(K_{\Omega})_{dir}^{\Br_{ur}(X)}=\overline{\bigcup_{q\in Q(K)}X_{q}(\mathbb{A}_{K})_{\bullet}^{\Br X_{q}}},

so there exists
q\in Q(K)
and
((x_{v}(q))_{v\in M_{K}^{\text{fin}}},x_{\infty}(q))\in X_{q}(\mathbb{A}_{K})^{\Br X_{q}}
whose image in
X(K_{\Omega})_{dir}
approximates that of
((x_{v})_{v\in M_{K}^{\text{fin}}},x_{\infty})
. For non-archimedean
v
, let
x_{v}(q)^{\prime}
be a point arbitrarily close to
x_{v}(q)
, but lying in
V_{q}
. We claim that there exists a point
x_{\infty}(q)^{\prime}\in V_{q}(K_{\Omega})
lying in the same connected component of
V(K_{\Omega})
as
x_{\infty}
, and lying on the same connected component of
X_{q}(K_{\Omega})
as
x_{\infty}(q)
. The claim concludes the proof, as then the point
((x_{v}(q)^{\prime})_{v},x_{\infty}(q)^{\prime})\in V_{q}(K_{\text{fin}})\times V_{q}(K_{\infty})=V_{q}(K_{\Omega})\subset X_{q}(\mathbb{A}_{K})
is orthogonal to
\Br X_{q}=\Br_{ur}V_{q}
by continuity of the Brauer–Manin pairing, and thus lies in
V_{q}(K_{\Omega})^{\Br_{ur}V_{q}}
and provides the sought approximation.

Let us now prove the claim. For an element
x\in X(K_{\infty})
, we denote by
[x]
its image in
\pi_{0,rel}(X(K_{\infty})/Q(K_{\infty}))
.
The image of
x_{\infty}(q)
in
X(K_{\infty})_{dir}
is close to that of
x_{\infty}
. I.e. there exists
t_{q}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
t_{q}^{-1}\cdot[x_{\infty}(q)]
is arbitrarily close to
[x_{\infty}]
. In particular, projecting to
Q(K_{\infty})
, we have that
q\cdot t_{q}^{-1}
is arbitrarily close to
f(x_{\infty})
.
Thus, by the inverse function theorem, there exists
\tilde{x}_{\infty}\in X_{t_{q}^{-1}\cdot q}(K_{\infty})
arbitrarily close to
x_{\infty}
.

Let now
\alpha:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow T(K_{\infty})
be a lift (as a homomorphism) of the diagonal map
\iota_{diag}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow Q(K_{\infty})
along the epimorphism
\pi:T(K_{\infty})\rightarrow Q(K_{\infty})
. Such a lift is provided, for instance, by the function
\alpha(t):=\exp_{Lie}(\log(t)\cdot w)
, where
w\in((\d\pi)_{e})^{-1}(v)
denotes an inverse image along the (surjective) differential
(\d\pi)_{e}:T_{e}(T(K_{\infty}))\rightarrow T_{e}(Q(K_{\infty}))
of the unique tangent vector
v\in T_{e}(Q(K_{\infty}))
at the origin
e
of
Q(K_{\infty})
such that
\iota_{diag}(t)=\exp_{Lie}(\log(t)\cdot v)
. Here “
\exp_{Lie}
” denotes the exponential map of a Lie group.

Define
x_{\infty}(q)^{\prime}:=\alpha(t_{q})\cdot\tilde{x}_{\infty}\in X(K_{\infty})
. By construction, this point projects to
q\in Q(K_{\infty})
, and thus lies in
X_{q}(K_{\infty})
. Moreover, since
x_{\infty}\in V(K_{\infty})
and
V
is preserved by the
T
-action, we also have
x_{\infty}(q)^{\prime}\in V_{q}(K_{\infty})
, and, since
\alpha(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
acts trivially on the set of connected components of
V(K_{\infty})
, it lies in the same connected component of
V(K_{\infty})
as
x_{\infty}
. Finally, note that the action
(t,x)\mapsto\alpha(t)\cdot x
of
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
on
X(K_{\infty})
preserves the connected components of the fibers and thus descends to an action on
\pi_{0,rel}(X(K_{\infty})/Q(K_{\infty}))
. This action lifts the
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-action on
Q(K_{\infty})
by construction, and so is the
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-action with which we had already endowed
\pi_{0,rel}(X(K_{\infty})/Q(K_{\infty}))
.

Hence
t_{q}^{-1}\cdot[x_{\infty}(q)^{\prime}]=[\tilde{x}_{\infty}]
, and this is close to
[\tilde{x}_{\infty}]
and thus also to
t_{q}^{-1}\cdot[x_{\infty}(q)]
. So,
t_{q}^{-1}\cdot[x_{\infty}(q)^{\prime}]
is close to
t_{q}^{-1}\cdot[x_{\infty}(q)]
. However, both lie in the same (finite, and thus discrete) fiber of
{\pi_{0,rel}}(X(K_{\infty})/Q(K_{\infty}))\rightarrow Q(K_{\infty})
above
t_{q}^{-1}\cdot q
, and so in fact
t_{q}^{-1}\cdot[x_{\infty}(q)^{\prime}]=t_{q}^{-1}\cdot[x_{\infty}(q)]
and
[x_{\infty}(q)^{\prime}]=[x_{\infty}(q)]
, i.e.
x_{\infty}(q)^{\prime}
and
x_{\infty}(q)
lie on the same connected component of
X_{q}(K_{\Omega})
, proving the claim as wished.
∎

Proof of (2).

We proceed by induction on
[E:K]
. Assume
E
is not a field, write
E=E_{1}\oplus E_{2}
with
[E_{1}:K],[E_{2}:K]<[E:K]
, and let
Q=Q_{1}\times_{K}Q_{2}
be the corresponding decomposition in quasi-trivial factors. Let
\pi:T\rightarrow Q
be the epimorphism given by our setting, and
T_{i}:=\pi^{-1}(Q_{i}),\,i=1,2
(these are subtori of
T
). Consider the composition:

V\rightarrow Q=Q_{1}\times_{K}Q_{2}\xrightarrow{pr_{1}}Q_{1}.

We have:

•

For each
q_{1}\in Q_{1}(K)
, the fibration
V_{q_{1}}:=V\times_{Q}(\{q_{1}\}\times_{K}Q_{2})\rightarrow\{q_{1}\}\times_{K}Q_{2}
is
T_{2}
-equivariant and so satisfies (

⋆

‣
13.1
) by the induction hypothesis;

•

The fibration
V\rightarrow Q_{1}
is
T_{1}
-equivariant and so again satisfies (

⋆

‣
13.1
) by the induction hypothesis.

Combining the above we deduce (

⋆

‣
13.1
) for
V\rightarrow Q
, as wished.
∎

4.
Harpaz–Wittenberg descent theory

In this section we show how to deduce
solvable descent
(Theorem
4.3
below) for rationally connected varieties from the fibration Theorem
1.4
. We follow
[
24
]
, where Harpaz and Wittenberg already (implicitly) proved this implication and used it in their proof of
supersolvable descent
(see
[
24
, Theorem 1.4]
).

Definition 4.1
.

Let
X
be a variety over a field
k
. A
finite descent type
is a finite integral cover
Y\rightarrow X_{\overline{k}}
such that
Y\rightarrow X
is Galois (i.e. such that the field extension
\overline{k}(Y)/k(X)
is Galois).

Definition 4.2
.

Let
\bar{Y}\rightarrow X_{\overline{k}}
be a finite descent type. A
torsor of type
\bar{Y}
is a cover
Y\rightarrow X
such that
Y_{\overline{k}}\cong\bar{Y}
as
X_{\overline{k}}
-schemes.

Theorem 4.3

(Solvable Descent)
.

Let
X
be a smooth geometrically integral variety over a number field
K
. Let
\bar{Y}
be a rationally connected solvable descent type over
X
. Then

X\left(K_{\Omega}\right)^{\operatorname{Br}_{\mathrm{ur}}(X)}=\overline{\bigcup_{f:Y\rightarrow X}f\left(Y\left(K_{\Omega}\right)^{\operatorname{Br}_{\mathrm{ur}}(Y)}\right)},

where
f:Y\rightarrow X
varies among all torsors of type
\bar{Y}
.

Proof assuming Theorem
1.4
.

As mentioned at the beginning of the section, this is implicitly proven in
[
24
]
. For completeness, we fill the details of this argument.

It suffices to prove this when
\bar{G}=\operatorname{Aut}(\bar{Y}/X_{\overline{K}})
is abelian, and then proceed by induction on the derived series of
G
as in
[
24
, Section 3C]
, to which we refer the reader for further details.

Since
\bar{G}
is abelian, the external action of
\Gal(X_{\overline{K}}/X)=\Gamma_{K}
on
\bar{G}
induces a structure of
\Gamma_{K}
-module on
\bar{G}
, and a corresponding finite abelian group scheme
G/K
.

We may assume without loss of generality that
X\left(K_{\Omega}\right)^{\operatorname{Br}_{\mathrm{ur}}(X)}\neq\emptyset
. Then by
[
24
, Lemma 3.7]
, the descent type
\bar{Y}\rightarrow X
descends to a
G
-torsor
Y\rightarrow X
. (This actually only requires
X(K_{\Omega})^{\Bet(X)}\neq\emptyset
, see
loc.cit.
).

Let now
S
be a finite set of places of
K
containing the archimedean ones, and let
(x_{v})_{v\in S}
be in
X(K_{S})
. We wish to prove that there exists a
G
-twist
Y^{\prime}
of
Y
and an adelic point
(y_{v})\in Y^{\prime}(K_{\Omega})^{\Br_{\mathrm{ur}}Y^{\prime}}
whose projection to
X
approximates
x_{v}
arbitrarily well for
v\in S
.

By
[
24
, Lemma 3.8]
, the group
G
fits in a short exact sequence
1\rightarrow G\rightarrow T\rightarrow Q\rightarrow 1
, where
T
and
Q
are tori and
Q
is quasi-trivial. Let
Z
be the contracted product
Z=Y\times^{G}T
. The second projection
Z\rightarrow T/G=Q
is a fibration whose fibers are
G
-twists of
Y
. The first projection
Z\rightarrow Y/G=X
is a
T
-torsor.

Following the arguments of
[
24
, Section 3B]
, there exists a cocycle
\sigma\in H^{1}(K,G)
such that a
K_{\Omega}
-point of
X
arbitrarily near to
(x_{v})_{v\in S}
lifts to the
T
-torsor
Z_{\sigma}\coloneqq Y_{\sigma}\times^{G}T
. Thus, after replacing
Y
with
Y_{\sigma}
(and
Z
with
Z_{\sigma}
), we may assume that the point
(x_{v})_{v\in S}
lifts to some
(z_{v})_{v\in S}\in Z(K_{v})
and that
Z(K_{\Omega})\neq\emptyset
.

Theorem
1.4
gives:

(4.1)

Z\left(K_{\Omega}\right)_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{Br}_{\mathrm{ur}}(Z)}=\overline{\bigcup_{q\in Q(K)}Z_{q}\left(K_{\Omega}\right)_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{Br}_{\mathrm{ur}}\left(Z_{q}\right)}}.

In fact, one may then find a
q\in Q(K)
and a point
(z^{\prime}_{v})_{v\in M_{K}}\in Z_{q}(\mathbb{A}_{K})^{\Br_{\mathrm{ur}}Z_{q}}
that approximates
z_{v}
for non-archimedean
v\in S
and such that
z^{\prime}_{v}
and
z_{v}
lie on the same connected component of
Z(K_{v})
for archimedean
v
. Note that the fibers of
Z\rightarrow Q
are
G
-twists of
Y
, and let
Y^{\prime}\coloneqq Z_{q}
. The projection of
z^{\prime}_{v}
to
X
approximates
x_{v}
arbitrarily well for non-archimedean
v\in S
and lies on the same connected component of
x_{v}
in
X(K_{v})
for archimedean
v
. For all archimedean
v
, we move
z^{\prime}_{v}
on its connected component in
Z_{q}(K_{v})
so that its projection to
X
is equal to
x_{v}
. Since moving within the archimedean connected components does not interfere with the Brauer–Manin obstruction, after this modification we still have
(z^{\prime}_{v})_{v\in M_{K}}\in Z_{q}(K_{\Omega})^{\Br_{\mathrm{ur}}Z_{q}}=Y^{\prime}(K_{\Omega})^{\Br_{\mathrm{ur}}Y^{\prime}}
, and this point provides the sought lift.
∎

We also borrow the following useful tool from Harpaz and Wittenberg’s work. It appears implicitly in the proof of Théorème 4.2 of
[
23
]
, but we restate it here for completeness, and add a variant with
X(K_{\Omega})_{dir}
in place of
X(K_{\Omega})
:

Theorem 4.4

(Harpaz–Wittenberg base change)
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Q
be a smooth proper morphism over a quasi-trivial torus
R_{E/K}\mathbb{G}_{m,E}
, and let
E\subset E^{\prime}
be an inclusion of finite étale
K
-algebras. Assume that, for all
c\in E^{*}
, letting
\varphi_{c}:R_{E^{\prime}/K}\mathbb{G}_{m,E^{\prime}}\rightarrow R_{E/K}\mathbb{G}_{m,E}
be the morphism
c\cdot N_{E^{\prime}/E}
(norm followed by translation by
c
), and defining
X^{\prime}
through the following cartesian diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

the identity

X^{\prime}(K_{\Omega})^{\Br_{ur}X^{\prime}}=\overline{\bigcup_{q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}(K)}X^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{A}_{K})^{\Br X^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}}}}

holds. Then

X(K_{\Omega})^{\Br_{ur}X}=\overline{\bigcup_{q\in Q(K)}X_{q}(\mathbb{A}_{K})^{\Br X_{q}}}.

The same statement holds after replacing
X(K_{\Omega}),X^{\prime}(K_{\Omega}),X_{q}(\mathbb{A}_{K}),
and
X^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{A}_{K})
by
X(K_{\Omega})_{dir},X^{\prime}(K_{\Omega})_{dir},X_{q}(\mathbb{A}_{K})_{\bullet},
and
X^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{A}_{K})_{\bullet}
, respectively.

Proof.

For the first part, see the proof of
[
23
, Théorème 4.2]
. We include the argument for completeness.

It suffices to prove the inclusion “
\subset
” as the other one is immediate. By
[
23
, Théorème 2.1]
, we have

(4.2)

X(K_{\Omega})^{\Br_{ur}X}\subset\overline{\bigcup_{c\in E^{*}}\psi_{c}\left(X^{\prime}_{c}(K_{\Omega})^{\Br_{ur}X^{\prime}_{c}}\right)},

where we are temporarily emphasizing the dependence of
X^{\prime}
on
c
via a subscript. Thus, our assumption gives:

\displaystyle X(K_{\Omega})^{\Br_{ur}X}\subset

\displaystyle\overline{\bigcup_{c\in E^{*}}\psi_{c}(X^{\prime}_{c}(K_{\Omega}))^{\Br_{ur}X^{\prime}_{c}}}=\overline{\bigcup_{c\in E^{*}}\psi_{c}\left(\overline{\bigcup_{q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}(K)}(X^{\prime}_{c})_{q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{A}_{K})^{\Br(X^{\prime}_{c})_{q^{\prime}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle\overline{\bigcup_{c\in E^{*}}\psi_{c}\left({\bigcup_{q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}(K)}(X^{\prime}_{c})_{q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{A}_{K})^{\Br(X^{\prime}_{c})_{q^{\prime}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{\bigcup_{c\in E^{*}}\bigcup_{q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}(K)}\psi_{c}\left({(X^{\prime}_{c})_{q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{A}_{K})^{\Br(X^{\prime}_{c})_{q^{\prime}}}}\right)}\subset\overline{\bigcup_{q\in Q(K)}\left({X_{q}(\mathbb{A}_{K})^{\Br X_{q}}}\right)},

which proves the first part.

For the second part, note that the map
\varphi_{c}:Q^{\prime}(K_{\infty})\rightarrow Q(K_{\infty})
satisfies
\varphi_{c}(t\cdot q^{\prime})=t^{[E^{\prime}:E]}\cdot\varphi_{c}(q^{\prime})
for
t\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}(K_{\infty})
. This equivariance induces quotient maps
\varphi_{c}:Q^{\prime}(K_{\infty})_{\text{dir}}\rightarrow Q(K_{\infty})_{\text{dir}}
and
\psi_{c}:X^{\prime}(K_{\infty})_{\text{dir}}\rightarrow X(K_{\infty})_{\text{dir}}
of
\varphi_{c}:Q^{\prime}(K_{\infty})\rightarrow Q(K_{\infty})
and
\psi_{c}:X^{\prime}(K_{\infty})\rightarrow X(K_{\infty})
, respectively. Accordingly, (
4.2
) induces an inclusion

X(K_{\Omega})_{\text{dir}}^{\Br_{ur}X}\subset\overline{\bigcup_{c\in E^{*}}\psi_{c}\left(X^{\prime}_{c}(K_{\Omega})_{\text{dir}}^{\Br_{ur}X^{\prime}_{c}}\right)},

and the whole sequence of inclusions above still holds after the replacements in the statement.
∎

We shall use Theorem
4.4
repeatedly to make reductions in proving Theorem
3.1
(see Proposition
9.1
). Applying it with
E^{\prime}
equal to the Galois closure of
E
we immediately get the first:

Corollary 4.5
.

Assume Theorem
3.1
holds when:

(R_{1})

The extension
E/K
is Galois.

Then Theorem
3.1
holds.

5.
Arithmetic symbols

This section is dedicated to arithmetic symbols. Lemma
5.1
below, which is likely known to experts, tells that certain spin symbols
[
13
]
of primes are trivial up to local terms at small places. We name the lemma “spin reciprocity”. Together with the classical reciprocity for power-symbols
[
38
, Theorem VI.8.3]
, this plays an important role in the proof of Theorem
10.9
. In Subsection
5.1
we introduce what we call
half-spin symbols
, while in Subsection
5.2
we lay out our conventions on
generalized Redéi symbols
, mostly following
[
26
]
.

Fix in this section a number field
E
and a natural number
n
such that
\mu_{n}\subset E^{*}
.

Lemma 5.1

(Spin reciprocity)
.

Let
\tau:E\rightarrow E
be an involution such that that
\zeta_{n}^{\tau}=\zeta_{n}^{-1}
. Let
S
be a finite set of places of
E
containing those dividing
\Delta_{E/E^{\tau}},n
or
\infty
, and such that
\tau(S)=S
. For a prime element
p
of
\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
such that
p\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}\neq p^{\tau}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
, we have

(5.1)

\left(\frac{p}{p^{\tau}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}}\right)_{n}=\prod_{v\in S}(p,p^{\tau}-p)_{w,n}.

Proof.

We claim that for all
x\in\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
and
v\notin S
such that
v(x)=0
, we have
\prod_{w\in\{v,v^{\tau}\}}(x,x^{\tau}-x)_{w,n}=1
.

The claim is clear if
v(x^{\tau}-x)=0
. Otherwise, if
e\coloneqq v(x^{\tau}-x)>0
and
v^{\tau}\neq v
, we have
\left(\frac{x}{v}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{x}{v^{\tau}}\right)_{n}=\left(\frac{x}{v}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{x^{\tau}}{v}\right)_{n}^{\tau}=\left(\frac{x}{v}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{x^{\tau}}{v}\right)_{n}^{-1}=\left(\frac{x}{v}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{x}{v}\right)_{n}^{-1}=1
, where in the penultimate identity we use that
x\equiv x^{\tau}\bmod v
. Hence
(x,x^{\tau}-x)_{v,n}(x,x^{\tau}-x)_{v^{\tau},n}=\left(\frac{x}{v}\right)_{n}^{e}\left(\frac{x}{v^{\tau}}\right)_{n}^{e}=1
. Finally, assume
v(x^{\tau}-x)>0
and
v^{\tau}=v
. Then
v
is inert above the place
w
of
E^{\tau}
under it, and the extension
\mathbb{F}_{w}\subset\mathbb{F}_{v}
has degree
2
. Thus
x\in\mathbb{F}_{v}^{*}
is an
(Nw+1)
-power. However, the hypothesis on
\zeta_{n}
implies that
n
divides
Nw+1
: indeed,
\tau
reduces
\bmod\,v
to the Frobenius automorphism of the quadratic extension
\mathbb{F}_{v}/\mathbb{F}_{w}
, so
\zeta_{n}^{\tau}\equiv\zeta_{n}^{Nw}\bmod v
and hence
\zeta_{n}^{\tau}=\zeta_{n}^{Nw}
, and using
\zeta_{n}^{\tau}=\zeta_{n}^{-1}
we get
\zeta_{n}^{Nw+1}=1
. Thus
x\in\mathbb{F}_{v}^{*}
is also an
n
-th power, hence
\left(\frac{x}{v}\right)_{n}=1
, and
(x,x^{\tau}-x)_{v,n}=\left(\frac{x}{v}\right)^{v(x^{\tau}-x)}_{n}=1
.

Now, using the claim and Hilbert’s product formula we infer that
1=\linebreak\prod_{v\in S\cup\{v_{p},v_{p}^{\tau}\}}(p,p^{\tau}-p)_{v,n}
, where
v_{p}
denotes the place of
E
corresponding to
p
. This concludes the proof after observing that
(p,p^{\tau}-p)_{v_{p}^{\tau},n}=1
while
(p,p^{\tau}-p)_{v_{p},n}=\left(\frac{p^{\tau}}{p\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}}\right)=\left(\frac{p}{p^{\tau}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}}\right)^{\tau}=\left(\frac{p}{p^{\tau}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}}\right)^{-1}
, where the latter holds because of the identity
\zeta_{n}^{\tau}=\zeta_{n}^{-1}
.
∎

5.1.
Half-spin symbols

In this subsection, let
\tau:E\rightarrow E
be an involution such that
\zeta_{n}^{\tau}=\zeta_{n}
, and
S
a finite set of places of
E
such that
\tau(S)=S
and that contains the ones dividing
n
,
\infty
or
\Delta_{E/E^{\tau}}
.

Definition 5.2
.

For
x\in\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
coprime with its conjugate
x^{\tau}
, the
half-spin symbol
is the power-residue symbol

\operatorname{HS}(x)\coloneqq\left(\frac{x}{\mathcal{I}_{x}}\right)_{n},

where
\mathcal{I}_{x}
denotes the unique prime ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{E^{\tau},S}
such that
\mathcal{I}_{x}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}=(x-x^{\tau})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
.

Such an ideal
\mathcal{I}_{x}
always exists as
(x-x^{\tau})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
is a
\tau
-invariant ideal and
S
contains all ramified primes for
E/E^{\tau}
. To make sense of the symbol
\left(\frac{x}{\mathcal{I}_{x}}\right)_{n}
note that, even though
x
does not necessarily belong to
\mathcal{O}_{E^{\tau},S}
, its reduction modulo
\mathcal{I}_{x}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
lies in
\mathcal{O}_{E^{\tau},S}/\mathcal{I}_{x}\subset\mathcal{O}_{E,S}/\mathcal{I}_{x}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
.

The definition of
\operatorname{HS}(x)
depends of course on
E,\tau,S
and
n
. We shall often emphasize the dependence on this data (or a subset of this data) by including them as a subscript, e.g. as in
\operatorname{HS}_{E,\tau,S,n}(x),\operatorname{HS}_{E,\tau,S}(x)
, etc…

The first point of the following proposition shows that the square of the half-spin symbols recovers, up to local factors at
S
, the spin symbol
\operatorname{Spin}_{S}(x)=\left(\frac{x^{\tau}}{x\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}}\right)_{n}
, hence the name.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
be coprime with
x^{\tau}
. The following hold:

(1)

\begin{aligned} \operatorname{HS}_{S}(x)^{2}=\left(\frac{x^{\tau}}{x\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}}\right)_{n}\cdot\prod_{v\in S}(x^{\tau}-x,x)_{n,v}.\end{aligned}

(2)

If
y
is an element of
\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
that is coprime in
\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
with both
x
and
y^{\tau}
, we have

\operatorname{HS}_{S}(xy)\operatorname{HS}_{S}(x)^{-1}\operatorname{HS}_{S}(y)^{-1}\left(\frac{y^{\tau}}{x\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}}\right)_{n}\\
=\prod_{v\in S}(N(x)/y_{2},z_{2}/x_{2})_{n,v}\cdot(-x_{2},z_{2})_{n,v},

where
x_{2}\coloneqq\sqrt{D}\cdot(x-x^{\tau}),y_{2}\coloneqq\sqrt{D}\cdot(y-y^{\tau}),z_{2}\coloneqq\sqrt{D}\cdot(xy-(xy)^{\tau})
for any
D\in E^{\tau}
such that
E=E^{\tau}(\sqrt{D})
.

(3)

If
y
is an element of
\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
that is coprime in
\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
with both
x
and
y^{\tau}
, is sufficiently close to
1
for all non-archimedean
v\in S
and its Minkowski direction is sufficently close to that of
1
, then

\operatorname{HS}_{S}(xy)\operatorname{HS}_{S}(x)^{-1}\operatorname{HS}_{S}(y)^{-1}\left(\frac{y^{\tau}}{x\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}}\right)_{n}=1.

The “sufficiently close” in point (3) depends on
x
.

5.1.1.
Proof of Proposition
5.3

Proof of (1).

Power-reciprocity states
\prod_{w\in S}(b,a)_{n}=\left(\frac{a}{b\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}}\right)_{n}\cdot\left(\frac{b}{a\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}}\right)_{n}^{-1}
whenever
a,b\in\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
are coprime. Applying it to
x,x^{\tau}-x
and using
\left(\frac{x^{\tau}}{x\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}}\right)_{n}=\left(\frac{x^{\tau}-x}{x\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}}\right)_{n}
, we get

\displaystyle\left(\frac{x}{(x^{\tau}-x)\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}}\right)_{n}\cdot\left(\frac{x^{\tau}}{x\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}}\right)_{n}^{-1}=\prod_{w\in S}(x^{\tau}-x,x)_{n,w}.

Since
\left(\frac{x}{(x^{\tau}-x)\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}}\right)_{n}=\left(\frac{x}{\mathcal{I}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}}\right)_{n}=\left(\frac{x}{\mathcal{I}}\right)_{n}^{2}=\operatorname{HS}(x)^{2}
, this concludes the proof. ∎

To prove (2), we use the following.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
K
be a number field,
n
be a natural number such that
\mu_{n}\subset K
, and
S
be a finite set of places of
K
containing those dividing
n
and the archimedean ones. Let
a,b,c\in\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
be three non-zero elements such that, letting
\mathfrak{d}\coloneqq(a,b,c)\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
be their gcd ideal, the ideals
\mathfrak{a}\coloneqq(a\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S})\cdot\mathfrak{d}^{-1},\mathfrak{b}\coloneqq(b\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S})\cdot\mathfrak{d}^{-1},\mathfrak{c}\coloneqq(c\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S})\cdot\mathfrak{d}^{-1}
of
\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
are pairwise coprime. Then

\left(\frac{a/b}{\mathfrak{c}}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{b/c}{\mathfrak{a}}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{c/a}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{-1}{\mathfrak{d}}\right)_{n}=\prod_{v\in S}(b,a)_{n,v}(c,b)_{n,v}(a,c)_{n,v}.

Proof.

By the Hilbert product formula, the right hand side is equal to
\prod_{v\in M_{K}\setminus S}(a,b)_{n,v}(b,c)_{n,v}(c,a)_{n,v}
. Fix now a place
v\notin S
, choose a uniformizer
\pi_{v}
of
K_{v}
, and write
a=\bar{a}_{v}\cdot\pi_{v}^{\alpha_{v}},b=\bar{b}_{v}\cdot\pi_{v}^{\beta_{v}},c=\bar{c}_{v}\cdot\pi_{v}^{\gamma_{v}}
, where
\bar{a}_{v},\bar{b}_{v},\bar{c}_{v}
are
v
-adic units. Write
\alpha^{\prime}_{v},\beta^{\prime}_{v},\gamma^{\prime}_{v},\delta_{v}
for the
v
-adic valuations of
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{d}
, respectively. By the hypothesis of coprimality of
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}
and
\mathfrak{c}
, only one among
\alpha^{\prime}_{v},\beta^{\prime}_{v}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{v}
can be positive. Note that
\alpha_{v}-\alpha^{\prime}_{v}=\beta_{v}-\beta^{\prime}_{v}=\gamma_{v}-\gamma^{\prime}_{v}=\delta_{v}
. We get:

\displaystyle(a,b)_{n,v}(b,c)_{n,v}(c,a)_{n,v}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\bar{a}_{v}/\bar{b}_{v}}{\pi_{v}}\right)^{\gamma_{v}}\left(\frac{\bar{b}_{v}/\bar{c}_{v}}{\pi_{v}}\right)^{\alpha_{v}}\left(\frac{\bar{c}_{v}/\bar{a}_{v}}{\pi_{v}}\right)^{\beta_{v}}\left(\frac{-1}{\pi_{v}}\right)^{\alpha_{v}\beta_{v}+\beta_{v}\gamma_{v}+\gamma_{v}\alpha_{v}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\bar{a}_{v}/\bar{b}_{v}}{\pi_{v}}\right)^{\gamma^{\prime}_{v}+\delta_{v}}\left(\frac{\bar{b}_{v}/\bar{c}_{v}}{\pi_{v}}\right)^{\alpha^{\prime}_{v}+\delta_{v}}\left(\frac{\bar{c}_{v}/\bar{a}_{v}}{\pi_{v}}\right)^{\beta^{\prime}_{v}+\delta_{v}}\left(\frac{-1}{\pi_{v}}\right)^{3\delta_{v}^{2}}

(5.2)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\bar{a}_{v}/\bar{b}_{v}}{\pi_{v}}\right)^{\gamma^{\prime}_{v}}\left(\frac{\bar{b}_{v}/\bar{c}_{v}}{\pi_{v}}\right)^{\alpha^{\prime}_{v}}\left(\frac{\bar{c}_{v}/\bar{a}_{v}}{\pi_{v}}\right)^{\beta^{\prime}_{v}}\left(\frac{-1}{\pi_{v}}\right)^{\delta_{v}}

If
\gamma^{\prime}_{v}>0
, then
\alpha^{\prime}_{v}=\beta^{\prime}_{v}=0
. Thus
\bar{a}_{v}/\bar{b}_{v}\equiv a/b\bmod\pi_{v}
, and
\left(\frac{\bar{a}_{v}/\bar{b}_{v}}{\pi_{v}}\right)^{\gamma^{\prime}_{v}}
is equal to
\left(\frac{a/b}{\pi_{v}}\right)^{\gamma^{\prime}_{v}}
, i.e. the local
v
-adic contribution to the Legendre symbol
\left(\frac{a/b}{\mathfrak{c}}\right)_{n}
. Analogously, when
\beta^{\prime}_{v}>0
or
\alpha^{\prime}_{v}>0
, the factors
\left(\frac{\bar{b}_{v}/\bar{c}_{v}}{\pi_{v}}\right)^{\alpha^{\prime}_{v}}
and
\left(\frac{\bar{c}_{v}/\bar{a}_{v}}{\pi_{v}}\right)^{\beta^{\prime}_{v}}
are equal to the local
v
-adic contributions of the symbols
\left(\frac{b/c}{\mathfrak{a}}\right)_{n}
and
\left(\frac{c/a}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)_{n}
. Lastly,
\left(\frac{-1}{\pi_{v}}\right)^{\delta_{v}}
is the local
v
-adic contribution to
\left(\frac{-1}{\mathfrak{d}}\right)_{n}
. Thus the product of (
5.2
) over all
v\notin S
is
\left(\frac{a/b}{\mathfrak{c}}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{b/c}{\mathfrak{a}}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{c/a}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{-1}{\mathfrak{d}}\right)_{n}
, proving the statement.
∎

Proof of (2).

Let
K=E^{\tau}
. Since the extension
E=K(\sqrt{D})
is unramified over
S
, we have
D\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S}=\mathfrak{d}_{s}^{2}
for some fractional ideal
\mathfrak{d}_{s}
. After possibly replacing
D
with
D\cdot u^{2}
for an appropriate
u\in K^{*}
, we assume without loss of generality that
D
is coprime with
N(x)
and
N(y)
in
\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
.

The proof is based on applying Lemma
5.4
to
x_{2},y_{2},z_{2}\in\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
, but we first need to verify the coprimality hypothesis.
For an element
a\in\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
, write
\mathcal{I}_{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
for the ideal such that
\mathcal{I}_{a}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}=(a-a^{\tau})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
. We have
x_{2}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S}=\mathfrak{d}_{s}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{x},\ y_{2}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S}=\mathfrak{d}_{s}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{y},\ z_{2}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S}=\mathfrak{d}_{s}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{z}
.
From the identity

(5.3)

z-z^{\tau}=(xy)-(xy)^{\tau}=y(x-x^{\tau})+x^{\tau}(y-y^{\tau})

and the coprimality of the pairs
x,x^{\tau}
and
y,y^{\tau}
in
\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
, it follows that, if
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
divides two among the triple
x-x^{\tau},y-y^{\tau},z-z^{\tau}
, then it must divide the third as well. Hence
(\mathcal{I}_{x},\mathcal{I}_{y},\mathcal{I}_{z})=(\mathcal{I}_{x},\mathcal{I}_{y})=(\mathcal{I}_{y},\mathcal{I}_{z})=(\mathcal{I}_{z},\mathcal{I}_{x})
. In other words, writing
\mathcal{I}_{x}=\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{x}\cdot\mathfrak{d},\mathcal{I}_{y}=\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{y}\cdot\mathfrak{d},\mathcal{I}_{z}=\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{z}\cdot\mathfrak{d}
with
\mathfrak{d}=(\mathcal{I}_{x},\mathcal{I}_{y},\mathcal{I}_{z})\subset\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
, the three ideals
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{x},\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{y},\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{z}
are pairwise coprime. Writing
x_{2}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S}=\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{x}\cdot\mathfrak{d}\cdot\mathfrak{d}_{s},y_{2}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S}=\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{y}\cdot\mathfrak{d}\cdot\mathfrak{d}_{s},z_{2}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S}=\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{z}\cdot\mathfrak{d}\cdot\mathfrak{d}_{s}
, we see that we have now verified the coprimality hypothesis.

We may thus apply Lemma
5.4
and get that

(5.4)

\left(\frac{z_{2}y_{2}^{-1}}{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{x}}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{x_{2}z_{2}^{-1}}{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{y}}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{y_{2}x_{2}^{-1}}{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{z}}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{-1}{\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{d}_{s}}\right)_{n}

is equal to the product of local symbols
\prod_{v\in S}(y_{2},x_{2})_{n,v}\cdot(z_{2},y_{2})_{n,v}\cdot(x_{2},z_{2})_{n,v}
.

We have
z_{2}y_{2}^{-1}=y\frac{x_{2}}{y_{2}}+x^{\tau}
. Since the ideal
\mathcal{I}_{x}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{I}_{y}^{\prime}
are coprime in
\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
, the fractional ideal
(x_{2}/y_{2})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S}=\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{x}/\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{y}
is reduced to minimal terms, and we then have that
z_{2}y_{2}^{-1}\equiv x^{\tau}\equiv x\bmod\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{x}
. Analogously, using
z_{2}x_{2}^{-1}=y+x^{\tau}\frac{y_{2}}{x_{2}}
and
y_{2}x_{2}^{-1}=(x^{\tau})^{-1}\frac{z_{2}}{x_{2}}-y(x^{\tau})^{-1}
, one finds that
z_{2}x_{2}^{-1}\equiv y\bmod\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{y}
and
y_{2}x_{2}^{-1}\equiv-y\cdot(x^{\tau})^{-1}\equiv-z\cdot N(x)^{-1}\bmod\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{z}
(for the latter we also notice that
x^{\tau}
is coprime with
y(x-x^{\tau})
by hypothesis, and hence with
z-z^{\tau}
by (
5.3
), and a fortiori with
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{z}
), respectively. Thus the product (
5.4
) is equal to

\left(\frac{x}{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{x}}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{y}{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{y}}\right)_{n}^{-1}\left(\frac{-z\cdot N(x)^{-1}}{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{z}}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{-1}{\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{d}_{s}}\right)_{n}.

Multiplying by the trivial term
\left(\frac{x}{\mathfrak{d}}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{y}{\mathfrak{d}}\right)^{-1}_{n}\left(\frac{z\cdot N(x)^{-1}}{\mathfrak{d}}\right)_{n}
(note that
z=xy
and
N(x)=xx^{\tau}\equiv x^{2}\bmod\,\mathfrak{d}
), we get that this is equal to the product

\displaystyle\left(\frac{x}{\mathcal{I}_{x}}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{y}{\mathcal{I}_{y}}\right)_{n}^{-1}\left(\frac{-z\cdot N(x)^{-1}}{\mathcal{I}_{z}}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{-1}{\mathfrak{d}_{s}}\right)_{n}=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{x}{\mathcal{I}_{x}}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{y}{\mathcal{I}_{y}}\right)_{n}^{-1}\left(\frac{z}{\mathcal{I}_{z}}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{-N(x)}{\mathcal{I}_{z}}\right)_{n}^{-1}\left(\frac{-1}{\mathfrak{d}_{s}}\right)_{n}=

\displaystyle\operatorname{HS}(x)\operatorname{HS}(y)^{-1}\operatorname{HS}(z)\left(\frac{-N(x)}{\mathcal{I}_{z}}\right)_{n}^{-1}\left(\frac{-1}{\mathfrak{d}_{s}}\right)_{n}.

Since
x\equiv x^{\tau}\bmod\mathcal{I}_{x}
, we have
\operatorname{HS}(x)^{2}=\left(\frac{x^{2}}{\mathcal{I}_{x}}\right)=\left(\frac{N(x)}{\mathcal{I}_{x}}\right)
, and so the product above is equal to

\operatorname{HS}(x)^{-1}\operatorname{HS}(y)^{-1}\operatorname{HS}(z)\left(\frac{N(x)}{\mathcal{I}_{x}}\right)\left(\frac{-N(x)}{\mathcal{I}_{z}}\right)_{n}^{-1}\left(\frac{-1}{\mathfrak{d}_{s}}\right)_{n}.

Further multiplying by the trivial term
\left(\frac{N(x)}{\mathfrak{d}_{s}}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{N(x)}{\mathfrak{d}_{s}}\right)_{n}^{-1}
, this becomes equal to

\operatorname{HS}(x)^{-1}\operatorname{HS}(y)^{-1}\operatorname{HS}(z)\left(\frac{-N(x)}{x_{2}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S}}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{-N(x)}{z_{2}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S}}\right)_{n}^{-1}.

Albert-Brauer-Hasse-Noether’s theorem gives that
\left(\frac{-N(x)}{z_{2}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S}}\right)_{n}=\left(\frac{z_{2}}{N(x)\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S}}\right)_{n}\cdot\prod_{v\in S}(-N(x),z_{2})_{n,v}
and
\left(\frac{N(x)}{x_{2}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S}}\right)_{n}=\left(\frac{x_{2}}{N(x)\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S}}\right)_{n}\cdot\prod_{v\in S}(N(x),x_{2})_{n,v}
. Summarizing the above we get

(5.5)

\displaystyle\operatorname{HS}(x)^{-1}\operatorname{HS}(y)^{-1}\operatorname{HS}(z)\left(\frac{z_{2}/x_{2}}{N(x)\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S}}\right)_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{v\in S}(-1,z_{2})_{n,v}(N(x),z_{2}/x_{2})_{n,v}\cdot(y_{2},x_{2})_{n,v}\cdot(z_{2},y_{2})_{n,v}\cdot(x_{2},z_{2})_{n,v}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{v\in S}(N(x)/y_{2},z_{2}/x_{2})_{n,v}\cdot(-x_{2},z_{2})_{n,v}.

This concludes the proof once we show that
\left(\frac{z_{2}/x_{2}}{N(x)\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S}}\right)_{n}=\left(\frac{y}{x^{\tau}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}}\right)_{n}
, which we do in the next paragraph.

Let
x\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}=\prod_{i}\mathcal{P}_{i}^{e_{i}}
be the factorization in prime ideals. We have
N(x)\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S}=\prod_{i}N(\mathcal{P}_{i})^{e_{i}}
, and each ideal
N(\mathcal{P}_{i})
is prime as otherwise
\mathcal{P}_{i}
would be inert over
K
, and thus divide
x^{\tau}
as well, contradicting the assumption that
x
and
x^{\tau}
are coprime. Since
\frac{z_{2}}{x_{2}}=y+x^{\tau}\frac{y_{2}}{x_{2}}
and thus
\frac{z_{2}}{x_{2}}=y^{\tau}+x\frac{y_{2}}{x_{2}}
, we have

\left(\frac{z_{2}/x_{2}}{N(x)\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K,S}}\right)_{n}=\prod_{i}\left(\frac{z_{2}/x_{2}}{N(\mathcal{P}_{i})}\right)_{n}^{e_{i}}=\prod_{i}\left(\frac{y^{\tau}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}}\right)_{n}^{e_{i}}=\left(\frac{y}{x^{\tau}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}}\right)_{n}.\qed

Proof of (3).

For
v\in S
, we have
\lim\limits_{y\rightarrow 1}\frac{z_{2}}{x_{2}}=\lim\limits_{y\rightarrow 1}\frac{y\cdot x_{2}+x^{\tau}\sqrt{D}\cdot(y-y^{\tau})}{x_{2}}=1
, so the non-archimedean contribution on the right hand side of (2) tends to
1
. The archimedean contribution also tends to
1
by the same argument, since the (archimedean) Hilbert symbols in question are invariant under the operation of multiplying
y
by a positive real factor.
∎

5.2.
Generalized Redéi symbols

Rédei symbols are triple symbols
[a,b,c]\in\pm 1
, defined for
a,b,c
in
\mathbb{Q}^{*}/\mathbb{Q}^{*2}
satisfying the coprimality condition
\gcd(\Delta(a),\Delta(b),\Delta(c))=1
(where
\Delta(x)
denotes the discriminant of the quadratic field associated to
x
) and the interlinking condition
(a,b)_{p}=(b,c)_{p}=(a,c)_{p}
for all primes
p\leq\infty

[
44
]
. Several generalizations of these symbols to aribitrary number fields and higher orders
n
have been proposed, see for instance Section 2 of Koymans and Smith’s paper
[
28
]
. We adopt here one such generalization, presented systematically by Kim and Morishita in
[
26
]
. In
loc.cit.
such generalization is presented under the name of “triple symbol”, but we adopt the term “generalized Redéi symbol” in this paper. We also prove in this section a well-posedness property (Lemma
5.7
) not included in
[
26
]
.

Definition
.

For a local field
k
of characteristic
0
and a
\Gamma_{k}
-module
M
, we say that an inhomogeneous cochain
\alpha\in C^{p}(\Gamma_{k},M),p\geq 0
is
unramified
if it lies in the image of the natural map

C^{p}(\Gal(k^{ur}/k),M^{\Gal(\bar{k}/k^{ur})})\rightarrow C^{p}(\Gamma_{k},M)
(with the convention that
k^{ur}=k
if
k
is real).

Definition
.

Given a number field
K
with algebraic closure
\overline{K}
, embeddings
\overline{K}\subset\overline{K_{v}},v\in M_{K}
in the algebraic closures of its completions, and a
\Gamma_{K}
-module
M
, we say that an inhomogeneous cochain
\alpha\in C^{p}(\Gamma_{K},M),p\geq 0
is
completely unramified
if, for every place
v
, the restriction
\alpha|_{\Gamma_{K_{v}}}\in C^{p}(\Gamma_{K_{v}},M)
is unramified.

To each element
x\in E^{*}
, we may associate a character
\chi_{x}:\Gamma_{E}\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)
via the Kummer isomorphism
E^{*}/E^{*n}\cong H^{1}(\Gamma_{E},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1))
. We denote by
S_{x}\subset M_{E}
the set of places the character
\chi_{x}
ramifies. Choose, for each place
v
of
E
, an embedding
\bar{E}\subset\overline{E_{v}}
.

For three elements
a,b,c\in E^{*}/E^{*n}
, consider the following condition.

(
C
) the characters
\chi_{a},\chi_{b},\chi_{c}
are tame (i.e. for all places
v
, the wild inertia group at
v
is contained in the kernels of all three characters), the sets
S_{a},S_{b},S_{c}
are disjoint, and for every place
v
, the Hilbert symbols
(a,b)_{v},(b,c)_{v},(a,c)_{v}
are trivial.

Condition (
C
) guarantees that the cup-product
\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{b}\cup\chi_{c}\in Z^{3}(\Gamma_{E},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(3))
is completely unramified
[
26
, Proposition 2]
. For
a,b,c\in E^{*}
satisying (
C
), we define (as in
[
26
, Section 1]
) the generalized
Redéi symbol
as

(5.6)

[a,b,c]_{n}\coloneqq\sum_{v\in M_{E}}\inv_{v}([\xi|_{\Gamma_{E_{v}}}-\eta_{v}])\in(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2),

where
\xi\in C^{2}(\Gamma_{E},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(3))
is a primitive of the cocycle
\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{b}\cup\chi_{c}
(in the sense that
\d\xi=\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{b}\cup\chi_{c}
), for every place
v
,
\eta_{v}\in C^{2}(\Gal(E_{v}^{\text{ur}}/E_{v}),(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(3))
is a primitive of the restriction
\xi|_{\Gamma_{E_{v}}}\in C^{2}(\Gamma_{E_{v}},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(3))
, and
\inv_{v}
denotes (the Tate twist of) the local invariant map
H^{2}(\Gamma_{E_{v}},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1))\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
.

Remark 5.5
.

In
[
26
]
, the symbol is defined with values in
\mu_{n}
rather than in
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)
. Although we are working under the assumption that
\mu_{n}\subset E
, and so the two groups are (non-canonically) isomorphic, this choice is not the natural one, as the coefficients of the cup product
\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{b}\cup\chi_{c}
lie naturally in
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(3)
. The invariant map takes one twist away, leaving
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)
as the natural value group for the generalized Redéi symbol. This will be important in later sections, as for instance our natural choice gives
[\sigma(a),\sigma(b),\sigma(c)]_{n}=\sigma([a,b,c]_{n})
for automorphisms
\sigma
of
E
.

The well-posedeness of (
5.6
) comes from the following lemma, see
[
26
, Section 2]
. We denote by
\Gamma_{S}\coloneqq\Gal(E_{S}/E)
the Galois group of the maximal extension
E_{S}/E
unramified outside
S
.

Lemma 5.6
.

For every triple
a,b,c\in E^{*}/E^{*n}
satisfying (
C
), there exist primitives
\xi
and
\eta_{v}
(for all
v
) as above, and the expression (
5.6
) is independent of their choice.

Moreover, for any set of places
S
containing
S_{a}\cup S_{b}\cup S_{c}
and all the places dividing
n
and
\infty
, the primitive
\xi
may be chosen to lie in
C^{2}(\Gamma_{S},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(3))
.

The following lemma does not appear in
[
26
]
.

Lemma 5.7
.

The symbol
[a,b,c]_{n}
is independent of the choice of embeddings
\bar{E}\subset\overline{E_{v}}
.

Before proving the lemma, we need to recall how conjugation acts on group cochains. To an element
g\in G
, one associates a
conjugation map

c_{g}:C^{\bullet}(G,M)\rightarrow C^{\bullet}(G,M)
of complexes, defined by
(c_{g}(\alpha))(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{p})\coloneqq g\cdot\alpha(g^{-1}\sigma_{1}g,\ldots,g^{-1}\sigma_{p}g)
. This map is homotopically equivalent to the identity. More precisely, we have
c_{g}-\text{id}=\d K_{g}+K_{g}\d
for the chain homotopy

\displaystyle K_{g}:C^{\bullet}(G,M)\rightarrow C^{\bullet-1}(G,M),

(5.7)

\displaystyle(K_{g}(\alpha))(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{p-1})\coloneqq g\cdot\alpha(g^{-1},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{p-1})\,+

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}(-1)^{i}g\cdot\alpha\left(g^{-1}\sigma_{1}g,\ldots,g^{-1}\sigma_{i}g,g^{-1}\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i+1},\ldots,\sigma_{p-1}\right)\,+

\displaystyle(-1)^{p}g\cdot\alpha\left(g^{-1}\sigma_{1}g,\ldots,g^{-1}\sigma_{p-1}g\right).

Proof of Lemma
5.7
.

Suppose given two different families of embeddings
\iota_{v},\iota^{\prime}_{v}:\bar{E}\hookrightarrow\bar{E}_{v},v\in M_{K}
. Since the images
\iota_{v}(\bar{E})
and
\iota^{\prime}_{v}(\bar{E})
coincide (both images are just the algebraic closure of
E
inside
\bar{E}_{v}
), there exists, for all
v
, a
g_{v}\in\Gamma_{E}
such that
\iota^{\prime}_{v}=\iota_{v}\circ g_{v}
. The two decomposition representations
\rho_{v},\rho^{\prime}_{v}:\Gamma_{E_{v}}\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{E}
induced by the two embeddings are then related by
\rho^{\prime}_{v}=\text{conj}_{g_{v}}\circ\rho_{v}
, where
\text{conj}_{g_{v}}:\gamma\mapsto g_{v}\gamma g_{v}^{-1}
denotes conjugation by
g_{v}
in
\Gamma_{E}
.

For the sake of the proof, we denote, for
\xi\in C^{n}(\Gamma_{E},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(3)),n\geq 0
, by
\xi|_{\Gamma_{v}}
and
\xi|^{\prime}_{\Gamma_{v}}
its restrictions to
\Gamma_{v}
via
\rho_{v}
and
\rho^{\prime}_{v}
, respectively. The relation
\rho^{\prime}_{v}=\text{conj}_{g_{v}}\circ\rho_{v}
gives that
\xi|^{\prime}_{\Gamma_{v}}=c_{g_{v}}(\xi)|_{\Gamma_{v}}
. We denote by
[a,b,c]_{n}
and
[a,b,c]^{\prime}_{n}
the two Redéi symbols given, respectively, by the first and second family of embeddings. Using the homotopy
K_{g_{v}}
, we get

c_{g_{v}}(\xi)=\xi+\d K_{g_{v}}(\xi)+K_{g_{v}}(\d\xi)=\xi+\d K_{g_{v}}(\xi)+K_{g_{v}}(\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{b}\cup\chi_{c}).

for each
v
. Restricting to
\Gamma_{v}
and subtracting
\eta_{v}+K_{g_{v}}(\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{b}\cup\chi_{c})|_{\Gamma_{v}}
from both sides, we get

(5.8)

\left(\xi|^{\prime}_{\Gamma_{v}}-\eta_{v}-K_{g}(\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{b}\cup\chi_{c})|_{\Gamma_{v}}\right)\equiv\left(\xi|_{\Gamma_{v}}-\eta_{v}\right),

modulo coborders. We claim that
K_{g}(\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{b}\cup\chi_{c})|_{\Gamma_{v}}
is unramified for all
v
. The statement then follows from the claim by summing local invariants of both sides of (
5.8
) over all
v
.

To prove the claim, we apply (
5.2
) to
\alpha=\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{b}\cup\chi_{c}
. Using
(\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{b}\cup\chi_{c})(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})=\chi_{a}(\sigma_{1})\chi_{b}(\sigma_{2})\chi_{c}(\sigma_{3})
(definition of cup-product) and the formula
\chi_{x}(\sigma\sigma^{\prime})=\chi_{x}(\sigma)+\chi_{x}(\sigma^{\prime})
, we get:

(5.9)

K_{g}(\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{b}\cup\chi_{c})(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})=\chi_{a}(g^{-1})\chi_{b}(\sigma_{1})\chi_{c}(\sigma_{2})-\chi_{a}(\sigma_{1})\chi_{b}(g^{-1}\sigma_{1})\chi_{c}(\sigma_{2})+\\
\chi_{a}(\sigma_{1})\chi_{b}(\sigma_{2})\chi_{c}(g^{-1}\sigma_{2})-\chi_{a}(\sigma_{1})\chi_{b}(\sigma_{2})\chi_{c}(\sigma_{2}).

If all three cocycles
\chi_{a},\chi_{b},\chi_{c}
are unramified at
v
, then (
5.9
) is also clearly unramified. On the other hand, suppose one of them ramifies at
v
, say
\chi_{a}
. Then
\chi_{b}
and
\chi_{c}
are unramified at
v
. Moreover, as argued in the proof of
[
26
, Proposition 2]
, condition (
C
) gives that the restrictions of the cup-products
\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{b}
and
\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{c}
to
Z^{2}(\Gamma_{E_{v}},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1))
are trivial as cochains. In other words, we have
\chi_{a}(\sigma_{1})\chi_{b}(\sigma_{2})=0
and
\chi_{a}(\sigma_{1})\chi_{c}(\sigma_{2})=0
for all
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Gamma_{E_{v}}
, and (
5.9
) simplifies to

\left(K_{g}(\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{b}\cup\chi_{c})|_{\Gamma_{E_{v}}}\right)(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})=\chi_{a}(g^{-1})\chi_{b}(\sigma_{1})\chi_{c}(\sigma_{2}),

which is unramified, proving the claim.
∎

We now discuss about the relation of generalized Redéi symbols with Heisenberg extensions. The Heisenberg group of matrices is:

\operatorname{Heiss}_{n}:=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1&x&z\\
0&1&y\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}:x,y\in(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1),\ z\in(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)\right\}.

This sits in a short exact sequence:

(5.10)

1\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)\rightarrow\operatorname{Heiss}_{n}\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)^{2}\rightarrow 1,

where the first embedding is the inclusion in the
z
-coordinate, and the surjection is the projection on the
x
- and
y
-coordinates.

Given two
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})
-linearly independent classes
[a],[b]\in E^{*}/E^{*n}
, the extension
E(\sqrt[n]{a},\sqrt[n]{b})/E
is Galois with group
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)^{2}
by Kummer theory. We have:

Lemma 5.8
.

Given two
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})
-linearly independent classes
[a],[b]\in E^{*}/E^{*n}
, there exists a
\operatorname{Heiss}_{n}
-extension
F/E
with fixed field
F^{(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)}=E(\sqrt[n]{a},\sqrt[n]{b})
if and only if the Hilbert symbol
(a,b)_{v}
is trivial for all
v
. Moreover, if such an extension
F
exists, and
S
is a set of places of
E
containing
S_{a}\cup S_{b}\cup S_{n}\cup S_{\infty}
such that
\Cl\mathcal{O}_{E,S}=0
, then there exists one that is unramified outside of
S
.

We denote an extension
F
as in the lemma by
E(\sqrt[n]{a},\sqrt[n]{b})_{2}
.

Proof.

Sequence (
5.10
) gives an exact sequence of sets

(5.11)

H^{1}(\Gamma_{E},\operatorname{Heiss}_{n})\rightarrow H^{1}(\Gamma_{E},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)^{2})\xrightarrow{\delta}H^{2}(\Gamma_{E},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)).

For
\varphi\coloneqq(\chi_{a},\chi_{b}):\Gamma_{E}\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)^{2}
, one has
\delta(\varphi)=\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{b}
, see
[
26
, Lemma 2]
.

By Galois theory, the existence of
F
is equivalent to the existence of a surjection
\Gamma_{E}\rightarrow\operatorname{Heiss}_{n}
lifting the surjection
\Gamma_{E}\rightarrow\Gal(E(\sqrt[n]{a},\sqrt[n]{b})/E)=(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)^{2}
. The latter surjection is just
(\chi_{a},\chi_{b})
. By (
5.11
), the existence of such a lift is then equivalent to
\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{b}
being trivial in
H^{2}(\Gamma_{E},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2))
. Indentifying the latter with
(\Br E)_{n}(1)
, the cup-product
\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{b}
corresponds to the global Hilbert symbol
(a,b)
, and by the Albert–Brauer–Hasse–Noether theorem, this is trivial if and only if all the local Hilbert symbols
(a,b)_{v}
are trivial.

For the last statement, write
F=E(\sqrt[n]{a},\sqrt[n]{b})(\sqrt[n]{c})
with
c\in E(\sqrt[n]{a},\sqrt[n]{b})^{*}
. Since
F
is Galois over
E(\sqrt[n]{a},\sqrt[n]{b})
, we have
F=E(\sqrt[n]{a},\sqrt[n]{b})(\sqrt[n]{c^{\prime}})
for every
E(\sqrt[n]{a},\sqrt[n]{b})/E
-conjugate
c^{\prime}
of
c
. Thus
c^{\prime}/c
is an
n
-th power in
E(\sqrt[n]{a},\sqrt[n]{b})
for any such
c^{\prime}
. It follows that, for all places
v
of
E
not in
S
, and all places
w\mid v
of
E(\sqrt[n]{a},\sqrt[n]{b})
, the residue class of
w(c)\bmod n
is independent of
w
. We denote by
v(c)\in\mathbb{Z}
a representative of this class. Since
\Cl\mathcal{O}_{E,S}=0
, there is a
d\in E^{*}
such that
d\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}=\prod_{\mathcal{P}\notin S}\mathcal{P}^{-v(c)}
(note that this is a finite product). Now
F^{\prime}=E(\sqrt[n]{a},\sqrt[n]{b})(\sqrt[n]{cd})
