is unramified over
E(\sqrt[n]{a},\sqrt[n]{b})
(and thus over
E
) outside
S
. The extension
F^{\prime}/E
is again Galois with group
\operatorname{Heiss}_{n}
and provides the desired lift.
∎

For the remainder of this section, fix a finite set of places
S_{0}
containing
\{v:v|n\cdot\infty\}
such that
\Cl\mathcal{O}_{E,S_{0}}=0
.

For
a,b,c\in E^{*}
we introduce a stronger variant of (
C
):

(
C^{\prime}
)
{}_{S_{0}}
The elements
a,b,c
are
v
-adic
n
-th powers for all
v\in S_{0}
; the valuation
v(x)
is coprime with
n
for every
v\in S_{x},x=a,b,c
; and (
C
) holds.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
a,b,c\in E^{*}
be algebraic numbers satisfying (
C^{\prime}
)
{}_{S_{0}}
. Then, for every finite place
v\in S_{c}
, we have that
\Frob_{v}(E(\sqrt[n]{a},\sqrt[n]{b})_{2}/E)
lies in the center
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)
of
\operatorname{Heiss}_{n}
, and

[a,b,c]_{n}=\sum_{v\in S_{c}}v(c)\cdot\Frob_{v}(E(\sqrt[n]{a},\sqrt[n]{b})_{2}/E)\in(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2).

Proof.

We denote by
pr_{x},pr_{y}:\operatorname{Heiss}_{n}\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1),\ pr_{z}:\operatorname{Heiss}_{n}\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)
the projections to the
x
,
y
and
z
coordinates. The multiplication rule for matrices gives that
pr_{z}(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2})-pr_{z}(\sigma_{1})-pr_{z}(\sigma_{2})=pr_{x}(\sigma_{1})pr_{y}(\sigma_{2})
for all
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\operatorname{Heiss}_{n}
. In other words,

\d pr_{z}=pr_{x}\cup pr_{y}\quad\text{in }C^{2}(\operatorname{Heiss}_{n},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)).

Pulling back this last identity along
\rho:\Gamma_{E}\rightarrow\Gal(E(\sqrt[n]{a},\sqrt[n]{b})_{2}/E)=\operatorname{Heiss}_{n}
, we get
\d\varphi=\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{b}
with
\varphi=pr_{z}\circ\rho
. Thus
\d(\varphi\cup\chi_{c})=\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{b}\cup\chi_{c}
, and

[a,b,c]_{n}=\sum_{v}\inv_{v}((\varphi\cup\chi_{c})|_{\Gamma_{v}}-\eta_{v}),

with
\eta_{v}
unramified such that
\d\eta_{v}=(\chi_{a}\cup\chi_{b}\cup\chi_{c})|_{\Gamma_{v}}
. For
v\in S_{0}
, (
C^{\prime}
)
{}_{S_{0}}
implies that
c
is a
v
-adic
n
-th power, so
\chi_{c}|_{\Gamma_{v}}
is trivial, and thus so is
(\varphi\cup\chi_{c})|_{\Gamma_{v}}
, meaning that
v
-adic contributions to the Redéi symbol are trivial. For
v\in S_{a}
, since
v(c)\neq 0
, we have
(a,c)_{v}=\left(\frac{c}{v}\right)_{n}^{v(a)}
. So the triviality of this symbol, combined with
(v(a),n)=1
(again from (
C^{\prime}
)
{}_{S_{0}}
), gives that
c
is a
v
-adic
n
-th power, and same for
v\in S_{b}
, and so these
v
also contribute trivially to the Redéi symbol. For
v\notin S_{0}\cup S_{a}\cup S_{b}\cup S_{c}
, the cocycle
\varphi\cup\chi_{c}
is unramified, and the contribution is also trivial.

The only places remaining are the
v\in S_{c}
. Arguing as above, both
a
and
b
are
v
-adic
n
-th powers, so
v
splits in
E(\sqrt[n]{a},\sqrt[n]{b})
and
\Frob_{v}:=\Frob_{v}(E(\sqrt[n]{a},\sqrt[n]{b})_{2}/E)
lies in
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)
. Moreover,
\d\left(\varphi|_{\Gamma_{v}}\right)=\chi_{a}|_{\Gamma_{v}}\cup\chi_{b}|_{\Gamma_{v}}
is trivial, meaning
\varphi|_{\Gamma_{v}}
is already a cocycle, and thus so is
(\varphi\cup\chi_{c})|_{\Gamma_{v}}
. Then the local contribution at
v
to the Redéi symbol is
\inv_{v}(\varphi|_{\Gamma_{v}}\cup\chi_{c}|_{\Gamma_{v}})
. Since the cocycle
\varphi|_{\Gamma_{v}}
is unramified, this local invariant is precisely
v(c)\cdot\varphi(\Frob_{v})=v(c)\cdot\Frob_{v}(E(\sqrt[n]{a},\sqrt[n]{b})_{2}/E)
.
∎

6.
Horizontal Brauer group

In this section, we use the language of derived categories to define the
horizontal Brauer group
of a smooth proper morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
with geometrically integral fibers. The main interest lies in the case where
f
has rationally connected fibers and
Y
is a smooth variety over a number field
K
, where such group parametrizes the quotients
\Br X_{y}/f^{*}\Br y
for
y
in a Hilbertian subset
H\subset Y(K)
. See Theorem
6.2
. We prove this theorem in Subsection
6.1
. Finally, Subsection
6.2
is devoted to a certain complex that arises naturally when the basis
Y
is geometrically a torus.

We also refer the interested reader to Section 5 of the paper
[
3
]
by Bright, Browning and Loughran for a similar approach to generate the Brauer groups of the fibers of rationally connected fibration, via the sheaf
R^{2}f_{*}\mu_{\infty}
. Although the approach in
loc.cit.
is simpler to ours (it does not require derived categories), it does not parametrize the Brauer groups of the fibers modulo constants exactly, but only generates them as the image of
H^{0}(Y,R^{2}f_{*}\mu_{\infty})
.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a smooth proper morphism of schemes with geometrically integral fibers. The
horizontal Brauer group of
f
is

\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)\coloneqq H^{2}(Y,\tau_{\geq 1}R^{\bullet}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}).

To every cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

where
f
(and thus also
f^{\prime}
) is smooth proper with geometrically integral fibers, we associate a natural pullback map:

(6.1)

g^{*}:\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(X^{\prime}/Y^{\prime}),

constructed as follows. Consider the composition of morphisms

\displaystyle(g^{*}\circ\tau_{\geq 1}\circ R^{\bullet}f_{*})\mathbb{G}_{m}

\displaystyle=(\tau_{\geq 1}\circ g^{*}\circ R^{\bullet}f_{*})\mathbb{G}_{m}

\displaystyle\rightarrow(\tau_{\geq 1}\circ R^{\bullet}f^{\prime}_{*}\circ(g^{\prime})^{*})\mathbb{G}_{m}=(\tau_{\geq 1}\circ R^{\bullet}f^{\prime}_{*})((g^{\prime})^{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})

\displaystyle\rightarrow(\tau_{\geq 1}R^{\bullet}f^{\prime}_{*})\mathbb{G}_{m}

in the derived category
\mathcal{D}^{+}(\Sh_{Y^{\prime}})
, where the two maps are give by the natural transformation
g^{*}\circ R^{\bullet}f_{*}\rightarrow R^{\bullet}f^{\prime}_{*}\circ(g^{\prime})^{*}
and the natural map
(g^{\prime})^{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}
, respectively. (We warn the reader that the natural map
(g^{\prime})^{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}
may not be an isomorphism, as we are working in the small étale site, whereas it would be an isomorphism in the big étale site.)
This composition induces maps on cohomology

H^{n}(Y^{\prime},g^{*}\tau_{\geq 1}R^{\bullet}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow H^{n}(Y^{\prime},\tau_{\geq 1}R^{\bullet}f^{\prime}_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}),

for all
n\geq 0
. Composing these with the natural pullback maps

g^{*}:H^{n}(Y,\tau_{\geq 1}R^{\bullet}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow H^{n}(Y^{\prime},g^{*}\tau_{\geq 1}R^{\bullet}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}),

we get natural maps

g^{*}:H^{n}(Y,\tau_{\geq 1}R^{\bullet}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow H^{n}(Y^{\prime},\tau_{\geq 1}R^{\bullet}f^{\prime}_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}),\ \ n\geq 0,

which we still denote by
g^{*}
with a slight abuse of notation. For
n=2
, this gives (
6.1
). When
Y^{\prime}
is a point
y
of
Y
, the pullback (
6.1
) defines a
specialization map

\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(X_{y}/y).

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a smooth proper morphism of schemes with geometrically integral fibers. The following hold true.

(i)

There is a natural exact sequence:

(6.2)

\Br Y\xrightarrow{f^{*}}\Br X\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)\xrightarrow{\partial}H^{3}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m})\xrightarrow{f^{*}}H^{3}(X,\mathbb{G}_{m})

Assume, moreover, that
Y
is regular, integral, of residual characteristic
0
, and that
R^{1}f_{*}\mu_{\infty}=R^{2}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=0
. Then:

(ii)

the specialization map

\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(X_{y}/y)

is an isomorphism for
y
in a Hilbert subset
H\subset Y
,

(iii)

\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)
is finite,

Our proof of Theorem
6.2
follows similar lines as that of Proposition 4.1 of
[
22
]
, which covers points (ii)-(iv) in the case where
H^{3}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m})=0
.

The generic point
\eta
of
Y
lies in all Hilbert subsets of
Y
. Hence point (ii) gives, under its hypotheses, an identification

\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)=\Br_{\text{hor}}(X_{\eta}/\eta),

which in turn identifies the morphism
\Br X_{\eta}\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(X_{\eta}/\eta)
appearing in (
6.2
) for
X_{\eta}\rightarrow\eta
with a morphism

(6.3)

r:\Br X_{\eta}\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y).

When
Y
is a smooth variety over a number field
K
, the vanishing
H^{3}(K,\mathbb{G}_{m})=0

[
5
, VII.11.4, p. 199]
induces identifications
\Br_{\text{hor}}(X_{y}/y)=\Br X_{y}/\Br y
for all
y\in Y(K)
. Thus the specialization
\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(X_{y}/y)
induces a Brauer–Manin pairing on the fibers:

(-,-)_{BM}:\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)\times\bigcup_{y\in Y(K)}X_{y}(\mathbb{A}_{K})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}.

6.1.
Proof of Theorem
6.2

Proof of (i).

We have
f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}=\mathbb{G}_{m}
(this holds in general for any flat proper morphism with geometrically integral fibers). Hence, applying the functor
\tau_{\geq 1}
to
R^{\bullet}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}
, we get a natural exact triangle:

(6.4)

\mathbb{G}_{m}[0]\rightarrow R^{\bullet}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\tau_{\geq 1}R^{\bullet}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}\xrightarrow{+1},

in
\mathcal{D}^{+}(\Sh_{Y})
. Taking hypercohomology and using the identity
H^{n}(Y,R^{\bullet}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})=H^{n}(X,\mathbb{G}_{m})
arising from
Grothendieck’s theorem on the composition of derived functors
[
43
, Tag 015L]
(which applies as
f_{*}:\Sh(X)\rightarrow\Sh(Y)
sends injectives to injectives), this triangle induces a long exact sequence:

\cdots\rightarrow H^{n}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m})\xrightarrow{f^{*}}H^{n}(X,\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow H^{n}(Y,\tau_{\geq 1}R^{\bullet}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})\xrightarrow{\partial}H^{n+1}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow\cdots.

of which (
6.2
) is a subsequence.
∎

From here on, we assume that
Y
is regular and integral and that
R^{1}f_{*}\mu_{\infty}=R^{2}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=0
. Applying
R^{\bullet}f_{*}
to the exact sequence

1\rightarrow\mu_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\rightarrow 1,

of étale sheaves on
X
, we obtain a natural “
\infty
-Kummer” exact triangle:

(6.5)

R^{\bullet}f_{*}\mu_{\infty}\rightarrow R^{\bullet}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow R^{\bullet}f_{*}(\mathbb{G}_{m}\otimes\mathbb{Q})\xrightarrow{+1}.

We have

(6.6)

R^{2}f_{*}(\mathbb{G}_{m}\otimes\mathbb{Q})=0.

In fact,
R^{2}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}
is the sheafification in the small étale site
Y_{\acute{e}t}
of the presheaf
Y^{\prime}\mapsto\Br(X\times_{Y}Y^{\prime})
. Since
f
is smooth and
Y
is regular, the scheme
X\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}
is regular for every
Y^{\prime}\in Y_{\acute{e}t}
, and hence its Brauer group is torsion
[
7
, Lemma 3.5.3]
. It follows that
R^{2}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}
is also torsion, and the identity
R^{2}f_{*}(\mathbb{G}_{m}\otimes\mathbb{Q})=(R^{2}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
gives (
6.6
).

By the just-proven vanishing
R^{2}f_{*}(\mathbb{G}_{m}\otimes\mathbb{Q})=0
, and the assumed vanishing
R^{1}f_{*}\mu_{\infty}=0
, we may truncate (
6.5
) to obtain a natural exact triangle

(6.7)

(R^{1}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})[2]\otimes\mathbb{Q}\rightarrow R^{2}f_{*}\mu_{\infty}[2]\rightarrow\tau_{[1,2]}R^{\bullet}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}\xrightarrow{+1}.

The following lemma is an adaptation of
[
51
, Proposition 2.9]
to our slightly different context.

Lemma 6.3
.

The sheaf
R^{1}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}
is represented by a torsion-free finitely generated isotrivial
Y
-scheme. Its stalk at a geometric point
\bar{y}
of
Y
is equal to
\Pic X_{\bar{y}}=\NS(X_{\bar{y}})
.

Proof.

The smooth base-change theorem
[
34
, Theorem VI.4.1]
and Grauert’s theorem
[
25
, pp. 288-291]
show that the two vanishing hypotheses are equivalent to the vanishing of
H^{1}(X_{\bar{y}},\mu_{\infty})
and
H^{2}(X_{\bar{y}},\mathcal{O}_{X_{\bar{y}}})
, respectively, for all geometric points
\bar{y}
of
Y
. The vanishing of the former implies that
X_{\bar{y}}
has no non-trivial connected abelian finite étale covers. Hence the following hold:
\Pic X_{\bar{y}}\cong\NS X_{\bar{y}}
,
\NS X_{\bar{y}}
is torsion-free, and
H^{1}(X_{\bar{y}},\mathcal{O}_{X_{\bar{y}}})=0
for all
\bar{y}

[
7
, Corollary 5.1.3]
. Again by Grauert’s theorem, the last of these three conditions is equivalent to the vanishing of
R^{1}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
.

The rest of the argument follows
[
51
]
, but we include it for completeness. We claim that the relative Picard scheme
\Pic_{X/Y}
is a disjoint union of finite étale covers of
Y
. In fact, the smoothness of
f
guarantees that each closed subscheme of
\operatorname{Pic}_{X/Y}
which is of finite type is proper over
Y

[
18
, p. 232, Theorem 3]
. The vanishings
R^{1}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=R^{2}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=0
(and the fact that both commute with base-change) imply that
\mathrm{Pic}_{X/Y}
is formally étale over
C
. Together, these facts imply that each connected component of
\mathrm{Pic}_{X/Y}
is finite étale over
C
, proving the claim.

Denote by
\bar{\eta}
the generic geometric point
\Spec\overline{\kappa(\eta)}
of
Y
. Choose a basis
v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}
of constant sections for the group-scheme
\Pic_{X_{\bar{\eta}}/\bar{\eta}}
. The section
v_{1}
dominates a connected component
Y_{1}
of
\Pic_{X/Y}
, thus the base-change
\Pic_{X/Y}\times_{Y}Y_{1}
is a
Y_{1}
-group scheme equipped with a canonical section. Taking further finite étale base-changes, we find an étale cover
Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
such that the base-change
\Pic_{X/Y}\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}
has
r
canonical sections, which restrict to
v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}
on the generic geometric fiber. Combined with the previous paragraph, this guarantees that
\Pic_{X/Y}\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}
is a (torsion-free, finitely generated) constant group scheme. Thus
\Pic_{X/Y}
is isotrivial by definition.

Finally, the geometric stalk
(\Pic_{X/Y})_{\bar{y}}
of
\Pic_{X/Y}
is equal to
\Pic(X_{\mathcal{O}_{\bar{y}}^{\text{sh}}})=\Pic_{X/Y}(\mathcal{O}_{\bar{y}}^{\text{sh}})
, where
\mathcal{O}_{\bar{y}}^{\text{sh}}
denotes the strict Henselianization of
Y
at
\bar{y}

[
34
, p.38]
. Since
\Pic_{X/Y}
is étale over
Y
, we have
\Pic_{X/Y}(\mathcal{O}_{\bar{y}}^{\text{sh}})=\Pic_{X/Y}(\bar{y})
by the properties of strictly Henselian rings. Finally, we have
\Pic_{X/Y}(\bar{y})=\Pic X_{\bar{y}}
by representability.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

There is a natural exact sequence

H^{0}(Y,\Pic_{X/Y})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\rightarrow H^{0}(Y,R^{2}f_{*}\mu_{\infty})\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)\rightarrow 0.

Proof.

Choose a geometric point
\bar{y}
of
Y
. By Lemma
6.3
, there is a finite étale morphism
p:Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
such that the restriction
\Pic_{X/Y}|_{Y^{\prime}}
is a constant sheaf with coefficient group
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
. We may assume that
Y^{\prime}
is integral and Galois over
Y
, with group
G
. Since
Y^{\prime}
is normal,
H^{1}(Y^{\prime},\mathbb{Z})=0
, and the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence gives an isomorphism
H^{1}(Y,\Pic_{X/Y})\cong H^{1}(G,\Pic_{X/Y}(Y^{\prime}))
, which is finite. Thus
H^{1}(Y,\Pic_{X/Y})\otimes\mathbb{Q}=0
. Noting that
H^{2}(Y,\tau_{\geq 1}M)=H^{2}(Y,\tau_{[1,2]}M)
for any complex of sheaves
M
, the sequence of the statement is contained in the cohomology sequence of (
6.7
).
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

There exists a finite étale connected cover
\pi:Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
such that, for any morphism
p:Z\rightarrow Y
with
Z
regular and integral for which the fiber product
Z\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}
is also integral, the pullback

p^{*}:\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(X_{Z}/Z),

where
X_{Z}\coloneqq X\times_{Y}Z
, is an isomorphism.

We observe that the integrality hypothesis is automatically satisfied if the fibers of
p
are geometrically connected. In fact, in this case, the fibers of the base change
p^{\prime}:Z^{\prime}:=Z\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
are also geometrically connected. So, since
Y^{\prime}
is connected,
Z^{\prime}
is too. Since
Z^{\prime}
is also regular, as it is a finite étale cover of
Z
, it is thus also integral.

Proof.

Let
\bar{y}
be a geometric point of
Y
, of which we make a more specific choice further down. By Lemma
6.3
, we may identify the sheaf
R^{1}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}
with the
\pi_{1}(Y,\bar{y})
-module
\NS(X_{\bar{y}})
. Moreover, by smooth-proper base change
[
34
, Corollary VI.4.2]
, we may identify the sheaf
R^{2}f_{*}\mu_{\infty}
, which is a direct limit of locally constant sheaves, with the
\pi_{1}(Y,\bar{y})
-module
H^{2}(X_{\overline{y}},\mu_{\infty})
, see
[
34
, p. 156]
.

Under these identifications, Lemma
6.4
gives a canonical exact sequence:

(6.8)

(\NS X_{\overline{y}}\otimes\mathbb{Q})^{\pi_{1}(Y,\overline{y})}\rightarrow H^{2}(X_{\overline{y}},\mu_{\infty})^{\pi_{1}(Y,\overline{y})}\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)\rightarrow 0.

Let now
\bar{z}
be the generic geometric point of
Z
, and let
\bar{y}\coloneqq p(\bar{z})
. Comparing (
6.8
) for
Y
and
Z
, and in virtue of the isomorphism
X_{\bar{y}}\cong(X_{Z})_{\bar{z}}
, we get a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\NS X_{\overline{y}}\otimes\mathbb{Q})^{\pi_{1}(Y,\overline{y})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(X_{\overline{y}},\mu_{\infty})^{\pi_{1}(Y,\overline{y})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\NS X_{\overline{y}}\otimes\mathbb{Q})^{\pi_{1}(Z,\bar{z})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(X_{\overline{y}},\mu_{\infty})^{\pi_{1}(Z,\bar{z})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br_{\text{hor}}(X_{Z}/Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}}

The action of
\pi_{1}(Y,\overline{y})
on
H^{2}(X_{\overline{y}},\mu_{\infty})
factors through a finite quotient by
[
22
, Lemma 4.2]
(in summary because
H^{2}(X_{\overline{y}},\mu_{\infty})
is a finite extension of the finite group
\Br X_{\overline{y}}
by
\NS(X_{\overline{y}})\otimes\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
, on which
\pi_{1}(Y,\overline{y})
acts through a finite quotient as
\NS(X_{\overline{y}})
is finitely generated and the action is continuous, see
loc.cit.
for more details), which corresponds to a finite irreducible Galois étale cover
\pi:Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
, say with group
G
. The assumption that
Y^{\prime}\times_{Y}Z
is integral implies that the composition
\pi_{1}(Z,\overline{y}^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Y,\overline{y})\rightarrow G
is surjective. We conclude with a diagram chase.
∎

We go back to the proof of points (ii) and (iii) of Theorem
6.2
.

Proof of (ii).

This follows from Lemma
6.5
, taking
Z
to be a point.
∎

Proof of (iii).

The well-known argument used to prove that the Brauer groups of rationally connected varieties are finite modulo constants (see e.g.
[
7
, Sec. 5.5]
) works with minimal modifications.
Namely, point (ii) provides an identification
\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)=\Br_{\text{hor}}(X_{\eta}/\eta)
. The hypercohomology spectral sequence

H^{i}(\kappa(\eta),H^{j}(\tau_{\geq 1}R^{\bullet}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}))\Rightarrow H^{i+j}(\kappa(\eta),\tau_{\geq 1}R^{\bullet}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})

yields an exact sequence

(6.9)

0\rightarrow H^{1}(\kappa(\eta),\Pic X_{\overline{\eta}})\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(X_{\eta}/\eta)\rightarrow\Br X_{\overline{\eta}}.

As
X_{\eta}
is rationally connected, the group
\Pic X_{\overline{\eta}}
is finitely generated and torsion-free, making the cohomology group
H^{1}(\kappa(\eta),\Pic X_{\overline{\eta}})
finite
[
7
, Theorem 5.5.1]
. Again by the rational connectedness of
X_{\eta}
, the group
\Br X_{\overline{\eta}}
is also finite
[
7
, Theorem 5.5.2]
.
∎

6.2.
The
r-\bar{\partial}
complex

In this subsection, let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a smooth proper morphism with geometrically integral fibers such that
R^{1}f_{*}\mu_{\infty}=R^{2}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=0
. Assume that
Y
is a smooth integral variety over a field
k
of characteristic
0
, and let
\eta
be its generic point. Recall that we have maps

r:\Br X_{\eta}\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y),\ \ \partial:\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)\rightarrow H^{3}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m}).

We also let
\bar{\partial}
be the composition

\bar{\partial}:\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)\rightarrow H^{3}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow H^{3}(Y\otimes_{k}\overline{k},\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\Gamma_{K}}.

Lemma 6.6
.

If the restriction

(6.10)

H^{3}(Y\otimes_{k}\overline{k},\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow H^{3}(\eta\otimes_{k}\overline{k},\mathbb{G}_{m})

is injective, then the two morphisms

(6.11)

\Br X_{\eta}\xrightarrow{r}\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)\xrightarrow{\bar{\partial}}H^{3}(Y\otimes_{k}\overline{k},\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\Gamma_{K}}

form a complex. The restriction (
6.10
) is injective if
Y\otimes_{k}\overline{k}
is a
\overline{k}
-torus.

Proof.

By the naturality of
\partial
, the image
\partial(r(\Br X_{\eta}))
lies in the kernel of
H^{3}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow H^{3}(\eta,\mathbb{G}_{m})
. Any element of this kernel must map to zero also in
H^{3}(\eta\otimes_{k}\overline{k},\mathbb{G}_{m})
. So, if
H^{3}(Y\otimes_{k}\overline{k},\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow H^{3}(\eta\otimes_{k}\overline{k},\mathbb{G}_{m})
is injective, it maps to zero in
H^{3}(Y\otimes_{k}\overline{k},\mathbb{G}_{m})
as well.

For the second part, let
d
be a natural number, and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}
be algebraically independent variables. For a field
F
, we denote by
F((t))
its field of Laurent series.
Gille and Pianzola
[
15
, Proposition 3.1(2)]
prove that the composition

H^{n}(\overline{k}[t_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,t_{d}^{\pm 1}],\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow H^{n}(\overline{k}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}),\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow H^{n}(\overline{k}((t_{1}))\cdots((t_{d})),\mathbb{G}_{m}),

is an isomorphism for all
n\geq 2
. In particular, the first map is injective. Setting
n=3
and choosing a
\overline{k}
-isomorphism
Y\otimes_{k}\overline{k}\cong\Spec\overline{k}[t_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,t_{d}^{\pm 1}]
concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

In Section
9
, we shall reduce the general case of Theorem
3.1
to the case where (
6.11
) is exact. When guaranteeing Brauer–Manin orthogonality of suitable adelic points on the fibers of
f
, we shall first obtain Brauer–Manin orthogonality to
\Br X_{\eta}
by combining Shafarevich’s ideas with the study of half-spin symbols (this is done in Section
10
and
11
, see in particular Proposition
11.2
for an explicit expression of the Brauer–Manin pairing associated to
\Br X_{\eta}
for a certain suitable choice of adelic points on the fibers), and afterwards we shall use the triple variation method mentioned in the introduction to deal with the cokernel of
r:\Br X_{\eta}\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)
(see Section
12
, and the closing argument in Section
13
).

7.
Representatives for
\Br X_{\eta}/f^{*}\Br\eta

For a smooth proper morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
with geometrically integral fibers and satisfying
R^{1}f_{*}\mu_{\infty}=R^{2}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=0
, we investigate the following two questions:

•

Given an element of
\Br X_{\eta}/f^{*}\Br\eta
, can a representative
b\in\Br f^{-1}(U)
be found for some
explicit
dense open
U\subset Y
?

•

If yes, can any control be attained on the residues of such a
b
?

The main result of this section, Theorem
7.2
, provides partial positive answers. Theorem
7.2
is formulated in Subsection
7.1
, proven in Subsection
7.2
, and applied to the setting of fibrations over Galois quasi-trivial tori in Subsection
7.3
. In Subsection
7.4
, we compute the local Brauer pairing associated to the representative just obtained. The resulting expression reveals “local half-spin symbols” arising, which was our motivation behind their treatment in Section
5
.

7.1.
Collections of augmented divisors and main theorem

Definition 7.1
.

For a regular integral Noetherian scheme
Y
, and pairwise distinct integral divisors
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}\subset Y
, a
collection of residue algebras

\mathfrak{A}
on the divisors
Z_{i}
’s is a collection of finite étale algebras
\mathfrak{A}\coloneqq\{A_{1}/\kappa(Z_{1}),\ldots,A_{r}/\kappa(Z_{r})\}
, where each
A_{i}
is a finite étale algebra over the function field
\kappa(Z_{i})
of
Z_{i}
.

Collections of residue algebras behave contravariantly with respect to dominant morphisms, in the following sense. For a dominant morphism
f:Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
of regular integral Noetherian schemes, and a collection
\mathfrak{A}=\{A_{i}/\kappa(Z_{i})\}_{1\leq i\leq r}
on integral divisors
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}
of
Y
such that the image
f(Y^{\prime})
is not contained in
\cup_{i}Z_{i}
, we define the
pullback
of
\mathfrak{A}
under
f
as

f^{-1}(\mathfrak{A})\coloneqq\{A^{\prime}_{i,j}/\kappa(Z^{\prime}_{i,j})\}_{1\leq i\leq r,1\leq j\leq r_{i}},

where, for each
i
,
\cup_{j=1}^{r_{i}}Z^{\prime}_{i,j}
is the decomposition into irreducible components of the reduced (possibly empty) divisor
f^{-1}(Z_{i})_{\text{red}}
, and
A^{\prime}_{i,j}\coloneqq\kappa(Z^{\prime}_{i,j})\otimes_{\kappa(Z_{i})}A_{i}
.

(Occasionally, we shall denote the pullback
f^{-1}(\mathfrak{A})
by
\mathfrak{A}\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}
.)

In this subsection, given
Y
,
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}
and
\mathfrak{A}
as in Definition
7.1
, we denote, for each
i
, by
\iota_{i}:\Spec\kappa(Z_{i})\hookrightarrow Y
the embedding of the generic point of
Z_{i}
in
Y
, and by
\varphi_{i}
the composition
\Spec A_{i}\rightarrow\Spec\kappa(Z_{i})\xrightarrow{\iota_{i}}Y
. We also denote by
U
the complement
Y\setminus\cup_{i}Z_{i}
, and by
j:U\rightarrow Y
the open embedding.

When
Y
is a curve, the first point in the following definition appears already in Section 2 of
[
22
]
.

Notation
.

For a collection of residue algebras
\mathfrak{A}=\{A_{1}/\kappa(Z_{1}),\ldots,A_{r}/\kappa(Z_{r})\}
on divisors
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}
of a regular integral Noetherian scheme
Y
, we define:

•

the group
(\Br Y)_{+}(\mathfrak{A})
as the subgroup of
\Br U
of those elements whose residue at each
Z_{i}
belongs to the kernel of the pullback
H^{1}(\kappa(Z_{i}),\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{1}(A_{i},\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})
;

•

the complex
\mathbb{G}_{m+}(\mathfrak{A})\in\mathcal{D}^{+}(\Sh_{Y})
of
\mathfrak{A}
-augmented invertible functions
as the complex

(7.1)

j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}\xrightarrow{u}\oplus_{i}\varphi_{i,*}{\mathbb{Z}},

supported in degrees
0
and
1
, where
u
is defined as the composition of the natural morphism
j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\oplus_{i}\iota_{i,*}\mathbb{Z}
with the natural embeddings
\iota_{i,*}{\mathbb{Z}}\hookrightarrow\varphi_{i,*}{\mathbb{Z}}
.

We denote by
v:\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m+}
the natural morphism in
\mathcal{D}^{+}(\Sh_{Y})
induced by the commutative diagram

(7.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{G}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\oplus_{i}\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

For the following theorem, recall that we have morphisms

r:\Br X_{\eta}\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y),\,\partial:\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)\rightarrow H^{3}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m}),

for smooth proper morphisms
f:X\rightarrow Y
such that
R^{1}f_{*}\mu_{\infty}=R^{2}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=0
.

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
Y
be a smooth geometrically integral variety over a field
k
of characteristic
0
, and
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a smooth proper morphism satisfying
R^{1}f_{*}\mu_{\infty}=R^{2}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=0
. Let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}
be smooth integral divisors on
Y
such that
\cup_{i}Z_{i}
is smooth, and
\mathfrak{A}=\{A_{i}/\kappa(Z_{i})\}_{i\in I}
be a collection of residue algebras. Let
\beta\in\Br X_{\eta}/f^{*}\Br\eta
be such that
\partial(r(\beta))\in H^{3}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m})
lies in the kernel of

H^{3}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m})\xrightarrow{v}H^{3}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m+}).

Then there exists
b\in(\Br X)_{+}(f^{-1}(\mathfrak{A}))
representing
\beta
.

7.2.
Proof of Theorem
7.2

We start with two general lemmas, the first of which is certainly (at least implicitly) well-known but the author could not find it explicitly stated in this generality in the literature.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
X
be a regular Noetherian scheme of residual characteristic
0
,
Z
be an integral smooth divisor of
X
,
\iota:\Spec\kappa(Z)\hookrightarrow X
be the embedding of its generic point in
X
, and
U
be a non-empty open subscheme of
X\setminus Z
. We denote by
j:U\hookrightarrow X
the open embedding. The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(X,j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(X,\iota_{*}\mathbb{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(U,\mathbb{G}_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(\kappa(Z),\mathbb{Z})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{Z}}

where the upper horizontal map is induced by the natural morphism
j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\iota_{*}\mathbb{Z}
, and the bottom horizontal map is the Witt residue (after identifying
H^{2}(\kappa(Z),\mathbb{Z})
with
H^{1}(\kappa(Z),\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})
), is commutative.

Proof.

Let
\zeta
be the generic point of
Z
. To prove the statement we may pullback the pair
(X,Z)
to the pair
(\Spec\mathcal{O}_{X,\zeta},\Spec\kappa(\zeta))
. In this manner we reduce to the case where
X
is the spectrum of a DVR,
U
is its generic point, and
Z
is its special point. In this special case, a proof may be found within the proof of Theorem 3.6.1 of
[
7
]
, see in particular the argument in p.86 of
ibid.
.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
Z
be a divisor of a smooth variety
Y
defined over a field of characteristic
0
,
U\coloneqq Y\setminus Z
be the complement of
Z
, and
j:U\hookrightarrow Y
be the open embedding. If
Z
is smooth, then
R^{n}j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}=0
for all
n\geq 1
.

Proof.

The étale sheaf
R^{n}j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}
is the sheafification in
Y_{\acute{e}t}
of the presheaf
Y^{\prime}\mapsto H^{n}(U\times_{Y}Y^{\prime},\mathbb{G}_{m})

[
34
, III.1.13]
. For
n=1
, this presheaf is
0
by Hilbert Theorem 90. For
n\geq 2
, this is a torsion presheaf by
[
7
, Lemma 3.5.3]
. So, for all
n\geq 2
,
(R^{n}j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})\otimes\mathbb{Q}=0
and then
R^{n}j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}\cong R^{n}j_{*}\mu_{\infty}
by the “
\infty
-Kummer sequence” (as used in Subsection
6.1
).
Finally, since
Z
is smooth, we have
R^{n}j_{*}\mu_{m}=0
for all
m\geq 1,\,n\geq 2
by Gysin purity
[
34
, VI.5.1]
, and so
R^{n}j_{*}\mu_{\infty}=0
by taking the limit.
∎

We also need the following.

Proposition 7.5
.

For a smooth variety
Y
over a field of characteristic
0
, and a collection of residue algebras
\mathfrak{A}=\{A_{i}/\kappa(Z_{i})\}_{i=1,\ldots,r}
on smooth integral divisors
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}\subset Y
, there is a natural isomorphism

H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m+})\cong(\Br Y)_{+}.

Moreover, if each
A_{i}
is integral, there are natural isomorphisms

H^{0}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m+})\cong k[Y]^{*},\quad H^{1}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m+})\cong\Pic Y.

Proof.

Taking hypercohomology of the exact triangle

(7.3)

\mathbb{G}_{m+}\rightarrow j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\oplus_{i}\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{+1}

induces a long exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow

\displaystyle H^{0}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m+})\rightarrow H^{0}(Y,j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow\oplus_{i}H^{0}(Y,\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow

\displaystyle H^{1}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m+})\rightarrow H^{1}(Y,j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow\oplus_{i}H^{1}(Y,\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow

\displaystyle H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m+})\rightarrow H^{2}(Y,j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow\oplus_{i}H^{2}(Y,\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\ldots\ .

For each
i
, we have
H^{1}(Y,\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})=0
since the Leray spectral sequence of
\varphi_{i}
gives an embedding
H^{1}(Y,\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})\hookrightarrow H^{1}(A_{i},\mathbb{Z})
, and the latter vanishes as
A_{i}
is a normal algebra. Moreover, by Lemma
7.4
, the Leray spectral sequence induces natural isomorphisms
H^{n}(Y,j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})\cong H^{n}(U,\mathbb{G}_{m})
for all
n\geq 0
.

Thus the long exact sequence above splits into two exact sequences:

(7.4)

0\rightarrow H^{0}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m+})\rightarrow H^{0}(U,\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow\oplus_{i}H^{0}(Y,\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{1}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m+})\rightarrow H^{1}(U,\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow 0,

and

(7.5)

0\rightarrow H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m+})\rightarrow H^{2}(U,\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow\oplus_{i}H^{2}(Y,\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\ldots\ .

Consider now, for each
i
, the commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(Y,j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(Y,\iota_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(Y,\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(U,\mathbb{G}_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(\kappa(Z_{i}),\mathbb{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(A_{i},\mathbb{Z})},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{i}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{Z_{i}}}

where the third vertical map is the edge map of the Leray spectral sequences of
\varphi_{i}
,
the first upper horizontal map is induced by the natural morphism of sheaves
j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\iota_{i,*}\mathbb{Z}
, the map
\partial_{Z_{i}}
is the Witt residue map (after identifying
H^{2}(\kappa(Z_{i}),\mathbb{Z})
with
H^{1}(\kappa(Z_{i}),\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})
), and the second bottom horizontal map is pullback along
\Spec A_{i}\rightarrow\Spec\kappa(Z_{i})
. The commutativity of the first square follows from Lemma
7.3
. Since
\iota_{i}
is an embedding, the pullback
\iota_{i}^{*}:H^{2}(Y,\iota_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{2}(\kappa(Z_{i}),\mathbb{Z})
coincides with the edge map of the Leray spectral spectral sequence of
\iota_{i}
. Hence the commutativity of the second square follows by the naturality of the Leray spectral sequence. The injectivity of the second and third vertical maps comes from Leray’s spectral sequence and the vanishings
R^{1}\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z}=R^{1}\iota_{i,*}\mathbb{Z}=0
(one has in general
R^{1}f_{*}\mathbb{Z}=0
for all morphisms of schemes
f:S\rightarrow S^{\prime}
with normal
S
).

The composition
H^{2}(U,\mathbb{G}_{m})\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{2}(Y,j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow H^{2}(Y,\iota_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{2}(Y,\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})
in the diagram is the morphism
H^{2}(U,\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow H^{2}(Y,\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})
appearing in (
7.5
). Hence, sequence (
7.5
) identifies
H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m+})
with the kernel of the composition

H^{2}(U,\mathbb{G}_{m})\xrightarrow{(\partial_{Z_{i}})}\oplus_{i}H^{2}(\kappa(Z_{i}),\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\oplus_{i}H^{2}(A_{i},\mathbb{Z}),

proving that
H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m+})
is naturally isomorphic to
(\Br Y)_{+}
.

Confronting (
7.4
) with the analog exact sequence in the degenerate case where
A_{i}=\kappa(Z_{i})
for all
i
, we obtain a commutative diagram:

(7.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{0}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{0}(U,\mathbb{G}_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\oplus_{i}H^{0}(Y,\iota_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(U,\mathbb{G}_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{0}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m+})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{0}(U,\mathbb{G}_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\oplus_{i}H^{0}(Y,\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m+})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(U,\mathbb{G}_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}

where
v
denotes the map of complexes (
7.2
). We have
H^{0}(Y,\iota_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})\cong H^{0}(\kappa(Z_{i}),\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
. Since
A_{i}
is integral, we also have
H^{0}(Y,\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})\cong H^{0}(A_{i},\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
. The natural map
H^{0}(Y,\iota_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{0}(Y,\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})
then gets identified with the identity
id:\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
.
The five lemma applied to the diagram (
7.6
) gives isomorphisms
H^{i}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m})\cong H^{i}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m+})
for
i=0,1
, concluding the proof of the first two isomorphisms.
∎

Proof of Theorem
7.2
.

The pullback
\mathfrak{A}\times_{Y}X
is
\{A^{\prime}_{i}/\kappa(Z^{\prime}_{i})\}
, where, for each
i
,
Z^{\prime}_{i}
denotes the pullback
f^{-1}(Z_{i})
, and
A^{\prime}_{i}
denotes the finite étale
\kappa(Z^{\prime}_{i})
-algebra
\kappa(Z^{\prime}_{i})\otimes_{\kappa(Z_{i})}A_{i}
. For each
i
, we denote by
\varphi^{\prime}_{i}
the morphism
\Spec A_{i}\rightarrow X
. We let
U^{\prime}\coloneqq f^{-1}(U)
, and
j^{\prime}:U^{\prime}\hookrightarrow X
be the open embedding.

To keep the notation compact, we denote by
[A^{\bullet}\rightarrow B^{\bullet}]
the mapping cone of a morphism
A^{\bullet}\rightarrow B^{\bullet}
of chain complexes in some abelian category
\mathcal{C}
.
Consider the morphism of exact triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{G}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rf_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\geq 1}Rf_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\oplus_{i}\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[Rf_{*}(j^{\prime}_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow\oplus_{i}Rf_{*}(\varphi^{\prime}_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\tau_{\geq 1}Rf_{*}(j^{\prime}_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow\oplus_{i}\tau_{\geq 1}Rf_{*}(\varphi^{\prime}_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})]}}
,
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

where the vertical maps are induced by the natural inclusions
\mathbb{G}_{m}\hookrightarrow j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}
and
\mathbb{G}_{m}\hookrightarrow j^{\prime}_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}
, and the exactness of the last row follows from the identifications
j_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}=f_{*}(j^{\prime}_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}),

\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z}=f_{*}\varphi^{\prime}_{i,*}\mathbb{Z}
.
Taking hypercohomology on
Y
, we get a commutative diagram with long exact rows, of which a fragment reads:

(7.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{3}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Br X)_{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)_{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{3}(Y,\mathbb{G}_{m+})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ldots,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

where
\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)_{+}\coloneqq H^{2}(Y,[\tau_{\geq 1}Rf_{*}(j^{\prime}_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow\oplus_{i}\tau_{\geq 1}Rf_{*}(\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})])
, and we are using the identification

\displaystyle H^{2}(Y,{[Rf_{*}(j^{\prime}_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow\oplus_{i}Rf_{*}(\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z})]})

\displaystyle\cong H^{2}(Y,Rf_{*}({[j^{\prime}_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\oplus_{i}\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z}]}))

\displaystyle\cong H^{2}(X,{[j^{\prime}_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\oplus_{i}\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z}]})

given by the natural isomorphism
H^{2}(Y,Rf_{*}-)\cong H^{2}(X,-)
provided by Grothendieck’s theorem on the composition of derived functors and the identification
H^{2}(X,{[j^{\prime}_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\oplus_{i}\varphi_{i,*}\mathbb{Z}]})\cong(\Br X)_{+}
given by Proposition
7.5
.
Consider now the element
r(\beta)\in\operatorname{Br}_{\text{hor }}(X/Y)\subset\operatorname{Br}_{\text{hor }}(X/Y)
. We have
\partial(v(r(\beta)))=v(\partial(r(\beta)))

=0
by hypothesis, so
v(r(\beta))
comes from an element
b\in(\Br X)_{+}
.

The proof is concluded once we show that

(\star)
the image of
b
under the map
r:\Br X_{\eta}\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Y)
is
r(\beta)
.

By functoriality of the objects involved, to prove
(\star)
we may restrict
Y
to any non-empty Zariski-open
V\subset Y
(replacing
Y
with
V
and
X
with
f^{-1}(V)=V\times_{Y}X
). Choosing
V\subset Y
to be any non-empty Zariski-open that is contained in
U
, we reduce to the case where
\mathfrak{A}
is empty, in which case the two rows in diagram (
7.7
) coincide and
v
is the identity, and the claim becomes tautologically true.
∎

7.3.
Application to fibrations over Galois quasi-trivial tori

In this subsection,
k
denotes a field of characteristic
0
whose every finite extension contains only finitely many roots of unity (e.g. a number field).

Notation
.

For the Galois group
\Gamma
of a finite Galois extension
E/k
, we let:

\displaystyle\Gamma^{*}:=\Gamma\setminus\{\text{id}\},

\displaystyle\Gamma[2]^{*}:=\{\tau\in\Gamma^{*}:\tau^{2}=\text{id}\},

\displaystyle\Gamma[2]^{**}:=\begin{cases}\Gamma[2]^{*}&\text{ if }8\nmid n,\\
\{\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{*}:\chi(\tau)\equiv\pm 1\bmod\,8\}&\text{ if }8\mid n,\end{cases}

where
n=n(E)
denotes the largest natural number such that
\mu_{n}\subset E
, and
\chi:\Gamma\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})^{*}
denotes the cyclotomic character.

Notation
.

For a Galois quasi-trivial
k
-torus
Q=R_{E/k}\mathbb{G}_{m}=\Spec(E[(y^{\sigma})^{\pm 1}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma})^{\Gamma}
, and
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{*}
, we let:

•

\bar{Z}_{\tau}(Q)
be the divisor of
Q\otimes_{K}\overline{K}
defined by the equation
y=y^{\tau}
;

•

Z_{\tau}(Q)\subset Q
be the unique integral divisor such that
Z_{\tau}(Q)\otimes_{k}\overline{k}
is the Galois-orbit of
\bar{Z}_{\tau}(Q)
.

We shall often write
\bar{Z}_{\tau},Z_{\tau}
instead of
\bar{Z}_{\tau}(Q),Z_{\tau}(Q)
. The field of definition of
\bar{Z}_{\tau}(Q)
is
E^{\tau}
, and thus the function field of
Z_{\tau}
coincides with that of its geometrically integral component
\{y=y^{\tau}\}
viewed as a divisor over
Q\otimes_{K}E^{\tau}
. The latter function field is the field of fractions of
E^{\tau}[y^{\sigma}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma}/(y-y^{\tau})
. We shall use the induced identification
K(Z_{\tau})=\Frac(E^{\tau}[y^{\sigma}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma}/(y-y^{\tau}))
below, especially to view
y
as an element of
K(Z_{\tau})
.

For
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{*}
, let
n_{\tau}:=\max\{r\mid n:\mu_{r}\subset E^{\tau}\}
. Let also
Q^{\sharp}:=Q\setminus\bigcup_{\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}}Z_{\tau}
.

Theorem 7.6
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Q
be a smooth proper morphism with geometrically integral fibers over a Galois quasi-trivial torus
Q=R_{E/k}\mathbb{G}_{m}
and such that
R^{1}f_{*}\mu_{\infty}=R^{2}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=0
. Every class in
\Br X_{\eta}/f^{*}\Br\eta
has a representative
b\in\Br f^{-1}(Q^{\sharp})\subset\Br X_{\eta}
such that, for all
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
:

(\star)

the residue
\partial_{Z^{\prime}_{\tau}}(b)\in H^{1}(k(Z^{\prime}_{\tau}),\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})
at
Z^{\prime}_{\tau}\coloneqq f^{-1}(Z_{\sigma})
is split by the cyclic extension
k(Z^{\prime}_{\tau})(y^{\frac{1}{n_{\tau}}})/k(Z^{\prime}_{\tau})
.

We shall prove Theorem
7.6
by first applying Theorem
7.2
to find a representative
b
whose residue at each
Z^{\prime}_{\tau}
is split by
\kappa(Z^{\prime}_{\tau})(y^{\frac{1}{n}})
. An elementary Galois-theoretic argument, presented at the end of this subsection, then shows that the residues must in fact already be split by
\kappa(Z^{\prime}_{\tau})(y^{\frac{1}{n_{\tau}}})
.

7.3.1.
Proof of Theorem
7.6

Consider the collection of residual algebras

\mathfrak{A}\coloneqq\{\kappa(Z_{\tau})(y^{\frac{1}{n}})/\kappa(Z_{\tau})\}_{\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}}

on the divisors
Z_{\tau},\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
of
Q
.
Let
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}={\mathfrak{A}}\otimes_{k}\overline{k}
be the base-change of
{\mathfrak{A}}
to
\bar{Q}:=Q\otimes_{k}\overline{k}
,
(\Br\bar{Q})_{+}\coloneqq(\Br\bar{Q})_{+}(\bar{\mathfrak{A}})
, and

(7.8)

(\Br X)_{+}:=\Br_{+}(\mathfrak{A}\times_{Q}X).

The following will be our key instrument in verifying the hypotheses of Theorem
7.2
.

Proposition 7.7
.

The restriction
H^{1}(K,(\Br\bar{Q})_{+})\rightarrow H^{1}(E,(\Br\bar{Q})_{+})
is injective.

We prove the proposition by providing an explicit basis of
(\Br\bar{Q})_{+}[n]
(see Lemma
7.8
).

For each unordered pair
\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}\in\binom{\Gamma}{2}
we choose an ordering
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
of the pair, and denote by
R\subset\Gamma^{2}
the set made of the resulting ordered pairs. Let
\binom{\Gamma}{2}_{**}\coloneqq\{\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}\in\binom{\Gamma}{2}:\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{-1}\in\Gamma[2]^{**}\},(\Gamma^{2})_{**}\coloneqq\{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in\Gamma^{2}:\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{-1}\in\Gamma[2]^{**}\}
, and
R_{**}\coloneqq R\cap(\Gamma^{2})_{**}
.

Lemma 7.8
.

The group
(\Br\overline{Q})_{+}[n]
is a free
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})
-module. The following sets form bases of its Tate twist
(\Br\overline{Q})_{+}[n](1)
:

•

the set
\{(y^{\sigma_{2}},y^{\sigma_{1}}-y^{\sigma_{2}})_{n}\}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in R_{**}}\cup\{(y^{\sigma_{2}},y^{\sigma_{1}})_{n}\}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in R}
;

•

the set
\{(y^{\sigma_{2}},y^{\sigma_{1}}-y^{\sigma_{2}})_{n}\}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in(\Gamma^{2})_{**}}\cup\{(y^{\sigma_{2}},y^{\sigma_{1}})_{n}\}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in R\setminus R_{**}}
.

Moreover, we have
(\Br\overline{Q})_{+}^{\Gamma_{E}}=(\Br\overline{Q})_{+}[n]
.

Proof.

We prove that the first set is a basis. Using the identity

(y^{\sigma_{2}},y^{\sigma_{1}}-y^{\sigma_{2}})_{n}-(y^{\sigma_{1}},y^{\sigma_{2}}-y^{\sigma_{1}})_{n}=(y^{\sigma_{2}},y^{\sigma_{1}})_{n},

which holds for all
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in\Gamma^{2},\sigma_{1}\neq\sigma_{2}
and follows from the relations
(a,-1)_{n}=0
,
(a,1-a)_{n}=0
and
(a,a)_{n}=0
(the first and last hold because
\sqrt{-1}\in\overline{k}(Q)
), it is then clear that the second is as well.

For
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in(\Gamma^{2})_{**}
, let
b_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\coloneqq(y^{\sigma_{2}},y^{\sigma_{1}}-y^{\sigma_{2}})_{n}
.
We claim that

(7.9)

(\Br\bar{Q})_{+}(1)=(\Br\bar{Q})(1)\oplus\langle b_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\rangle_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in R_{**}},

where in the second summand the elements are
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})
-linearly independent.

Let us prove the claim. The pullback
\mathfrak{A}\otimes_{k}\overline{k}
of the collection
\mathfrak{A}
to
\bar{Q}
is the collection
\{k(\bar{Z}_{\tau}^{\sigma})((y^{\sigma})^{\frac{1}{n}})/k(\bar{Z}_{\tau}^{\sigma})\}_{\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**},\sigma\in\langle\tau\rangle\setminus\Gamma}
. Thus
(\Br\bar{Q})_{+}
is the set of elements
b\in\Br\bar{Q}^{\sharp}
whose residue at each
\bar{Z}_{\tau}^{\sigma}
is split by
k(\bar{Z}_{\tau}^{\sigma})((y^{\sigma})^{\frac{1}{n}})
, or, equivalently, whose residue at each
\bar{Z}_{\tau}^{\sigma}
is a multiple of the Kummer character
\widehat{y^{\sigma}}:\Gamma_{k(\bar{Z}_{\tau}^{\sigma})}\rightarrow\mu_{n}\cong\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{Z}/\mathbb{Z}
of order
n
.

For each
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in R_{**}
, the element
b_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\coloneqq(y^{\sigma_{2}},y^{\sigma_{2}}-y^{\sigma_{1}})_{n}
is unramified over
\bar{Q}\setminus\bar{Z}_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{-1}}^{\sigma_{2}}
and its residue at
\bar{Z}_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{-1}}^{\sigma_{2}}
is the Kummer character
\widehat{y^{\sigma_{2}}}
. Combined with the discussion in the previous paragraph, this implies that every
b\in(\Br\bar{Q})_{+}
may be written uniquely in the form

b=b_{0}+\sum_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in R_{**}}\alpha_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}b_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}},

with
b_{0}\in(\Br\bar{Q})
and
\alpha_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\in(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(-1)
(by taking
\alpha_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}
to be the unique class such that
\partial_{Z_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(b)=\alpha_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\widehat{y^{\sigma_{2}}}
). This proves the claim.

The group
(\Br\bar{Q})[n]
is the free
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})
-module with basis formed by the elements
(y^{\sigma_{2}},y^{\sigma_{1}})_{n}
as
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
varies in
R
, see e.g.
[
7
, Proposition 9.1.2]
. Now (
7.9
) gives the basis (i) for
(\Br\bar{Q})_{+}
.

As for the last statement, taking
\Gamma_{E}
-invariants in (
7.9
) and noting that each
b_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}
is invariant under the action of
\Gamma_{E}
and has order dividing
n
, it suffices to prove that
(\Br\bar{Q})^{\Gamma_{E}}=(\Br\bar{Q})[n]
. Since the torus
Q_{E}
is split, the
\Gamma_{E}
-module
\Br\bar{Q}
is isomorphic to
(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(-1))^{d(d-1)/2},d=[E:k]
(see again
[
7
, Proposition 9.1.2]
), and so
(\Br\bar{Q})^{\Gamma_{E}}=(\Br\bar{Q})[n]
as
n
was defined as the maximum order of a root of unity that is contained in
E
.
∎

We also need the following simple fact.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
n
be a natural number and
a\in(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})^{*}
be such that
a^{2}=1
. Let
C_{2}
be the group with
2
elements, and
M
be the
C_{2}
-module
\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
where the generator of
C_{2}
acts via multiplication by
a
. Then

H^{1}(C_{2},M)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }n\text{ is odd};\\
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}&\text{if }v_{2}(n)\leq 2;\\
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}&\text{if }v_{2}(n)\geq 3\text{ and }a\equiv\pm 1\bmod 8;\\
0&\text{if }v_{2}(n)\geq 3\text{ and }a\equiv\pm 3\bmod 8.\end{cases}

Proof.

We may assume that
n=p^{m}
is a prime-power. The case
a=1
is immediate, while
a\neq 1
is a special case of
[
36
, Lemma 9.1.4]
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.7
.

We prove that
H^{1}(\Gamma,(\Br\bar{Q})_{+}^{\Gamma_{E}})=0
. By the inflation-restriction sequence, this is equivalent to the statement. Let
M\coloneqq(\Br\bar{Q})_{+}^{\Gamma_{E}}
. For
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in(\Gamma^{2})_{**}
, let
B_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\coloneqq\langle\,(y^{\sigma_{2}},y^{\sigma_{1}}-y^{\sigma_{2}})_{n}\,\rangle<M
. For
\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}\in\binom{\Gamma}{2}
, let
C_{\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}}\coloneqq\langle\,(y^{\sigma_{1}},y^{\sigma_{2}})_{n}\,\rangle<M
.
By Lemma
7.8
, we have

M=\bigoplus_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in(\Gamma^{2})_{**}}B_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\oplus\bigoplus_{\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}\in\binom{\Gamma}{2}\setminus\binom{\Gamma}{2}_{**}}C_{\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}}.

The left action of
\Gamma
on
M
permutes the summands. More precisely, we have
\gamma(B_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}})=B_{\gamma\sigma_{1},\gamma\sigma_{2}}
and
\gamma(C_{\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}})=C_{\{\gamma\sigma_{1},\gamma\sigma_{2}\}}
. We use the following fact
[
4
, Corollary III.5.4]
.

Fact
Let
G
be a profinite group, and
M
a discrete
G
-module whose underlying abelian group is of the form
M=\bigoplus_{i\in I}M_{i}
. Assume that the
G
-action permutes the factors according to some action of
G
on
I
. Let for each
i
,
H_{i}<G
be the isotropy subgroup of
M_{i}
. Let
J
be a set of representatives for the
G
-action on
I
. Then
M\cong\oplus_{i\in J}\Ind^{G}_{H_{i}}M_{i}
.

The natural left
\Gamma
-action on
(\Gamma^{2})_{**}
is free. The left
\Gamma
-action on
\binom{\Gamma}{2}\setminus\binom{\Gamma}{2}_{**}
is free on the subset
\binom{\Gamma}{2}\setminus\binom{\Gamma}{2}_{*}\subset\binom{\Gamma}{2}\setminus\binom{\Gamma}{2}_{**}
, while the stabilizer of an element
\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}\in\binom{\Gamma}{2}_{*}\setminus\binom{\Gamma}{2}_{**}
is the subgroup
\{1,\gamma\}<\Gamma,\ \gamma=\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{-1}
. So

M\cong\Ind^{\Gamma}_{e}M_{0}\oplus\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Gamma[2]^{*}\setminus\Gamma[2]^{**}}\Ind^{\Gamma}_{\{1,\gamma\}}C_{e,\gamma},

for some abelian group
M_{0}
, and where
C_{e,\gamma}
is the
\{1,\gamma\}
-module
\langle\,y\cup y^{\gamma}\,\rangle=\linebreak(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(-1)\cdot(y\cup y^{\gamma})
, on which
\gamma
acts via multiplication by
-\chi(\gamma)
, where
\chi
denotes the cyclotomic character. We have
\gamma\notin\Gamma[2]^{**}
and so either
n
is odd or
8\mid n,\,\chi(\gamma)\equiv\pm 3\bmod 8
by definition. Hence
H^{1}(\{1,\gamma\},C_{e,\gamma})=0
by Lemma
7.9
and
H^{1}(\Gamma,M)=0
by Shapiro’s lemma.
∎

Proposition 7.10
.

We have that

\Ker(H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{Q})\rightarrow H^{1}(K,(\Br\bar{Q})_{+}))=\Ker(H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{Q})\rightarrow H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{\eta})).

Proof.

The inclusions
\Br\bar{Q}\subset(\Br\bar{Q})_{+}\subset\Br\bar{\eta}
induce a commutative diagram:

(7.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{Q})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(K,(\Br\bar{Q})_{+})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{\eta})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(E,\Br\bar{Q})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(E,(\Br\bar{Q})_{+})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(E,\Br\bar{\eta})}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces res}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces res}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces res}

The second restriction is injective by Proposition
7.7
.
The bottom horizontal composition is also injective. In fact, for any split torus
T=\Spec k[t_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,t_{r}^{\pm 1}]
over a field
k
of characteristic
0
with generic point
\eta
, the natural maps
H^{n}(T\otimes_{k}\overline{k},\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow H^{n}(\eta\otimes_{k}\overline{k},\mathbb{G}_{m}),\,n\geq 2
are split injections of
\Gamma_{k}
-modules by Gille and Pianzola’s
[
15
, Proposition 3.1(2)]
(the retraction is provided by the composition that we already used in the proof of Lemma
6.6
). Since
Q_{E}
is a split
E
-torus, we get that
\Br\bar{Q}\subset\Br\bar{\eta}
is a split injection of
\Gamma_{E}
-modules by taking
n=2
, and the injectivity follows.
Now the lemma follows by a diagram chase.
∎

We are now ready to start the proof of Theorem
7.6
.

Proof of Theorem
7.6
, first part.

Let
b\in\Br X_{\eta}
. In this first part, we prove that there exists
b_{0}\in\Br\eta
such that
b-f^{*}b_{0}\in(\Br X)_{+}
.

Let
Q^{o}\subset Q
denote the complement
Q\setminus\operatorname{Sing}(Z_{\Gamma[2]^{**}})
of the singular locus
\operatorname{Sing}(Z_{\Gamma[2]^{**}})
of
Z_{\Gamma[2]^{**}}:=\bigcup_{\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}}Z_{\tau}
.
We shall apply Theorem
7.2
to
f|_{f^{-1}(Q^{o})}:f^{-1}(Q^{o})\rightarrow Q^{o}
.

Let
E^{p,q}_{r},{\vphantom{+}}^{+}E^{p,q}_{r}
and
{\vphantom{\eta}}{}^{\eta}E^{p,q}_{r}
denote the
r
-th pages of the three Hochschild-Serre spectral sequences:

\displaystyle H^{p}(K,H^{q}(Q^{o}\otimes_{K}\overline{K},\mathbb{G}_{m}))

\displaystyle\Rightarrow H^{p+q}(Q^{o},\mathbb{G}_{m}),

\displaystyle H^{p}(K,H^{q}(Q^{o}\otimes_{K}\overline{K},\mathbb{G}_{m+}))

\displaystyle\Rightarrow H^{p+q}(Q^{o},\mathbb{G}_{m+}),

\displaystyle H^{p}(K,H^{q}(\eta\otimes_{K}\overline{K},\mathbb{G}_{m}))

\displaystyle\Rightarrow H^{p+q}(\eta,\mathbb{G}_{m}).

For each of these, we denote by
F_{HS}^{p}H^{p+q}
the associated abutment filtration on
H^{p+q}
. Proposition
7.5
provides natural isomorphisms
{\vphantom{+}}{}^{+}E^{p,q}_{2}\cong E^{p,q}_{2}
for all
q\leq 2
, induced by the morphism
v:\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m+}
. We have
\Pic(Q\otimes_{K}\overline{K})=0
and
H^{3}(K,\overline{K}[Q]^{*})=0
(resp.
\Pic\bar{\eta}=0
and
H^{3}(K,\overline{K}(\eta))=0
), where the second follows from the vanishings
H^{3}(K,\overline{K}^{*})=0
and
H^{3}(K,\widehat{Q})\cong H^{3}(K,\Ind^{E}_{K}\mathbb{Z})=H^{3}(E,\mathbb{Z})=0
which hold because
K
and
E
are number fields. These give
E^{3,0}_{2}=E^{2,1}_{2}=0
(resp.
{\vphantom{\eta}}{}^{\eta}E^{3,0}_{2}={\vphantom{\eta}}^{\eta}E^{2,1}_{2}=0
), and thus the same holds for
{\vphantom{+}}{}^{+}E
.

Comparing the abutments in position
(p,q)=(1,2)
of the three sequences, we then get commutative diagrams

(7.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{2}_{HS}H^{3}(Q^{o},\mathbb{G}_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(K,\Br(Q^{o}\otimes_{K}\overline{K}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{2}_{HS}H^{3}(Q^{o},\mathbb{G}_{m+})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(K,(\Br(Q^{o}\otimes_{K}\overline{K}))_{+})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{2}_{HS}H^{3}(Q^{o},\mathbb{G}_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(K,\Br(Q^{o}\otimes_{K}\overline{K}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{2}_{HS}H^{3}(\eta,\mathbb{G}_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{\eta})}}

where the horizontal maps are injective, and the vertical maps in the second diagram are induced by the pullback along
\eta\rightarrow Q
.

Consider now the maps

r:\Br X_{\eta}\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(f^{-1}(Q^{o})/Q^{o}),\ \ \partial:\Br_{\text{hor}}(f^{-1}(Q^{o})/Q^{o})\rightarrow H^{3}(Q^{o},\mathbb{G}_{m})

associated with the morphism
f^{-1}(Q^{o})\rightarrow Q^{o}
. The element
\partial(r(b))\in H^{3}(Q^{o},\mathbb{G}_{m})
lies in
F^{2}_{HS}H^{3}(Q^{o},\mathbb{G}_{m})
by Lemma
6.6
. Its image in
{F^{2}_{HS}H^{3}(\eta,\mathbb{G}_{m})}\subset H^{3}(\eta,\mathbb{G}_{m})
vanishes because
b\in\Br X_{\eta}
.

Since the complement of
Q^{o}
in
Q
has codimension at most
2
, purity for the Brauer group gives us identities
\Br(Q^{o}\otimes_{K}\overline{K})=\Br(Q\otimes_{k}\overline{K}),\Br(Q^{o}\otimes_{K}\overline{K})_{+}=\Br(Q\otimes_{k}\overline{K})_{+}
and
(\Br f^{-1}(Q^{o}))_{+}=(\Br X)_{+}
. In view of these identities, Proposition
7.10
tells us that

\displaystyle\Ker(H^{1}(K,\Br(Q^{o}\otimes_{K}\overline{K}))\rightarrow H^{1}(K,(\Br(Q^{o}\otimes_{K}\overline{K}))_{+}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Ker(H^{1}(K,\Br(Q^{o}\otimes_{K}\overline{K}))\rightarrow H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{\eta})).

A diagram chase on the two diagrams (
7.11
) now shows that
\partial(r(b))
maps trivially to
{F^{2}_{HS}H^{3}(Q^{o},\mathbb{G}_{m+})}\subset H^{3}(Q^{o},\mathbb{G}_{m+})
. Theorem
7.2
then tells us that there exists
b_{0}\in\Br\eta
such that
b-f^{*}b_{0}\in(\Br f^{-1}(Q^{o}))_{+}=(\Br X)_{+}
.
∎

To conclude the proof, we need the following elementary lemma:

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
F
be a field of characteristic
0
, and
F^{\prime}:=F(\sqrt[n]{u})
, where
u\in F^{*}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
are such that
T^{n}-u
is irreducible over the field
F(\mu_{n})
. Then the maximal abelian subextension of
F^{\prime}/F
is
F(\sqrt[m]{u})
, where
m
is the maximal divisor of
n
with
\mu_{m}\subset F^{*}
.

Proof.

This is an exercise in Kummer theory. ∎

Proof of Theorem
7.6
, second and final part.

Applying Theorem
7.2
we find
b\in(\Br X)_{+}
such that
r(b)=\beta
. Recalling the definition of
(\Br X)_{+}
, we have that
b\in\Br f^{-1}(Q^{\sharp})
and the residue
r_{\tau}\in H^{1}(\Gamma_{k(Z^{\prime}_{\tau})},\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})
of
b
at
Z^{\prime}_{\tau}
is split by the extension
k(Z^{\prime}_{\tau})(y^{\frac{1}{n}})/k(Z^{\prime}_{\tau})
for each
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
. Since the splitting field of
r_{\tau}
is (cyclic and hence) abelian, Lemma
7.11
then implies that
r_{\tau}
is split by
k(Z^{\prime}_{\tau})(y^{\frac{1}{m_{\tau}}})
, with
m_{\tau}=\max\{d:d\mid n\text{ and }\mu_{d}\subset k(Z^{\prime}_{\tau})\}
. The algebraic closure of
k
in
k(Z^{\prime}_{\tau})
is
E^{\tau}
, and so
m_{\tau}=n_{\tau}=\max\{d:d\mid n\text{ and }\mu_{d}\subset E^{\tau}\}
, concluding the proof.
∎

7.4.
Relation with half-spin symbols

In this subsection, let
K
be a number field,
Q:=R_{E/K}\mathbb{G}_{m}=\Spec(E[(y^{\sigma})^{\pm 1}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma})^{\Gamma}
be a Galois quasi-trivial
K
-torus,
n
be the largest natural number such that
\mu_{n}\subset E
, and
S
be a finite set of places of
K
that contains the places dividing
n,\Delta_{E/K}
or
\infty
.
We let
\mathcal{Q}:=\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{E,S}[(y^{\sigma})^{\pm 1}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma})^{\Gamma}
, and let
\mathcal{Z}_{\tau}
be the Zariski-closure in
\mathcal{Q}
of
Z_{\tau}
.

The following lemma relates the Brauer pairing with classes whose residues are the one that appeared earlier in this section with the (local components of) half-spin symbols.

Lemma 7.12
.

Let
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}
be a flat morphism with
\mathcal{X}
excellent, regular, integral and Noetherian, and
b\in\Br f^{-1}(\mathcal{Q}^{\sharp})
be an element whose residue on each irreducible component
\mathcal{W}_{\tau}
of
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{\tau}:=f^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{\tau})
is
a_{\tau}\cdot\hat{y}\in H^{1}(k(\mathcal{W}_{\tau}),\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})
, for a constant
a_{\tau}\in(\mathbb{Z}/n_{\tau}\mathbb{Z})(-1)
that only depends on
\tau
(and not on the chosen component
\mathcal{W}_{\tau}
). Then for every
v\notin S
and
x\in\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
we have

\inv_{v}(b(x))=\sum_{\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}}a_{\tau}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\mid v\\
w\in M_{E^{\tau}}\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{q}{\mathcal{I}_{w}}\right]_{n_{\tau}},

where
q=f(x)
, and
\mathcal{I}_{w}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{E^{\tau},w}
denotes the power of the uniformizer with exponent
\tilde{w}(q-q^{\tau})
, for a place
\tilde{w}|w
of
E
.

(As is tacitly assumed in the statement, the ideal
\mathcal{I}_{w}
is independent of the choice of
\tilde{w}
, since
\tilde{w}^{\tau}(q-q^{\tau})=\tilde{w}(q^{\tau}-q)=\tilde{w}(q-q^{\tau})
.)

Proof.

The morphism
x:\Spec\mathcal{O}_{v}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
factors through the spectrum of the local ring
\mathcal{O}_{X,\bar{x}}
at the special point
\bar{x}
of
x
.
In particular, there is no loss of generality in substituting
\mathcal{X}
with
\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{X,\bar{x}})
, and we assume that
\mathcal{X}
is local affine with special point
\bar{x}
.

For each
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
, let
\mathcal{Q}_{\tau}:=\mathcal{Q}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{E^{\tau},S}=\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{E^{\tau},S}[(y^{\tau})^{\pm 1}]_{\tau\in\Gamma})^{\tau}
. In
\mathcal{Q}_{\tau}
, let
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{id,\tau}
be the divisor defined by the
\tau
-equivariant equation
y=y^{\tau}
. Note that
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{id,\tau}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{E^{\tau},S}}\mathcal{O}_{E,S}=\mathcal{Z}_{id,\tau}
. The restriction
y|_{\mathcal{Z}_{id,\tau}}
is invariant under
\tau
, and so defines by étale-descent a function on
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{id,\tau}
, which is invertible because
y
is.

The point
q\in\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
defines by pullback a point
q_{\tau}:=q\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K,S}}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\tau},S}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\tau}(\mathcal{O}_{v}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K,S}}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\tau},S})
. Let
\bar{q}_{\tau}\subset\mathcal{Q}_{\tau}
be its subscheme of closed points, and
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Q}_{\tau},\bar{q}_{\tau}}:=\bigcap_{\xi\in\bar{q}_{\tau}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Q}_{\tau},\xi}
be the associated semi-local stalk.
Consider now a function
\tilde{y}_{\tau}\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Q}_{\tau},\bar{q}_{\tau}}^{\times}
whose restriction to
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{id,\tau}\cap\Spec\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Q}_{\tau},\bar{q}_{\tau}}
is equal to the restriction of
y
(in the sense of the previous paragraph), and a uniformizer
f_{\tau}\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Q}_{\tau},\bar{q}_{\tau}}
for the divisor
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{id,\tau}\cap\Spec\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Q}_{\tau},\bar{q}_{\tau}}
.

In a Zariski-neighbourhood of
\bar{q}_{\tau}
, the (twisted) Brauer class

(f_{\tau},\tilde{y}_{\tau})_{n_{\tau}}\in\Br(k(\mathcal{Q}_{\tau}))(1)

can ramify only on
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{id,\tau}
(and only if
\bar{q}\in\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{id,\tau}
, otherwise it is unramified), with residue the Kummer character
\hat{y}\in H^{1}(k(\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{id,\tau}),\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})(1)
of order
n_{\tau}
.

Since the norm of
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{id,\tau}
to
\mathcal{Q}
is
\mathcal{Z}_{\tau}
and the two divisors have the same function field, the functoriality of residues under corestriction
[
7
, Prop. 1.4.7]
gives that the residue of

\cores_{\mathcal{O}_{E^{\tau},S}/\mathcal{O}_{K,S}}(f_{\tau},\tilde{y}_{\tau})_{n_{\tau}}\in\Br(k(\mathcal{Q}))(1)

at
\mathcal{Z}_{\tau}
is also
\hat{y}\in H^{1}(k(\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{id,\tau}),\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})(1)
under the identification
k(\mathcal{Z}_{\tau})=k(\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{id,\tau})
, and it has no other residues in a neighbourhood of
\bar{q}
.

So by functoriality of residues under pullback
[
7
, Theorem 3.7.5]
, the residues of
f^{*}b_{0}
with

b_{0}:=\sum_{\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}}a_{\tau}\cores_{\mathcal{O}_{E^{\tau},S}/\mathcal{O}_{K,S}}(f_{\tau},\tilde{y}_{\tau})_{w,n_{\tau}}\in\Br k(\mathcal{Q})

are the pullback of the residues of
b_{0}
. These pullbacks are none other (by the very choice of
b_{0}
) than the residues of
b
. Thus
b-f^{*}b_{0}
lies in
\Br\mathcal{X}
by purity for the Brauer group. In particular:

\inv_{v}(b(x))=\inv_{v}(b_{0}(q))=\sum_{\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}}a_{\tau}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\mid v\\
w\in M_{E^{\tau}}\end{subarray}}[f_{\tau}(q_{\tau}),\tilde{y}_{\tau}(q_{\tau})]_{w,n_{\tau}},

where we are using the square brackets to denote the additive analog of the Hilbert symbol (with values in
(\mathbb{Z}/n_{\tau}\mathbb{Z})(1)
instead of
\mu_{n_{\tau}}
). Here both
f_{\tau}(q_{\tau})
and
\tilde{y}_{\tau}(q_{\tau})
take their values in
(K_{v}\otimes_{K}E^{\tau})^{*}=\prod_{w\mid v,w\in M_{E}^{\tau}}(E^{\tau})_{w}^{*}
, and the symbol
[-,-]_{w,n}
is meant as the taking the (additive) Hilbert symbol after projecting both components to
(E^{\tau})_{w}^{*}
.

Since, for each
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
,
\tilde{y}_{\tau}(q_{\tau})
is integral by construction, in the sense that it lies in
(\mathcal{O}_{v}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K,S}}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\tau},S})=\prod_{w\mid v,w\in M_{E}^{\tau}}\mathcal{O}_{E^{\tau},w}^{*}
and
w(f_{\tau}(q_{\tau}))
is the local intersection number
(\mathcal{Z}_{id,\tau},q_{\tau})_{w}=\tilde{w}(q-q^{\tau})
(for
\tilde{w}
any place extending
w
to
E
, which is necessarily unramified), we have

(f_{\tau}(q_{\tau}),\tilde{y}_{\tau}(q_{\tau}))_{w,n_{\tau}}=\left(\frac{\tilde{y}_{\tau}(q_{\tau})}{w}\right)_{n_{\tau}}^{\tilde{w}(q-q^{\tau})}=\left(\frac{q}{w}\right)_{n_{\tau}}^{\tilde{w}(q-q^{\tau})},

giving the sought formula.
∎

8.
A multi-section

The purpose of this section is to construct a multi-section of the fibration appearing in Theorem
3.1
. See Theorem
8.1
and its Corollary
8.3
.

In this section, let
k
be a field of characteristic
0
, and
\overline{k}
be an algebraic closure.

A
pointing
of a finite étale algebra
E/k
is a
k
-homomorphism
\iota:E\rightarrow\overline{k}
. Fix for the rest of this section a finite étale algebra
E/k
and a pointing
\iota
.

Let
Q\coloneqq\Spec\left(\overline{k}[y_{\sigma}^{\pm 1}]_{\sigma\in\Hom_{k}(E,\overline{k})}\right)^{\Gamma_{k}}
be the quasi-trivial torus associated to
E
.

Covers of
Q
.

Let
\mathcal{S}:=\Hom_{k}(E,\overline{k})
, and:

\displaystyle R_{\infty}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\overline{k}[y_{\sigma}^{\pm\frac{1}{\infty}}]_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}}.

For
1\leq n\leq\infty
and
k\subset L\subset\overline{k}
, we let:

\displaystyle R_{L,m}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\overline{k}[y_{\sigma}^{\pm\frac{1}{n}}]_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}}\right)^{\Gamma_{L}}\subset R_{\infty},

and, if
\Gamma_{L}
acts trivially on
\mathcal{S}
, we let:

\displaystyle\ \ R_{L,m,(1)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\overline{k}[y_{\iota}^{\pm 1},y_{\sigma}^{\pm\frac{1}{n}}]_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\iota\}}\right)^{\Gamma_{L}}=L[y_{\iota}^{\pm 1},y_{\sigma}^{\pm\frac{1}{n}}]_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\iota\}}.

The algebras
R_{L,m}
(resp. the algebras
R_{L,m,(1)}
) form a filtered system when ordered by inclusion. This order is equivalent (in both cases) to the one induced by the partial order defined by
(L,m)\preccurlyeq(L^{\prime},m^{\prime})
if and only if
m|m^{\prime}
and
L\subset L^{\prime}
. We let

Q^{\infty}\coloneqq\Spec R_{\infty},\ \ Q^{L,m}\coloneqq\Spec R_{L,m},\ \ Q^{L,m,(1)}\coloneqq\Spec R_{L,m,(1)}

be the corresponding profinite étale covers of
Q
.

We say that a pair
(L,m)
is
finite
if
m<\infty
and
L/k
is a finite extension. Note that

\lim\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rightarrow\\
(L,m)\text{ finite}\end{subarray}}R_{L,m}=R_{\infty,\overline{k}},\ \lim\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rightarrow\\
(L,m)\text{ finite}\end{subarray}}R_{L,m,(1)}=R_{\infty,\overline{k},(1)},

(8.1)

\lim\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\leftarrow\\
(L,m)\text{ finite}\end{subarray}}Q^{L,m}=Q^{\infty,\overline{k}},\ \lim\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\leftarrow\\
(L,m)\text{ finite}\end{subarray}}Q^{L,m,(1)}=Q^{\infty,\overline{k},(1)}.

We abbreviate
R_{\infty,\overline{k}},Q^{\infty,\overline{k}},\ldots
to
R_{\infty},Q^{\infty},\ldots
.

Let
\bar{\Delta}_{\sigma,\tau}\subset Q\otimes_{k}\overline{k},\sigma,\tau\in\mathcal{S},\sigma\neq\tau
be the divisor defined by the equation
y_{\sigma}=y_{\tau}
. Let
\bar{\Delta}=\cup_{\sigma,\tau:\sigma\neq\tau}\bar{\Delta}_{\sigma,\tau}
. This union is Galois-equivariant, let
\Delta\subset Q
be such that
\Delta\otimes_{k}\overline{k}=\bar{\Delta}.

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
T\rightarrow Q
be an epimorphism of
k
-tori,
X
be a
T
-variety, and
f:X\rightarrow Q
be a
T
-equivariant morphism. Assume that
f
is proper with rationally connected fibers. Then there exists a multi-section
s_{\infty}
as in the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q^{\infty,(1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Moreover, for any such
s_{\infty}
, and any
b\in\Br f^{-1}(Q\setminus\Delta)
such that the residue of
b\otimes\overline{k}
at the divisor
f^{-1}(\bar{\Delta}_{\sigma,\tau})
is split by a power of
y_{\sigma}
(equiv.
y_{\tau}
) for all
\sigma\neq\tau
, we have
s_{\infty}^{*}b=0
.

We use the following in the proof.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
X
be a rationally connected variety defined over an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
0
, and
C
be a finite cyclic group acting on
X
. Then
X(k)^{C}\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

We embed
C
in
\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Let
Z
be the contracted product
Z\times^{C}\mathbb{G}_{m}
. The “second factor projection”
\pi:Z\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}/C\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
has a section
\sigma
by the Graber–Harris–Starr Theorem
[
16
, Theorem 1.1]
. Consider the
C
-equivariant cartesian diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{G}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{k}\mathbb{G}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{G}_{m}/C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

The section
\sigma
of the bottom row induces a
C
-equivariant section
\sigma^{\prime}
of the top row. Projecting this section to
X
, we get a
C
-equivariant morphism
\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow X
. By the valuative criterion of properness, this morphism extends to a morphism
\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow X
. Since
0
and
\infty
are fixed by
C
, their images in
C
are too.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.1
.

We may assume without loss of generality that
k
is algebraically closed. Then
E=k^{d}
for a natural number
d
, and
Q=\Spec k[y_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,y_{d}^{\pm 1}]
,
Q^{\infty}=\Spec k[y_{1}^{\pm\frac{1}{\omega}},\ldots,y_{d}^{\pm\frac{1}{\omega}}],Q^{\infty,1}=\Spec k[y_{1}^{\pm 1},y_{2}^{\pm\frac{1}{\omega}},\ldots,y_{d}^{\frac{1}{\omega}}]
. (Here
\mathcal{S}
degenerates to just the various projections
k^{d}\rightarrow k
, which we enumerate
1,\ldots,d
. Up to permutation of the coordinates, we may always assume that
\iota
is the first projection).

We prove the first part. The epimorphism of tori
T\rightarrow Q
admits a multi-section
Q^{\infty}\rightarrow T
in the category of
k
-group schemes. The
T
-action then induces a
Q^{\infty}
-action on
X
with respect to which the fibration
f:X\rightarrow Q
is
Q^{\infty}
-equivariant. This induces an action of the procyclic group
I\coloneqq\Ker(Q^{\infty}\rightarrow Q^{\infty,(1)})\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
on the fibers of
f
, which are rationally connected. Since
X
is of finite type over
k
, this action must factor through a finite quotient of
I
, and thus there is at least one fixed point
x\in X(k)
by Lemma
8.1
. In particular
Q^{\infty}\cdot x=Q^{\infty,(1)}\cdot x
, and this orbit provides the sought multi-section
s_{\infty}:Q^{\infty,(1)}\rightarrow X
.

We now prove the second statement. Let
Q_{\Delta}^{\infty,(1)}\coloneqq Q^{\infty,(1)}\times_{Q}(Q\setminus\Delta)
, and let
n
be the order of
b
. Let us first prove that
s_{\infty}^{*}b
is contained in the subgroup
\Br Q^{\infty,(1)}
of
\Br Q_{\Delta}^{\infty,(1)}
. Let
\partial_{ij}(b)\in H^{1}(\kappa(D_{ij}),\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})
be the residue of
b
at
f^{-1}(\bar{\Delta}_{ij})
, and write
\partial_{ij}(b)=c_{ij}\cdot\widehat{(y_{j})}_{n}
for all ordered pairs
i<j
with
c_{ij}
lying in
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(-1)
. The algebra

b^{\prime}\coloneqq b-\sum_{i<j}c_{ij}(y_{j},y_{i}-y_{j})_{n}\quad\in\Br(X\setminus f^{-1}(\Delta))

has trivial residues on all divisors of
X
. By purity for the Brauer group, it
belongs to
\Br X\subset\Br(X\setminus f^{-1}(\Delta))
. Since the functions
y_{j}
with
j\geq 2
are infinitely divisible on
Q_{\Delta}^{\infty,(1)}
, and
j
is greater or equal than
2
for all pairs of indices
(i,j)
appearing in the sum above, the cyclic algebras
(y_{j},y_{i}-y_{j})_{n_{ij}}
appearing in the sum pull back to the trivial algebra on
Q_{\Delta}^{\infty,(1)}
. Thus
s_{\infty}^{*}b=s_{\infty}^{*}b^{\prime}
, which lies in
\Br Q^{\infty,(1)}
.
Finally, note that the Brauer group of
Q^{\infty,(1)}
, i.e. the Brauer group of the algebra
k[y_{1}^{\pm 1},y_{2}^{\pm\frac{1}{\omega}},\ldots,y_{d}^{\pm\frac{1}{\omega}}]
, is trivial (as follows e.g. by taking the appropriate limit in
[
7
, Proposition 9.1.2]
). This proves that
s_{\infty}^{*}b=0
and concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 8.3
.

Let
f
be as in Theorem
3.1
with
Q
Galois, and
B\subset(\Br X)_{+}
be a finite subgroup. Then there exists a finite pair
(L,m)
and a multi-section
s
as in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q^{L,m,(1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

such that
s^{*}B=0
.

Proof.

The elements of
(\Br X)_{+}
lie in
\Br f^{-1}(Q\setminus\Delta)
and satisfy the assumption of Theorem
8.1
. Thus the corollary follows from Theorem
8.1
by a limit argument, using that
\operatorname{Mor}(Q^{\infty,(1)},X)=\lim\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rightarrow\\
(L,m)\text{ finite}\end{subarray}}\operatorname{Mor}(Q^{L,m,(1)},X)
(this identity follows from (
8.1
)), where
\operatorname{Mor}(S,S^{\prime})
denotes the set of morphisms from a scheme
S
to a scheme
S^{\prime}
.
∎

9.
Reductions

The following proposition is the main result of this section. Its proof is an application of “Harpaz–Wittenberg base-change” (Theorem
4.4
).

Proposition 9.1
.

Assume Theorem
3.1
holds when, letting
n:=\max\{r:\mu_{r}\subset E\}
, the morphism
f:X\rightarrow Q
satisfies the following additional properties:

(R_{1})

the extension
E/K
is Galois;

(R_{2})

the horizontal Brauer group
\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)
has exponent dividing
n
;

(R_{3})

the complex
\Br X_{\eta}\xrightarrow{r}\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)\xrightarrow{\bar{\partial}}H^{3}(\bar{Q},\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\Gamma_{K}}
is exact;

(R_{4})

the morphism
f
admits a multi-section
s:Q^{\overline{K},n,(1)}\rightarrow X
for which there exists a subgroup
B\subset(\Br X)_{+}
surjecting onto
\Br X_{\eta}/f^{*}\Br\eta
with
s^{*}B=0
.

Then Theorem
3.1
holds.

9.1.
Reduction from
(R_{1})
-
(R_{3})

We prove here:

Proposition 9.2
.

Assume Theorem
3.1
holds for morphisms
f
that satisfy, in addition, properties
(R_{1})-(R_{3})
. Then Theorem
3.1
holds.

Before coming to the proof, we introduce, for a general smooth proper morphism
f:X\rightarrow Q
over a quasi-trivial
K
-torus
Q
, the following:

\displaystyle\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)^{\prime}:=\Ker(\bar{\partial}:\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)\rightarrow H^{3}(\bar{Q},\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\Gamma_{K}}),

\displaystyle H^{3}(Q,\mathbb{G}_{m})_{alg}:=\Ker(H^{3}(Q,\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow H^{3}(\bar{Q},\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\Gamma_{K}}),

\displaystyle\partial_{alg}:=\iota\circ\left(\partial|_{\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)^{\prime}}\right):\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\partial}H^{3}(Q,\mathbb{G}_{m})_{alg}\xhookrightarrow{\iota}H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{Q}),

where
\iota:H^{3}(Q,\mathbb{G}_{m})_{alg}\hookrightarrow H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{Q})
denotes the inclusion induced by the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence
H^{i}(K,H^{j}(\bar{Q},\mathbb{G}_{m}))\Rightarrow H^{i+j}(Q,\mathbb{G}_{m})
in view of the vanishings
\Pic\bar{Q}=H^{3}(K,\overline{K}[Q]^{*})=0
(see the proof of Theorem
7.6
).

Lemma 9.3
.

The sequence

\Br X_{\eta}\xrightarrow{r}\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\varphi\circ\partial_{alg}}H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{\eta}),

where
\varphi
denotes the natural map
H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{Q})\rightarrow H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{\eta})
, is exact.

Proof.

The exact sequence
\Br X\xrightarrow{r}\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)\xrightarrow{\partial}H^{3}(Q,\mathbb{G}_{m})
(see (
B.2
)) induces an exact sequence

(9.1)

\Br X\xrightarrow{r}\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\partial_{alg}}H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{Q}),

since
\Ker(\partial|_{\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)^{\prime}})=\Ker(\partial_{alg})
.

We may give analogous definitions for
X_{\eta}/\eta
as the ones we gave right before the lemma for
X/Q
. More precisely, let

\displaystyle\Br_{\text{hor}}(X_{\eta}/\eta)^{\prime}:=\Ker(\bar{\partial}:\Br_{\text{hor}}(X_{\eta}/\eta)\rightarrow H^{3}(\bar{\eta},\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\Gamma_{K}}),

\displaystyle H^{3}(\eta,\mathbb{G}_{m})_{alg}:=\Ker(H^{3}(\eta,\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow H^{3}(\bar{\eta},\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\Gamma_{K}}),

\displaystyle\partial_{alg}:=\partial\circ i:\Br_{\text{hor}}(X_{\eta}/\eta)^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\partial}H^{3}(\eta,\mathbb{G}_{m})_{alg}\xhookrightarrow{i}H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{\eta}),

where
i:H^{3}(\eta,\mathbb{G}_{m})_{alg}\hookrightarrow H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{\eta})
denotes the inclusion induced by the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence, using the vanishings
\Pic\bar{\eta}=H^{3}(K,\overline{K}(\eta)^{*})=0
. With these definitions, we have an analogous sequence to (
9.1
) for
X_{\eta}/\eta
. Confronting this new sequence with (
9.1
), we get a natural commutative diagram with exact rows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{Q})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br X_{\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br_{\text{hor}}(X_{\eta}/\eta)^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{\eta})},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{alg}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{alg}}

where we added the diagonal map
r
. The second vertical map is injective as it is a restriction of the natural isomorphism
\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)\xrightarrow{\cong}\Br_{\text{hor}}(X_{\eta}/\eta)
. A diagram chase now concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 9.4
.

Assume
Q
is Galois. Then
\partial_{alg}(\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)^{\prime})
lies in the kernel of the restriction map
H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{Q})\rightarrow H^{1}(E,\Br\bar{Q})
if and only if the complex
\Br X_{\eta}\xrightarrow{r}\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)\xrightarrow{\bar{\partial}}H^{3}(\bar{Q},\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\Gamma_{K}}
is exact.

Proof.

A diagram chase on (
7.10
) reveals that

\Ker(H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{Q})\xrightarrow{\varphi}H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{\eta}))=\Ker(H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{Q})\xrightarrow{res}H^{1}(E,\Br\bar{Q})).

Thus
\partial_{alg}(\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)^{\prime})
lies in the kernel of
H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{Q})\xrightarrow{res}H^{1}(E,\Br\bar{Q})
if and only if
\varphi\circ\partial_{alg}=0
, which, by Lemma
9.3
, happens if and only if
r(\Br X_{\eta})=\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)^{\prime}
.
∎

For the next lemma, consider a field extension
E^{\prime}/E
with
E^{\prime}/K
Galois. For every
c\in E^{*}
, we have a corresponding cartesian diagram arising from Harpaz–Wittenberg base-change:

(9.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Lemma 9.5
.

For each
c\in E^{*}
, the pullback
\varphi_{c}^{*}:\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)^{\prime}\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(X^{\prime}/Q^{\prime})^{\prime}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Consider the commutative diagram

(9.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{3}(Q\otimes_{k}\overline{k},\mathbb{G}_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br_{\text{hor}}(X^{\prime}/Q^{\prime})^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br_{\text{hor}}(X^{\prime}/Q^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{3}(Q^{\prime}\otimes_{k}\overline{k},\mathbb{G}_{m})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{c}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{c}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi_{c}\otimes_{k}\overline{k})^{*}}
