The isomorphism in the middle follows from Lemma
6.5
(see also the sentence after the lemma), since the fibers of
\varphi_{c}
are geometrically integral, as they are forms of norm
1
tori. Moreover, the pullback

(9.4)

(\varphi_{c}\otimes_{k}\overline{k})^{*}:H^{3}(Q\otimes_{k}\overline{k},\mathbb{G}_{m})\rightarrow H^{3}(Q^{\prime}\otimes_{k}\overline{k},\mathbb{G}_{m})

is injective, because
\varphi_{c}\otimes_{k}\overline{k}:Q^{\prime}\otimes_{k}\overline{k}\rightarrow Q\otimes_{k}\overline{k},x\mapsto c\cdot N_{E^{\prime}\otimes_{k}\overline{k}/E\otimes_{k}\overline{k}}(x)
is, after composing with the translation on
Q\otimes_{k}\overline{k}
by
c^{-1}
, a group-epimorphism of
\overline{k}
-tori with connected kernel (the kernel is a norm
1
torus), and all group-epimorphisms of
\overline{k}
-tori with connected kernel have a section
2
2

2

By the duality between
\overline{k}
-tori and free finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}
-modules, this is the dual of the fact that a saturated injection of free finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}
-modules admits a retraction.
. Such section provides a retraction of (
9.4
).
A diagram chase on (
9.3
) now proves the lemma.
∎

Proof of Proposition
9.2
.

This follows by applying Harpaz–Wittenberg base-change (Theorem
4.4
) with any finite extension of fields
E^{\prime}/E
with
E^{\prime}
Galois over
K
, containing the roots of unity of order the exponent of
\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)
, and splitting
\partial_{alg}(\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)^{\prime})\subset H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{Q})
(in the sense that
\partial_{alg}(\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)^{\prime})
maps to zero under the restriction
H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{Q})\rightarrow H^{1}(E^{\prime},\Br\bar{Q})
). Clearly
(R_{1})
and
(R_{2})
hold for
X^{\prime}\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
.

Property
(R_{3})
holds as well for
X^{\prime}\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
as Lemma
9.5
and the naturality of
\partial_{alg}
imply that
\partial_{alg}(\Br_{\text{hor}}(X^{\prime}/Q^{\prime})^{\prime})
is the image under
\varphi_{c}^{*}:H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{Q})\rightarrow H^{1}(K,\Br\bar{Q}^{\prime})
of
\partial_{alg}(\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q))
. This image restricts to zero in
H^{1}(E^{\prime},\Br\bar{Q}^{\prime})
by construction, and thus Lemma
9.4
gives
(R_{3})
.
∎

9.2.
Reduction to
(R_{1})-(R_{3})

We prove here:

Proposition 9.6
.

Assume Theorem
3.1
holds under
(R_{1})-(R_{4})
. Then Theorem
3.1
holds under
(R_{1})-(R_{3})
.

For the next lemma, consider a field extension
E^{\prime}/E
with
E^{\prime}/K
Galois. As before we have, for each
c\in E^{*}
, a corresponding Harpaz–Wittenberg base-change diagram (
9.2
). We let
\Gamma^{\prime}:=\Gal(E^{\prime}/K)
, and

\displaystyle Q^{\prime}=:\Spec(\overline{K}[(z^{\sigma^{\prime}})^{\pm 1}]_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}})^{\Gamma_{K}},

\displaystyle(Q^{\prime})^{\text{aff}}:=\Spec(\overline{K}[(z^{\sigma^{\prime}})]_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}})^{\Gamma_{K}}\cong\mathbb{A}^{d^{\prime}}_{K},\,d^{\prime}:=[E^{\prime}:K],

\displaystyle D:=\left\{\prod_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}}z^{\sigma^{\prime}}=0\right\}\subset(Q^{\prime})^{\text{aff}}.

Note that
D
is an integral divisor, but not necessarily geometrically integral. The function field of
D
coincides with the function field
E^{\prime}(z^{\sigma^{\prime}})_{\sigma^{\prime}\in(\Gamma^{\prime})^{*}}
of the divisor
\{z=0\}
of
(Q^{\prime})^{\text{aff}}\otimes_{K}E^{\prime}
. We shall use the induced identification
K(D)=E^{\prime}(z^{\sigma^{\prime}})_{\sigma^{\prime}\in(\Gamma^{\prime})^{*}}
below.

Lemma 9.7
.

For each
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**},\,a\in(\mathbb{Z}/n_{\tau}\mathbb{Z})(-1)
, there exists a Brauer element
b_{\tau,a}\in(\Br\eta^{\prime})
with

\displaystyle\partial_{\varphi_{c}^{-1}(Z_{\tau})}(b_{\tau,a})

\displaystyle=a\cdot\hat{y}\in H^{1}(k(\varphi_{c}^{-1}(Z_{\tau})),\mathbb{Z}/n_{\tau}\mathbb{Z}),

\displaystyle\partial_{Z_{\tau^{\prime}}}(b_{\tau,a})

\displaystyle=a\cdot\hat{z}\in H^{1}(k(Z_{\tau^{\prime}}),\mathbb{Z}/n_{\tau}\mathbb{Z}),\text{ for all }\tau^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}[2]^{**},

\displaystyle\partial_{D}(b_{\tau,a})

\displaystyle=a\cdot\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime},\,\sigma^{\prime}\mapsto\sigma}\widehat{z^{\sigma^{\prime}}}\in H^{1}(K(D),\mathbb{Z}/n_{\tau}\mathbb{Z}),

and no other residues on
(Q^{\prime})^{\text{aff}}
. The element
b_{\tau,a}
is unique up to constant elements (i.e. elements in
\im\Br K
).

Proof.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}_{\tau}:=\{g\in\Gamma^{\prime}:g|_{E}\in\{1,\tau\}\}
.
The residues in the statement are realized geometrically (i.e. after base-changing to
\overline{K}
) by the following element of
\Br\bar{\eta}^{\prime}
:

(9.5)

\displaystyle\bar{b}_{\tau,a}:=\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{\tau}\setminus\Gamma^{\prime}}(a\cdot\bar{b}_{\tau})^{\sigma^{\prime}},\text{ where}

\displaystyle\bar{b}_{\tau}:=\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1},g_{2}:g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}\in\Gamma^{\prime}[2]\\
g_{1}\mapsto\text{id},\,g_{2}\mapsto\tau\end{subarray}}(z^{g_{2}}\cup(z^{g_{1}}-z^{g_{2}}))_{n_{\tau}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1},g_{2}:g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{+}\\
g_{1}\mapsto\text{id},\,g_{2}\mapsto\tau\end{subarray}}(z^{g_{2}}\cup z^{g_{1}})_{n_{\tau}}-(y^{\tau}\cup(y-y^{\tau}))_{n_{\tau}}\right),

where the first sum is well-defined because
\bar{b}_{\tau}^{\sigma^{\prime}}=\bar{b}_{\tau}
for all
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{\tau}
. Although this last identity is clear when
\sigma^{\prime}\mapsto\text{id}
, it is not immediate when
\sigma^{\prime}\mapsto\tau
. However, we have

\bar{b}_{\tau}^{\tau}=\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1},g_{2}:g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}\in\Gamma^{\prime}[2]\\
g_{1}\mapsto\text{id},\,g_{2}\mapsto\tau\end{subarray}}(z^{g_{1}}\cup(z^{g_{2}}-z^{g_{1}}))_{n_{\tau}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1},g_{2}:g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{-}\\
g_{1}\mapsto\text{id},\,g_{2}\mapsto\tau\end{subarray}}(z^{g_{2}}\cup z^{g_{1}})_{n_{\tau}}-(y\cup(y^{\tau}-y))_{n_{\tau}}\right),

and, using the identities

\displaystyle(z^{g_{2}}\cup(z^{g_{1}}-z^{g_{2}}))_{n_{\tau}}-(z^{g_{1}}\cup(z^{g_{2}}-z^{g_{1}}))_{n_{\tau}}

\displaystyle=(z^{g_{2}}\cup z^{g_{1}})_{n_{\tau}},

\displaystyle(y^{\tau}\cup(y-y^{\tau}))_{n_{\tau}}-(y\cup(y^{\tau}-y))_{n_{\tau}}

\displaystyle=(y^{\tau}\cup y)_{n_{\tau}},

we see that

\displaystyle\bar{b}_{\tau}-\bar{b}_{\tau}^{\tau}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1},g_{2}:g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}\in\Gamma^{\prime}[2]\\
g_{1}\mapsto\text{id},\,g_{2}\mapsto\tau\end{subarray}}(z^{g_{2}}\cup z^{g_{1}})_{n_{\tau}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1},g_{2}:g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{+}\sqcup\Gamma^{\prime}_{-}\\
g_{1}\mapsto\text{id},\,g_{2}\mapsto\tau\end{subarray}}(z^{g_{2}}\cup z^{g_{1}})_{n_{\tau}}-(y^{\tau}\cup y)_{n_{\tau}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1},g_{2}:g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}\in\Gamma^{\prime}\\
g_{1}\mapsto\text{id},\,g_{2}\mapsto\tau\end{subarray}}(z^{g_{2}}\cup z^{g_{1}})_{n_{\tau}}-\left(\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{2}\in\Gamma^{\prime},\\
g_{2}\mapsto\tau\end{subarray}}z^{g}\right)\cup\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1}\in\Gamma^{\prime},\\
g_{1}\mapsto\text{id}\end{subarray}}z^{g}\right)\right)_{n_{\tau}}\right)=0,

proving that
\bar{b}^{\prime}_{\tau}
is
\Gamma^{\prime}_{\tau}
-invariant as wished. Thus, by construction,
\bar{b}_{\tau,a}
is
\Gamma^{\prime}
-invariant and, a fortiori,
\Gamma_{K}
-invariant. So, by Lemma
9.8
below, there exists
b_{\tau,a}^{\prime}\in\Br\eta^{\prime}
mapping to
\bar{b}_{\tau,a}
under
\Br\eta^{\prime}\rightarrow\Br\bar{\eta}^{\prime}
.

The residues of
b_{\tau,a}^{\prime}
on
(Q^{\prime})^{\text{aff}}
thus coincide with those of the statement after base-change to
\overline{K}
. Therefore, the difference in

\operatorname{Res}(\mathbb{A}^{d^{\prime}}_{K}):=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}D\subset\mathbb{A}^{d^{\prime}}_{K}\\
\text{irrreducible divisor}\end{subarray}}H^{1}(K(D),\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})

of the total residue
\partial_{\text{tot}}(b_{\tau,a}^{\prime})
of
b_{\tau,a}^{\prime}
and the element
r\in\operatorname{Res}(\mathbb{A}^{d^{\prime}}_{K})
corresponding to the collection in the statement is algebraic, i.e. this difference lies in
\operatorname{Res}_{alg}(\mathbb{A}^{d^{\prime}}_{K}):=\bigoplus_{D}H^{1}(\overline{K}(D)/K(D),\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})
. All elements of
\operatorname{Res}_{alg}(\mathbb{A}^{d^{\prime}}_{K})
are total residues of elements of the algebraic Brauer group of
K(t_{1},\ldots,t_{d})
. Indeed, each element of
\operatorname{Res}_{alg}(\mathbb{A}^{d^{\prime}}_{K})
corresponds to a collection of cyclic characters
\chi_{i}:\Gal(\overline{K}(D_{i})/K(D_{i}))\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
on finitely many divisors
D_{1},\ldots,D_{r}
, and then it is the total residue of
\sum_{i}\cores_{K_{i}/K}(\chi_{i},f_{i})
, where
K_{i}:=\overline{K}\cap K(D_{i})
and
f_{i}\in K_{i}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}]
is a uniformizer for a geometrically irreducible component of
D_{i}\otimes_{K}K_{i}
.

From the discussion above, we have
r-\partial_{\text{tot}}(b_{\tau,a}^{\prime})=\partial_{\text{tot}}(b)
for some
b\in\Br\eta^{\prime}
. Letting
b_{\tau,a}:=b_{\tau,a}^{\prime}+b
we get the desired Brauer element.
∎

Lemma 9.8
.

The map
\Br K(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})\rightarrow(\Br\overline{K}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}))^{\Gamma_{K}}
is surjective for all
r
.

Proof.

By the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence
H^{i}(K,H^{j}(\overline{K}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}),\mathbb{G}_{m}))\linebreak\Rightarrow H^{i+j}(K(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}),\mathbb{G}_{m})
and the vanishing
H^{1}(\overline{K}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}),\mathbb{G}_{m})=0
by Hilbert 90, the cokernel of the map injects in
H^{3}(K,\overline{K}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})^{*})
. However, this last group is
0
because
K
is a number field (for the case
r=1
see e.g.
[
19
, p. 241]
, whose argument straightforwardly generalizes to all
r
).
∎

Lemma 9.9
.

If, for some natural number
m
,
f
admits a multi-section
s:Q^{\overline{K},m,(1)}\rightarrow X
, then, for all
c\in E^{*}
,
f^{\prime}
admits a multi-section
s^{\prime}:(Q^{\prime})^{\overline{K},m,(1)}\rightarrow X^{\prime}
. Moreover, for each
b\in(\Br X)_{+}
such that
s^{*}b=0
, we have, for each
c\in E^{*}
,
\psi_{c}^{*}(b)=b^{\prime}+(f^{\prime})^{*}b_{0}
for some
b^{\prime}\in(\Br X^{\prime})_{+}
such that
(s^{\prime})^{*}(b^{\prime})=0
and
b_{0}\in\Br\eta^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Fix
c\in E^{*}
. We may lift the morphism
\varphi_{c}:Q^{\prime}\rightarrow Q
to a morphism
\tilde{\varphi}_{c}:(Q^{\prime})^{\overline{K},m,(1)}\rightarrow Q^{\overline{K},m,(1)}
. Indeed, writing
Q^{\prime}=\Spec(\overline{K}[(z^{\sigma^{\prime}})^{\pm 1}]_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}})^{\Gamma_{K}},\,\Gamma^{\prime}:=\Gal(E^{\prime}/K)
, the morphism
\varphi_{c}
is
\Spec
-dual of the inclusion
(\overline{K}[(y_{\sigma})^{\pm 1}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma})^{\Gamma_{K}}\subset(\overline{K}[(z^{\sigma^{\prime}})^{\pm 1}]_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}})^{\Gamma_{K}}
defined by setting
y_{\sigma}=c^{\sigma}\cdot\prod_{\sigma^{\prime}\mapsto\sigma}z_{\tau}
, and then
\tilde{\varphi}_{c}
may be obtained as the
\Spec
-dual of the inclusion
\overline{K}[y^{\pm 1},(y_{\sigma})^{\pm\frac{1}{m}}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}}\subset\overline{K}[z^{\pm 1},(z^{\sigma^{\prime}})^{\pm\frac{1}{m}}]_{\sigma^{\prime}\in(\Gamma^{\prime})^{*}}
.

Then a multi-section
s^{\prime}
is given by the composition

s^{\prime}:=(s\circ\tilde{\varphi}_{c},p^{\prime}):(Q^{\prime})^{\overline{K},m,(1)}\rightarrow X\times_{Q}Q^{\prime}=X^{\prime},

where
p^{\prime}
denotes the natural morphism
(Q^{\prime})^{\overline{K},m,(1)}\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
.

Let us prove the last sentence. For
b\in(\Br X)_{+}
, and each
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
, let
a_{\tau}\cdot\widehat{y}\in H^{1}(k(Z^{\prime}_{\tau}),\mathbb{Z}/n_{\tau}\mathbb{Z}),\,a_{\tau}\in(\mathbb{Z}/n_{\tau}\mathbb{Z})(-1)
be its residue at the divisor
Z^{\prime}_{\tau}=f^{-1}(Z_{\tau})
. Taking
b_{0}:=\sum_{\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}}b_{\tau,a_{\tau}}
(where each
b_{\tau,a_{\tau}}
is defined as in Lemma
9.7
), the residues at the divisors
(\psi_{c})^{-1}(Z^{\prime}_{\tau}),\,\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
of the difference
b^{\prime}:=\psi_{c}^{*}(b)-(f^{\prime})^{*}b_{0}
cancel out. Thus the only residues
b^{\prime}
has over
X
are at the divisors
Z^{\prime}_{\tau^{\prime}},\,\tau^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}[2]^{**}
. Each residue at these divisors is a multiple of the Kummer character
\hat{z}
of order
m_{\tau^{\prime}}
(see the residues of
b_{0}
in Lemma
9.7
), and so
b^{\prime}\in(\Br X^{\prime})_{+}
as wished.

It remains to verify that
(s^{\prime})^{*}(b^{\prime})=0
. We have
(s^{\prime})^{*}(\psi_{c})^{*}(b^{\prime})=(\psi_{c}\circ s^{\prime})^{*}(b)=0
as
\psi_{c}\circ s^{\prime}
factors through
s
. It thus suffices to prove that the pullback of
b_{0}
along
p^{\prime}:(Q^{\prime})^{\overline{K},m,(1)}\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
is trivial. The morphism
p^{\prime}
factors as
(Q^{\prime})^{\overline{K},m,(1)}\rightarrow\bar{Q}^{\prime}\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
. As a scolium of the proof of Lemma
9.7
, the restriction
\bar{b}_{0}
of
b_{0}
to
\bar{Q}^{\prime}
is a weighted sum of the elements
\bar{b}_{\tau}\in\Br\bar{\eta}^{\prime}
presented in (
9.5
), and each of these pullbacks trivially to
Q^{\overline{K},m,(1)}
because
m
is a multiple of
n_{\tau}
and each of the first factors of the cup-products appearing in the expression defining each
b_{\tau}
becomes an
m
-th power after pullback to
Q^{\overline{K},m,(1)}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
9.6
.

Assume
f:X\rightarrow Q
satisfies
(R_{1})-(R_{3})
, and let
B\subset(\Br X)_{+}
be a set of representatives for
\Br X_{\eta}/f^{*}\Br\eta
. By Corollary
8.3
, there exists a natural number
m
and a multi-section
s:Q^{\overline{K},m,(1)}\rightarrow X
such that
s^{*}B=0
. Then letting
E^{\prime}/K
be a Galois extension that contains the
m
-th roots of unity, Lemma
9.9
guarantees that, for every
c\in E^{*}
, the associated Harpaz–Wittenberg base-change
X^{\prime}\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
still has a multi-section
s^{\prime}:Q^{\overline{K},m,(1)}\rightarrow X^{\prime}
, and that
\psi_{c}^{*}(B)\subset B^{\prime}+f^{*}\Br\eta^{\prime}
for some
B^{\prime}\subset\Br X^{\prime}
such that
(s^{\prime})^{*}(B^{\prime})=0
. Thus
\varphi_{c}^{*}(r(B))=r(B^{\prime})
, where
\varphi_{c}^{*}
denotes the map
\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)\rightarrow\Br_{\text{hor}}(X^{\prime}/Q^{\prime})
. Since
r(B)=\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)^{\prime}
and
\varphi_{c}^{*}(\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)^{\prime})=\Br_{\text{hor}}(X^{\prime}/Q^{\prime})^{\prime}
by Lemma
9.5
, then we infer that
B^{\prime}
surjects via
r
onto
\Br_{\text{hor}}(X^{\prime}/Q^{\prime})^{\prime}
, and so in particular, it surjects onto the quotient
\Br X_{\eta^{\prime}}/(f^{\prime})^{*}\Br\eta^{\prime}\subset\Br_{\text{hor}}(X^{\prime}/Q^{\prime})^{\prime}
, where the inclusion is induced by
r
, and we also infer that
r(\Br X_{\eta^{\prime}})=\Br_{\text{hor}}(X^{\prime}/Q^{\prime})^{\prime}
, i.e. that
(R_{3})
holds for
f^{\prime}
. Thus, after the Harpaz–Wittenberg base-change, all properties
(R_{1})-(R_{4})
hold, concluding the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
9.1
.

Combine Propositions
9.2
and
9.6
.
∎

10.
Cyclic strong approximation

The main result of this section is the approximation result Theorem
10.7
. Although independent, the result is motivated mostly by its application in the proof of Theorem
3.1
. Indeed, in Section
11
, we shall use Theorem
10.7
to create rational fibers with suitable adelic points in the setting of Theorem
3.1
.

Observe also that Theorem
10.7
creates grids (see definition below) of approximating elements and not just a single one. Again, this is motivated by our application of Theorem
10.7
in the proof of Theorem
3.1
, as these are the grids that enable the “triple variation argument”.

10.1.
Arithmetic ingredients

Fix in this subsection a finite Galois extension
L/E
of number fields.

Definition
.

An idele
(x_{w})_{w}\in\mathbb{I}_{E}
and a conjugacy class
c\subset\Gal(L/E)
are
compatible
if the image of
(x_{w})_{w}
under the reciprocity map

rec_{L\cap E^{ab}/E}:\mathbb{I}_{E}\rightarrow C_{E}\rightarrow\Gal(L\cap E^{ab}/E)

associated to the maximal abelian subextension
L\cap E^{ab}/E
of
L/E
coincides with the projection of
c
to
\Gal(L\cap E^{ab}/E)
.

The following general result is proven in Appendix
A
.

Theorem 10.1
.

Let
S
be a finite set of places of
E
containing the archimedean ones. Let
(x_{w})_{w}\in\mathbb{I}_{E}
be an idele with
x_{w}=1
for all
w\notin S
, and
c\subset\Gal(L/E)
be a compatible conjugacy class.

Then there exist infinitely many prime elements
p\in\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
whose Frobenius class in
\Gal(L/E)
is
c
, that approximate
x_{w}
arbitrarily well for all finite
w\in S
, and whose direction in the Minkowski space
E\otimes\mathbb{R}
approximates the direction of
(x_{w})_{w\in M_{E}^{\infty}}\in E\otimes\mathbb{R}
arbitrarily well.

The following lemma will occasionally prove useful in verifying the hypothesis of Theorem
10.1
.

Lemma 10.2
.

An idele
(x_{w})_{w\in M_{E}}\in\mathbb{I}_{E}
lies in the kernel of the reciprocity map

rec_{L\cap E^{ab}/E}:\mathbb{I}_{E}\rightarrow C_{E}\rightarrow\Gal(L\cap E^{ab}/E)

if and only if

\sum_{w}\inv_{w}(\varphi_{w},x_{w})=0\ \text{for all }\varphi\in\Hom(\Gal(L/E),\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}),

where
\varphi_{w}
denotes the restriction
\varphi|_{\Gamma_{w}}
.

Proof.

Each
\varphi
factors through
\Gal(L\cap E^{ab}/E)
and induces by Class Field Theory an idelic character
\tilde{\varphi}:\mathbb{I}_{E}\xrightarrow{rec_{L\cap E^{ab}/E}}\Gal(L\cap E^{ab}/E)\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
. We have

\sum_{w}\inv_{w}(\varphi_{w},x_{w})=\tilde{\varphi}((x_{w})_{w})

Indeed, the compatibility of local and global Class Field Theories gives that
\tilde{\varphi}((x_{w})_{w})=\sum_{w}\varphi_{w}(x_{w})
, and
[
21
, Corollary 9.6(a)]
gives that
\varphi_{w}(x_{w})=\inv_{w}(\varphi_{w},x_{w})
. Since the
\varphi
’s generate the dual abelian group of
\Gal(L\cap E^{ab}/E)
(as they in fact form the whole group), the lemma follows.
∎

10.1.1.
A special case of Hecke-Chebotarev density

In this subsubsection, let
n
be a natural number such that
\mu_{n}\subset E
, and
S
be a finite set of places of
E
.

Consider
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})
-linearly independent characters

\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{r}:\Gamma_{E}\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1),

and Heisenberg representations

\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{s}:\Gamma_{E}\rightarrow\operatorname{Heiss}_{n},\quad\rho_{j}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\varphi_{j}&\psi_{j}\\
0&1&\varphi^{\prime}_{j}\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix},

where, for each
j=1,\ldots,s
, the characters
\varphi_{j},\varphi^{\prime}_{j}
lie in
V:=\langle\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{r}\rangle
(i.e. they are linear combinations of the
\chi_{i}
’s).

The characters and representations above fit into one big Galois representation as follows:

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
\chi_{i},\rho_{j}
be as above. There exists a group structure
H
on the set
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)^{r}\times(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)^{s}
sitting in a central exact sequence

1\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)^{s}\rightarrow H\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)^{r}\rightarrow 1

such that

\rho:\Gamma_{E}\rightarrow H,\quad\rho:=(\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{r},\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{s}).

is a homomorphism. Moreover, if the wedge-products
\varphi_{j}\wedge\varphi^{\prime}_{j},\,j=1,\ldots,s
are
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})
-linearly independent in
\wedge^{2}V
, then
\rho
is surjective, the group structure is unique, and
[H,H]=(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)^{s}
.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{\chi}:=(\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{r})
and
\boldsymbol{\psi}:=(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{s})
. For each
j
, let
\mathbf{v}_{j},\mathbf{w}_{j}\in(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})^{r}
be such that
\varphi_{j}=\mathbf{v}_{j}\cdot\boldsymbol{\chi},\varphi^{\prime}_{j}=\mathbf{w}_{j}\cdot\boldsymbol{\chi}
.
The cocycle conditions
\psi_{j}(\sigma\sigma^{\prime})=\psi_{j}(\sigma)+\psi_{j}(\sigma^{\prime})+\varphi_{j}(\sigma)\varphi^{\prime}_{j}(\sigma^{\prime})
give

\displaystyle\rho(\sigma\sigma^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\chi}(\sigma)+\boldsymbol{\chi}(\sigma^{\prime}),\boldsymbol{\psi}(\sigma)+\boldsymbol{\psi}(\sigma^{\prime})+\boldsymbol{\xi}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})).

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=(\varphi_{1}(\sigma)\varphi^{\prime}_{1}(\sigma^{\prime}),\ldots,\varphi_{s}(\sigma)\varphi^{\prime}_{s}(\sigma^{\prime})).

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}(\boldsymbol{\chi}(\sigma))\beta_{1}(\boldsymbol{\chi}(\sigma^{\prime})),\ldots,\alpha_{s}(\boldsymbol{\chi}(\sigma))\beta_{s}(\boldsymbol{\chi}(\sigma^{\prime}))),

where
\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}\in\Hom((\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)^{r},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1))
denote the homomorphisms
\mathbf{a}\mapsto\mathbf{v}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{a}
and
\mathbf{a}\mapsto\mathbf{w}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{a}
, respectively.
So
\rho
becomes a homomorphism if we give
H
the group structure defined by

\displaystyle\left(\lambda_{1},\mu_{1}\right)\cdot\left(\lambda_{2},\mu_{2}\right)=\left(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2},\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+c(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\right),\ \text{with}

\displaystyle c(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}):=(\alpha_{1}(\lambda_{1})\beta_{1}(\lambda_{2}),\ldots,\alpha_{s}(\lambda_{1})\beta_{s}(\lambda_{2})),

which fits in a central sequence as in the statement.
3
3

3

One also sees from the multiplication law that the associated cohomology class
[H]\in
H^{2}((\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)^{r},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)^{s})
is represented by the
2
-cocycle
c=(\alpha_{1}\cup\beta_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s}\cup\beta_{s})

[
4
, Ch. IV, Sec. 3]
, but we do not need this here.

To prove the final statement, we first prove that
[H,H]=(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)^{s}
for the group structure on
H
defined above. Assume by contradiction that
[H,H]
is a proper subgroup of
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)^{s}
, and let
(c_{1},\ldots,c_{s})\in(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})^{s}\setminus\{(0,\ldots,0)\}
be such that the linear form
\sum_{j}c_{j}x_{j}
is identically
0
on
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})\in[H,H]
. The commutator of two elements
\left(\lambda_{1},\mu_{1}\right),\left(\lambda_{2},\mu_{2}\right)\in H
is the vector
([\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}](\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\linebreak\ldots,[\alpha_{s},\beta_{s}](\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}))
of
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)^{s}
, where
[\alpha,\beta](\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}):=\alpha(\lambda_{1})\beta(\lambda_{2})-\alpha(\lambda_{2})\beta(\lambda_{1})
. Thus, we would have that

(10.1)

\sum_{j}c_{j}\cdot[\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}](\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

is identically zero for
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)^{r}
.

Let
A:=(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)^{r},A^{D}:=\Hom(A,(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1))
. Note that
A^{D}
is just isomorphic to
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})^{r}
(with no Tate twists), and so is canonically isomorphic to
V
. An explicit isomorphism is the map
V\xrightarrow{\simeq}A^{D}
sending
\chi_{1}^{c_{1}}\cdots\chi_{r}^{c_{r}}
to
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r})\mapsto\sum_{i}a_{i}c_{i}
. By definition of
\alpha_{j}
and
\beta_{j}
, this isomorphism sends
\varphi_{j}
to
\alpha_{j}
and
\varphi^{\prime}_{j}
to
\beta_{j}
.
Denote now by
\operatorname{Alt}_{2}(A)
the module of alternating maps
A\times A\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)
, and consider the isomorphisms

(1)

\wedge^{2}V\xrightarrow{\simeq}\wedge^{2}A^{D},
induced by the map
V\xrightarrow{\simeq}A^{D}
defined above,

(2)

\wedge^{2}A^{D}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname{Alt}_{2}(A),\quad\alpha\wedge\beta\mapsto[\alpha,\beta]
.

To see that the second an invertible, note that an inverse is given by
\mu\mapsto\sum_{i<j}\mu_{i,j}(e_{i}^{*}\wedge e_{j}^{*}),\ \mu_{i,j}:=\mu(e_{i}\wedge e_{j})
, where
e_{1},\ldots,e_{r}
is a coordinate basis of
A
(i.e. each
e_{i}
is an element of the
i
-th coordinate copy of
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)
in
A=(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)^{r}
of order
n
).

The composition of (1) and (2) sends, for each
j
,
\varphi_{j}\wedge\varphi^{\prime}_{j}
to
[\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}]
. In particular, (
10.1
) would pull back to a non-trivial relation among
\varphi_{j}\wedge\varphi^{\prime}_{j},\,j=1,\ldots,s
, contradicting our assumption. So
[H,H]=(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)^{s}
as wished.

Let us now prove that
\rho
is surjective. Because the
\chi_{i}
’s are linearly independent,
\boldsymbol{\chi}=(\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{r}):\Gamma_{E}\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)^{r}
is surjective. But
\boldsymbol{\chi}=pr\circ\rho
, where
pr
denotes the projection
H\rightarrow H/[H,H]=(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)^{r}
. Hence
pr(\im\rho)=H/[H,H]
, and so
[H,H]\cdot\im\rho=H
. The group
H
is a product of
p
-groups, so its Frattini subgroup contains its derived subgroup, and we infer that
\im\rho=H
, i.e.
\rho
is surjective. Finally, the uniqueness of the group structure on
H
is a direct consequence of this surjectivity.
∎

We have the following corollary to Theorem
10.1
.

Corollary 10.4
.

Let
\chi_{i},\rho_{j}
be as above. Assume further that, for each
i
, there exists a prime
\mathcal{P}_{i}
of
\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
at which
\chi_{i}
is totally ramified,
\chi_{i^{\prime}}
is unramified for all
i^{\prime}\neq i
, and at which
L
is unramified. Assume further that the wedge-products
\varphi_{j}\wedge\varphi^{\prime}_{j},\,j=1,\ldots,s
are
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})
-linearly independent in
\wedge^{2}V
.

Then for all
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r},b_{1},\ldots,b_{s})\in(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)^{r}\times(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)^{s}
, there exist infinitely many prime elements
p\in\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
that are arbitrarily close to
1
for all
v\in S
, have Minkowski direction arbitrarily close to that of
1
, and:

•

are split in
L
and are split over
\mathbb{Q}
;

•

are such that for some absolute Frobenius
\Frob_{p}\in\Gamma_{E}
of
p
:

\chi_{i}(\Frob_{p})=a_{i}
for all
i
and
\psi_{j}(\Frob_{p})=b_{j}
for all
j
.

Proof of Corollary
10.4
.

Let

\rho_{L}:\Gamma_{L}\rightarrow H,\ \rho_{L}=(\chi_{1}|_{L},\ldots,\chi_{r}|_{L},\psi_{1}|_{L},\ldots,\psi_{s}|_{L})

be the restriction of
\rho
to
\Gamma_{L}
. The assumption on the ramification gives in particular that the restricted characters
\chi_{1}|_{L},\ldots,\chi_{r}|_{L}
are still
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})
-linearly independent. Hence we may apply Lemma
10.3
not only to
\rho
but also to
\rho_{L}
, and infer that
\rho_{L}
is a surjective homomorphism.

Let now
c\subset H
be the conjugacy class of
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r},b_{1},\ldots,b_{s})
. We wish to apply Theorem
10.1
(for the field
L
rather than
E
) to this class, and in order to do so, we need to construct a suitable compatible idele in
L
. The abelianization of
H
is
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)^{r}
, and the projection of
c
to it is
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r})
.
Through Artin’s reciprocity map, each character
\chi_{i}|_{L}:\Gamma_{L}\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)
defines an idelic character
\tilde{\chi}_{i}:\mathbb{I}_{L}\rightarrow C_{L}\xrightarrow{\text{rec}}\Gamma_{L}^{\text{ab}}\xrightarrow{\chi_{i}|_{L}}(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)
. Recall that by Class Field Theory,
\chi_{i}(I_{w})=\tilde{\chi}_{i}(\mathcal{O}_{w}^{*})
, where
I_{w}
denotes the absolute inertia group of a place
w
. Denoting by
w_{i}
the place of
L
corresponding to the prime
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
, and letting
\tilde{x}_{i}\in\mathcal{O}_{w_{i}}^{*}
be such that
\chi_{i}(x_{i})=a_{i}
and
\tilde{x}_{w}:=1
for
w\notin\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{r}\}
, the idele
(\tilde{x}_{w})
is compatible with
c
.

Now, by Theorem
10.1
, we have infinitely many prime elements
q
of
L
that split over
\mathbb{Q}
, that approximate the idele
(\tilde{x}_{w})
at finite places in
\tilde{S}:=S\cup\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{r}\}
, approximate the direction of this idele in the Minkowski space, and whose Frobenius conjugacy class in
\Gal(\bar{L}^{\Ker\rho}/L)=H
is
c
. So, for a prime
\bar{q}
of
\bar{L}
dividing
q
, we have
\rho(\Frob_{\bar{q}})=h\cdot(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r},b_{1},\ldots,b_{s})\cdot h^{-1}
for some
h\in H
. Replacing, if necessary,
\bar{q}
by
\gamma\cdot\bar{q}
for
\gamma\in\Gamma_{L}
with
\rho(\gamma)=h
, we have
\rho(\Frob_{\bar{q}})=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r},b_{1},\ldots,b_{s})
.

Since the primes
q\cdot\mathcal{O}_{L,S}
have degree
1
over
\mathbb{Q}
and a fortiori also over
E
, the norms
p=N_{L/E}(q)
are prime elements of
\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
with the same absolute Frobenii in
\Gal(\overline{K}/E)
as the primes
q
above them, and are then the sought prime elements of
E
.
∎

10.2.
Grids

Definition 10.5
.

For a number field
E
and a finite set of places
T
, a
k
-dimensional (
k\geq 0
)
grid
of size
M
in
\mathcal{O}_{E,T}
is an injective function
\mathcal{M}:\{1,\ldots,M\}^{k}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{E,T}\setminus\{0\}
of the shape

\mathcal{M}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})=x_{0}\ \cdot\ x_{1,i_{1}}\ \cdot\ x_{2,i_{2}}\ \cdot\cdots\cdot\ x_{k,i_{k}},

where
x_{0}\in\mathcal{O}_{E,T}\setminus\{0\}
, and
\{x_{1,1},\ldots,x_{1,M}\},\ldots,\{x_{k,1},\ldots,x_{k,M}\}\subset\mathcal{O}_{E,T}\setminus\{0\}
are ordered subsets.

We shall denote a grid as in Definition
10.5
as follows:

\mathcal{M}=x_{0}\cdot\{x_{1,1},\ldots,x_{1,M}\}\cdot\{x_{2,1},\ldots,x_{2,M}\}\cdots\{x_{k,1},\ldots,x_{k,M}\}\subset\mathcal{O}_{E,T}\setminus\{0\}.

With a slight abuse of notation, we write
x\in\mathcal{M}
to mean that
x
lies in the image of a grid
\mathcal{M}
.

Definition
.

(1). A grid
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathcal{O}_{E,T}\setminus\{0\}
is
square-free
if the norm-ideal
N_{E/\mathbb{Q}}(x\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,T})
is square-free for all
x\in\mathcal{M}
.

(2). A square-free grid
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathcal{O}_{E,S}\setminus\{0\}
is
prime-variational
if all the
x_{i,j}
(
i\geq 1
) are prime elements of
\mathcal{O}_{E,T}
whose underlying primes in
\mathbb{Q}
are pairwise distinct and are all coprime to
N_{E/\mathbb{Q}}(x_{0})
.

10.3.
The groups
B_{L}
and
B^{\prime}_{L}

Let
K
be a number field.

Notation
.

For a Galois field extension
E/K
with group
\Gamma
and a
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{*}
,
let
n_{\tau}\coloneqq\max\{r\mid n:\mu_{r}\subset E^{\tau}\}
and
n^{-}_{\tau}\coloneqq\max\{r\mid n:\zeta_{r}^{\tau}=\zeta_{r}^{-1}\}
, where
n
denotes the maximum order of roots of unity in
E
and
\zeta_{r}
denotes a primitive
r
-th root of unity.

Notation
.

For a Galois quasi-trivial
K
-torus
Q:=R_{E/K}\mathbb{G}_{m}=(\Spec E[(y^{\sigma})^{\pm 1}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma})^{\Gamma}
, and a Galois field extension
L/E
, we let
B_{L}(Q)\subset\Br K(Q)
be the subgroup generated by the Brauer classes

(1)

\cores_{E/K}\left(\varphi,y\right)
, for all
\varphi\in\Hom(\Gal(L/E),\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})
,

(2)

\cores_{E/K}\left(y,y^{\sigma}\right)_{n}
, for all
\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}
,

(3)

\cores_{E/K}\left(y,y^{\tau}-y\right)_{n^{-}_{\tau}}
, for all
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{*}
.

We let
B^{\prime}_{L}(Q)\subset\Br K(Q)
be the subgroup generated by
B_{L}
and the Brauer classes

(4)

\cores_{E/K}\left(y,{y^{\tau}-y}\right)_{n_{\tau}}
, for all
\sigma\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
.

We shall often just write
B_{L},B^{\prime}_{L}
for
B_{L}(Q)
and
B^{\prime}_{L}(Q)
.

Lemma 10.6
.

The group
B_{L}
(resp.
B^{\prime}_{L}
) is contained in
\Br Q
(resp.
\Br Q^{\sharp}
).

Proof.

It is clear that elements of type (1) and (2) lie in
\Br Q
and that those of type (4) lie in
\Br Q^{\sharp}
. To see that elements of type (3) also lie in
\Br Q
, we refer to
[
9
, Lemma 3.3]
(the reference just computes the residues at the divisors
Z_{\tau}
and shows that they vanish).
∎

10.4.
Main result

Fix for the rest of Section
10
a number field
K
, an algebraic closure
\overline{K}
, and a tower of finite extensions
L/E/K
, all contained in
\overline{K}
, with both
L/E
and
E/K
Galois. Let
\Gamma=\Gal(E/K)
, and
n
be the maximal order of roots of unity in
E
.

Let
S
be a finite set of places of
K
containing those dividing
n,

\infty
or
\Delta_{L/K}
, and
T
be the places of
E
above
S
. Let
Q\coloneqq R_{E/K}\mathbb{G}_{m}
, and
\mathcal{Q}\coloneqq R_{\mathcal{O}_{E,T}/\mathcal{O}_{K,S}}\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

Theorem 10.7
.

For every
(q_{v})_{v\in S}\in Q^{\sharp}(K_{S})^{B^{\prime}_{L}}
and all natural numbers
k\geq 3,M\geq 1
with
(k,n)=1
, there exists a
k
-dimensional prime-variational grid

\mathcal{M}=p_{0}\cdot\{p_{1,1},\ldots,p_{1,M}\}\cdots\{p_{k,1},\ldots,p_{k,M}\}\subset\mathcal{O}_{E,T}\setminus\{0\}\subset E^{*}=Q(K),

of size
M
, where
p_{0},p_{1,1},\ldots,p_{1,M},\ldots,p_{k,1},\ldots,p_{k,M}
are prime elements of
\mathcal{O}_{E,T}
generating pairwise distinct prime ideals, such that:

•

(the images of) all
q\in\mathcal{M}
approximate arbitrarily well the image of
(q_{v})_{v\in S}
in the quotient
Q(K_{S})/\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,

•

for all
q\in\mathcal{M}
and
v\notin S
we have either
q\in\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
or
q\in\im(Q^{L,n,(1)}(K_{v})\rightarrow Q(K_{v}))
,

•

the half-spin symbols
\operatorname{HS}_{\tau,T,n_{\tau}}(q)
are defined and trivial for all
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
and
q\in\mathcal{M}
.

(The quotient in the first point is meant through the diagonal action of
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
on the archimedean factors
\prod_{v\mid\infty}Q(K_{v})=\prod_{w\mid\infty}E_{w}^{*}
as in Section
2
.)

Definition.
Given a grid
\mathcal{M}=x_{0}\cdot\{x_{1,1},\ldots,x_{1,M}\}\cdots\{x_{k,1},\ldots,x_{k,M}\}
in
\mathcal{O}_{E,T}\setminus\{0\}
, we call the function

(10.2)

\begin{matrix}f:\{1,\ldots,M-1\}^{3}&\rightarrow&\Fun_{\Gamma}(\Gamma^{3}\setminus\Delta_{3},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2)),\\
(a,b,c)&\mapsto&\left(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\mapsto[(x_{1,a+1}{x_{1,a}}^{-1})^{\sigma_{1}},(x_{2,b+1}{x_{2,b}}^{-1})^{\sigma_{2}},(x_{3,c+1}{x_{3,c}}^{-1})^{\sigma_{3}}]_{E,n}\right),\end{matrix}

when defined (i.e. when all Redéi symbols appearing are defined), the
triple Redéi-variation of order
n
of
\mathcal{M}
.
4
4

4

It might be more appropriate to
call this the “triple Redéi-variation
at the indices

1,2,3
”, and define in general the “triple Redéi-variation at indices
z,w,t
” as (
10.2
) but after replacing the Redéi symbol with
[(x_{z,a+1}{x_{z,a}}^{-1})^{\sigma_{1}},(x_{w,b+1}{x_{w,b}}^{-1})^{\sigma_{2}},(x_{t,c+1}{x_{t,c}}^{-1})^{\sigma_{3}}]_{E,n}
. However, the variation at the first three indices is sufficient for the purposes of this paper, and so we focus on that.
Here
\Delta_{3}\subset\Gamma^{3}
denotes the multi-diagonal.

The following enhancement of the theorem above will be used in later sections.

Theorem 10.8
.

For every function
g:\{1,\ldots,M-1\}^{3}\rightarrow\Fun_{\Gamma}(\Gamma^{3}\setminus\Delta,(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2))
, the grid
\mathcal{M}
in Theorem
10.7
may be found so that, in addition,

•

its triple Redéi-variation of order
n
is defined and equal to
g
;

•

for all
v\notin S,\,q,q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
, we have
q^{\prime}q^{-1}\in\im(Q^{L,n}(K_{v})\rightarrow Q(K_{v}))
.

Remark
.

(1). Obviously Theorem
10.8
is stronger than Theorem
10.7
. We separated the statements because Theorem
10.7
might have some interest on its own, as it provides a generalization of the aforementioned result of Shafarevich, while enhancing Theorem
10.7
to Theorem
10.8
is a purely technical complication that is solely done for the later purposes of this paper.

(2). The Redéi-variation condition in Theorem
10.8
is essential to the later arguments in this paper (namely, in achieving control of the triple variation), while the second condition is a very simple addition that simplifies the computations in Section
12
(see in particular Proposition
12.15
).

10.5.
An arithmetic version

Theorem
10.7
follows from the following theorem of purely arithmetic nature.

Theorem 10.9
.

For every
M\geq 1
, every
k\geq 3
coprime with
n
, every function
g:\{1,\ldots,M-1\}^{3}\rightarrow\Fun_{\Gamma}(\Gamma^{3}\setminus\Delta,(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2))
, and every
(x_{w})_{w\in T}\in\prod_{w\in T}E_{w}^{*}
such that

(H1)

\sum_{w\in T}\inv_{w}(\varphi_{w},x_{w})=0
for all characters
\varphi\in\Hom(\Gal(L/E),\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})
, where
\varphi_{w}
denotes the localization of
\varphi
at
w
,

(H2)

\prod_{w\in T}(x_{w},(x_{\sigma^{-1}(w)})^{\sigma})_{w,n}=1
for all
\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}
,

(H3)

\prod_{w\in T}(x_{w},(x_{\tau^{-1}(w)})^{\tau}-x_{w})_{w,n^{-}_{\tau}}=1
for all
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{*}
,

(H4)

\prod_{w\in T}(x_{w},(x_{\tau^{-1}(w)})^{\tau}-x_{w})_{w,n_{\tau}}=1
for all
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
,

there exists a
k
-dimensional grid of size
M

\mathcal{M}=p_{0}\cdot\{p_{1,1},\ldots,p_{1,M}\}\cdot\ \cdots\ \cdot\{p_{k,1},\ldots,p_{k,M}\},

where
p_{0},p_{1,1},\ldots,p_{1,M},\ldots,p_{k,1},\ldots,p_{k,M}
are prime elements of
\mathcal{O}_{E,T}
generating pairwise distinct prime ideals, and
such that for every
x\in\mathcal{M}

(1)

x
approximates arbitrarily well
x_{w}
for all finite
w\in T
,

(2)

the Minkowski direction of
x
approximates arbitrarily well the direction of the vector
(x_{w})_{w\in M_{E}^{\infty}}\in\prod_{w\in M_{E}^{\infty}}E_{w}=E\otimes\mathbb{R}
,

(3)

every prime ideal
\mathcal{P}
dividing the ideal
x\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,T}\subset\mathcal{O}_{E,T}
splits completely in
L
and is split over
\mathbb{Q}
;

(4)

x
reduces to a non-zero
n
-th power modulo
\mathcal{P}^{\sigma}
for all prime ideals
\mathcal{P}
dividing
x\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,T}
and all
\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}\coloneqq\Gamma\setminus\{\text{id}\}
;

(5)

\operatorname{HS}_{\tau,T,n_{\tau}}(x)=1
for every
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
,

and, moreover,

(6)

the triple Redéi-variation of
\mathcal{M}
of order
n
is defined and equal to
g
.

(7)

for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},\,j,j^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
, we have that
p_{i,j}p_{i,j^{\prime}}^{-1}
is an
n
-th power modulo the prime ideals generated by
p_{0}
and all
p_{i^{\prime},\alpha}
with
i^{\prime}\neq i
and
\alpha\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
.

The proof of Theorem
10.9
(to be found in Subsection
10.6
) is based on a combination of Chebotarev’s density theorem and Hecke equidistribution (through Theorem
10.1
and its Corollary
10.4
) to deal with conditions (1), (2), (3), and (6), and a carefully placed pidgeonhole principle to deal with conditions (4) and (5), following an old technique of Shafarevich
[
40
]
(see
[
36
, Theorem 9.3.2]
for an analog result in English).

Remark
.

Dropping the assumption (H4), then the same proof gives, with minimal adjustements, all points except (5). Analogously,
weaking the assumption “
(q_{v})_{v\in S}\in Q^{\sharp}(K_{S})^{B^{\prime}_{L}}
” in Theorem
10.7
to “
(q_{v})_{v\in S}\in Q(K_{S})^{B_{L}}
”, then this theorem holds with the exception of the third point concerning half-spin symbols.

Notation
.

Given a finite field extension
k^{\prime}/k
, a
k
-scheme
X
whose structural morphism factors as
X\rightarrow\Spec k^{\prime}\rightarrow\Spec k
, and a
k
-embedding
\iota:k^{\prime}\hookrightarrow F
, we let
X(F)_{\iota}\subset X(F)
be the set of
F
-points that correspond to morphisms
\Spec F\rightarrow X
of
k^{\prime}
-schemes (and not just of
k
-schemes) when we view
\Spec F
as a
k^{\prime}
-scheme via
\iota
.

Observe that
X(F)=\bigsqcup_{\iota:k^{\prime}\hookrightarrow F}X(F)_{\iota}
.

Lemma 10.10
.

For a field extension
F/K
, a
K
-embedding
\iota:L\hookrightarrow F
, and a point
q\in Q(K)
,
q
lies in
\im(Q^{L,n,(1)}(F)_{\iota}\rightarrow Q(F))
(resp. in
\im(Q^{L,n}(F)_{\iota}\rightarrow Q(F))
) if and only if
\iota(q^{\sigma})
is an
n
-th power for all
\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}
(resp. all
\sigma\in\Gamma
).

Proof.

The map
Q^{L,n,(1)}(F)_{\iota}\rightarrow Q(F)
factors as
Q^{L,n,(1)}(F)_{\iota}\rightarrow(Q\otimes_{K}L)(F)_{\iota}\rightarrow Q(F)
.
The last map is bijective. In fact, this is more general: for any
k
-scheme
X
, finite field extension
k^{\prime}/k
, and
F,\iota
as above, the natural map
(X\otimes_{k}k^{\prime})(F)_{\iota}\rightarrow X(F)
is a bijection by the universal property of fibered products.

The point
q\in Q(K)
corresponds to the homomorphism of
K
-algebras obtained by taking
\Gamma
-invariants of the morphism of
E
-algebras
E[(y^{\sigma})^{\pm 1}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma}\rightarrow E,\,y^{\sigma}\mapsto q^{\sigma}
. Its inverse image in
(Q\otimes_{K}L)(F)_{\iota}
is then the point corresponding to the homomorphism of
L
-algebras
L[(y^{\sigma})^{\pm 1}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma}\rightarrow F,\,y^{\sigma}\mapsto q^{\sigma}
. The point lifts to
Q^{L,n,(1)}(F)_{\iota}
if and only if we may complete the following commutative diagram with the upper row:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L[y^{\pm 1},(y^{\sigma})^{\pm\frac{1}{n}}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L[(y^{\sigma})^{\pm 1}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\sigma}\mapsto q^{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}

which we may do if and only if
q^{\sigma}
is an
n
-th power in
F
for all
\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}
, proving the first statement. The proof of the
Q^{L,n}
-case is analogous.
∎

Proof of Theorem
10.8
assuming Theorem
10.9
.

Let
(\tilde{x}_{w})_{w\in T}\in\prod_{w\in T}E_{w}^{*}
be the image of
(q_{v})_{v\in S}\in\prod_{v\in S}Q(K_{v})
under the identifications
Q(K_{v})=\prod_{w\mid v}E_{w}^{*}
. After a small approximation of
(q_{v})_{v\in S}
, we assume that
\tilde{x}_{w}\neq(\tilde{x}_{\tau^{-1}(w)})^{\tau}
for all
w\in T
and
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{*}
. The collection
(q_{v})_{v\in S}
is orthogonal to
B^{\prime}_{L}
, i.e. is orthogonal to the Brauer classes of type (1)-(4) listed at the beginning of this section. All these Brauer classes are of the shape
\cores_{E/K}b
for some
b\in\Br E(Q)
.

The local Brauer pairing associated to an
E/K
-corestriction is computed as follows
[
7
, Proposition 1.4.7]
:

(\star)
Let
p
be a
K_{v}
-point on a
K
-variety
V
, for some place
v
of
K
. Let
b\in\Br V_{E}
. Then

\inv_{v}((\cores_{E/K}b)(p))=\sum_{w\mid v,\ w\in M_{E}}\inv_{w}(b(p_{w})),

where
p_{w}
denotes the image of
p
under the embedding
V(K_{v})\subset V(E_{w})
.

Using
(\star)
, we see that each hypothesis (H1)-(H4) for
(\tilde{x}_{w})_{w\in T}
is equivalent to the orthogonality of
(q_{v})_{v\in S}\in Q(K_{v})
to the respective algebras of type (1)-(4), and so holds by assumption.

So we may apply Theorem
10.9
to
(\tilde{x}_{w})_{w\in T}
. and get a grid

\mathcal{M}=p_{0}\cdot\{p_{1,1},\ldots,p_{1,M}\}\cdot\ \cdots\ \cdot\{p_{k,1},\ldots,p_{k,M}\}\subset\mathcal{O}_{E,T}\setminus\{0\}.

We claim that
\mathcal{M}
satisfies the sought conditions for Theorem
10.7
.
Following the identification

\displaystyle Q(K_{S})/\mathbb{R}_{>0}=\prod_{w\in T\cap M_{E}^{\text{fin}}}E_{w}^{*}\times(E\otimes\mathbb{R})/\mathbb{R}_{>0},

points (1) and (2) of Theorem
10.9
give that every
x\in\mathcal{M}
approximates arbitrarily well
(q_{v})_{v\in S}
in the quotient
Q(K_{S})/\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Moreover, the last point of Theorem
10.7
holds by (5).

We now show that
q
lies in
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\cup\im(Q^{L,n,(1)}(K_{v})\rightarrow Q(K_{v}))
for all
q\in\mathcal{M}
and
v\notin S
. When
w(q)=0
for all places
w
of
E
dividing
v
, we clearly have
q\in\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
. Otherwise we have
w(q)>0
for some place
w\mid v
. Let
\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{O}_{E,T}
be the associated prime ideal, which by (3) is split over
K
(a fortiori, since it is split even over
\mathbb{Q}
) and splits in
L
. These splittings induces
K
-embeddings
E\subset L\subset K_{v}
. The images of
q^{\sigma},\,\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}
in
K_{v}
under these embedding are
n
-th power by (4), and so we get liftability to
Q^{L,n,(1)}(K_{v})
by Lemma
10.10
.

The Redéi condition of Theorem
10.8
is already given, so it only remains to show that
q^{\prime}q^{-1}\in\im(Q^{L,n}(K_{v})\rightarrow Q(K_{v}))
for all
v\notin S,\,q,q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
.
We may assume that
\mathcal{M}\not\subset\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
and so we either have
v(N_{E/K}(q_{0}))>0
or there exists some
q_{i,j}
with
v(N_{E/K}(q_{i,j}))>0
. Let
\mathcal{P}
(resp.
w
) be the prime (resp. place) of
E
associated to
q_{0}
in the first case, and to
q_{i,j}
in the second case.
For all
\sigma\neq\text{id}
, both
(q^{\prime})^{\sigma}
and
q^{\sigma}
are
n
-th power modulo all conjugates
\mathcal{P}
with
\sigma\neq\text{id}
by (3), and so the same holds for their quotient
(q^{\prime}q^{-1})^{\sigma}
. Moreover,
q^{\prime}q^{-1}
is an
n
-th power also modulo
\mathcal{P}
by (7). Putting these together, we have that the image of
(q^{\prime}q^{-1})^{\sigma}
in
E_{w}\cong K_{v}
is an
n
-th power for all
\sigma\in\Gamma
, and we conclude by Lemma
10.10
.
∎

10.6.
Proof of Theorem
10.9

It is more convenient to carry out the proof in additive notation, working with
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)
instead of
\mu_{n}
. As in Section
2
,
\left[\frac{\star}{\star}\right]
stands for the additive variant of the Legendre symbol
\left(\frac{\star}{\star}\right)
. Analogously, we denote by
\operatorname{hs}(x)
the additive variant of the half-spin symbol
\operatorname{HS}(x)
(possibly with subscripts) under the identification
\mu_{n}=(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)
.

Recall that
\Gamma^{*}=\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
, that
\Gamma[2]^{*}
denotes the set of elements of order
2
in
\Gamma
, and that
\Gamma[2]^{**}=\{\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{*}:\chi(\tau)\equiv\pm 1\bmod\,8\}
if
8\mid n
, while it is equal to
\Gamma[2]^{*}
if
v_{2}(n)\in\{1,2\}
and is empty if
n
is odd. Here
\chi
denotes the cyclotomic character modulo
n
.

We shall also use the following terminology in the proof.

Notation
.

For a function
f:\Gamma^{*}\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)
, we let
f^{\dagger}:\Gamma^{*}\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)
be defined by
f^{\dagger}(\sigma):=\chi(\sigma)f(\sigma^{-1})
.

Definition
.

(1) A function
f:\Gamma^{*}\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)
is
even
if
f^{\dagger}=f
and
(1+\chi(\tau))|f(\tau)
for all
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{*}
.

(2) An
augmented function

\mathbf{f}
is a pair
\mathbf{f}=(f,h)
of a function
f:\Gamma^{*}\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)
and a collection of elements
h(\tau)\in(\mathbb{Z}/n_{\tau}\mathbb{Z})(1)
, indexed by
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
.

(3) An
augmented function

\mathbf{f}=(f,h)
is
even
if
f
is even and
2h(\tau)\equiv f(\tau)\bmod\,{n_{\tau}}
for all
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
.

Lemma 10.11
.

Let
p\in\mathcal{O}_{E,T}
be a prime element of degree
1
over
K
such that

(PH1)

\prod_{w\in T}(p,p^{\sigma})_{w,n}=1
for all
\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}
,

(PH2)

\prod_{w\in T}(p,p^{\tau}-p)_{w,n^{-}_{\tau}}=1
for all
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{*}
,

(PH3)

\prod_{w\in T}(p,p^{\tau}-p)_{w,n_{\tau}}=1
for all
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
.

Then the augmented function
(f_{p},h_{p})
defined by

f_{p}(\sigma):=\left[\frac{p^{\sigma}}{p}\right]_{n},\quad h_{p}(\tau)\coloneqq\operatorname{hs}_{\tau,n_{\tau}}(p)\in(\mathbb{Z}/n_{\tau}\mathbb{Z})(1),

is even.

Proof.

For
\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}
, (PH1) gives
\left[\frac{p^{\sigma}}{p}\right]_{n}=\left[\frac{p}{p^{\sigma}}\right]_{n}
by power-symbol reciprocity. Since
\left[\frac{p}{p^{\sigma}}\right]_{n}=\left[\frac{p^{\sigma^{-1}}}{p}\right]_{n}^{\sigma}
, this gives
f_{p}^{\dagger}=f_{p}
.

For
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{*}
, (PH2) gives
\left[\frac{p^{\sigma}}{p}\right]_{n^{-}_{\tau}}=0
by spin reciprocity (Lemma
5.1
). Since
\left[\frac{p^{\tau}}{p}\right]_{n^{-}_{\tau}}
is the reduction of
\left[\frac{p^{\tau}}{p}\right]_{n}
under the reduction modulo
n^{-}_{\tau}
map
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n^{-}_{\tau}\mathbb{Z})(1)
, this shows that
f_{p}(\tau)
is a multiple of
n^{-}_{\tau}
. As
n^{-}_{\tau}=\gcd(n,1+\chi(\tau))
, this proves that
f_{p}
is even.

Finally, for
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
, (PH3) gives
2\operatorname{hs}_{\tau,n_{\tau}}(p)=\left[\frac{p^{\tau}}{p}\right]_{n_{\tau}}
by Proposition
5.3
(1). As above,
\left[\frac{p^{\tau}}{p}\right]_{n_{\tau}}
is the reduction of
\left[\frac{p^{\tau}}{p}\right]_{n}=f_{p}(\tau)
modulo
n_{\tau}
, and we conclude.
∎

Proof of Theorem
10.9
.

By Lemma
10.2
, (H1) tells that the idele
(x_{w})_{w\in M_{E}}
extending
(x_{w})_{w\in T}
by setting
x_{w}:=1
if
w\notin T
, lies in the kernel of the Artin reciprocity map

\text{rec}_{L\cap E^{ab}/E}:\mathbb{I}_{E}\rightarrow C_{E}\rightarrow\Gal(L\cap E^{ab}/E).

So this idele is compatible with the trivial conjugacy class of
\Gal(L/E)
, and Theorem
10.1
(Hecke-Chebotarev density) gives a prime element
p_{0}\in\mathcal{O}_{E,T}
with degree
1
over
\mathbb{Q}
, that splits completely in
L
, approximates
x_{w}
arbitrarily well for finite
w\in T
, and whose Minkowski direction approximates the direction of
(x_{w})_{w\in M_{E}^{\infty}}\in\prod_{w\in M_{E}^{\infty}}E_{w}=E\otimes\mathbb{R}
arbitrarily well.

Let
E_{T}^{*}:=\prod_{w\in T}E_{w}^{*}
, and let
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
act by diagonal multiplication on the archimedean factors.
Since the natural image of
p_{0}
in the quotient
E_{T}^{*}/\mathbb{R}_{>0}
approximates the image of
(x_{w})_{w\in T}
, and hypothesis (H2)-(H4) are invariant under the
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-action, these hypotheses must also hold for the diagonal image of
p_{0}
in
E_{T}
, and so translate into (PH1)-(PH3) for
p_{0}
. Thus
(f,h):=(f_{p_{0}},h_{p_{0}})
is an even augmented function by Lemma
10.11
.

Choose now arbitrarily small neighbourhoods
U_{w}\subset E_{w}^{*}
of
1
for all finite
w\in T
, and a neighbourhood
U_{\infty}
of the trivial direction
1\cdot\mathbb{R}_{>0}
in
(E\otimes\mathbb{R})/\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. We define

\mathcal{S}:=\left\{p\in\mathcal{O}_{E,T}\text{ prime}:\,\begin{gathered}p\in U_{w}\text{ for all finite }w\in T,\ p\cdot\mathbb{R}_{>0}\in U_{\infty},\\
p\text{ has degree $1$ over }\mathbb{Q}\text{ and splits completely in }L,\\
\left[\frac{p_{0}^{\sigma}}{p}\right]_{n}=-\frac{1}{k}\cdot f(\sigma)\text{ for all }\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}.\end{gathered}\right\}.

Note that
\mathcal{S}
is infinite by Corollary
10.4
. More precisely, since
\left[\frac{\alpha}{p}\right]_{n}=\chi_{\alpha}(\Frob_{p})
for any
\alpha\in E^{*}/E^{*n}
whose associated character
\chi_{\alpha}
is unramified at
p
, the infinitude of
\mathcal{S}
follows from the corollary applied to the characters
\chi_{p_{0}^{\sigma}},\,\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}
(and no Heisenberg representations).
We shall create
\mathcal{M}
as a subset of
\{p_{0}\}\times\mathcal{S}^{k}
.

To an element
x\in\mathcal{O}_{E,T}
that factors as

x=p_{0}p_{1}\cdots p_{k},\ \ \text{ with }p_{i}\in\mathcal{S}\text{ for }i\geq 1,

and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}
generating pairwise distinct prime ideals, we associate a
(k\times k)
-matrix

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}(X_{1,1},h_{1})&\cdots&X_{1,k}\\
\hline\cr\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
\hline\cr X_{k,1}&\cdots&(X_{k,k},h_{k})\\
\end{array}\right),

whose off-diagonal (resp. diagonal) entries are functions (resp. augmented functions) from
\Gamma^{*}
to
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)
,
defined by

\displaystyle X_{i,j}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left[\frac{{p}_{i}^{\sigma}}{{p}_{j}}\right]_{n}=\left[\frac{{p}_{j}}{{p}_{i}^{\sigma}}\right]_{n}\in(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1),\ \sigma\in\Gamma^{*},\ i,j=1,\ldots,k,

\displaystyle h_{i}(\tau)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\operatorname{hs}_{\tau}(p_{i}),\ \tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**},\ i=1,\ldots,k.

The identity
\left[\frac{{p}_{i}^{\sigma}}{{p}_{j}}\right]_{n}=\left[\frac{{p}_{j}}{{p}_{i}^{\sigma}}\right]_{n}
written above comes from power-symbol reciprocity, since both
p_{i}
and
p_{j}
are close to
1
at all places in
T
.

The following hold true for any
x
as above:

(10.3)

\displaystyle X_{i,j}=X_{j,i}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\text{for all }i,j;

(10.4)

\displaystyle(X_{i,i},h_{i})\text{ is an even augmented function }

\displaystyle\text{for all }i;

The first is the simple observation that
\left[\frac{{p}_{j}}{{p}_{i}^{\sigma}}\right]_{n}=\left[\frac{({p}_{j})^{\sigma^{-1}}}{{p}_{i}}\right]_{n}^{\sigma}
. The second is just a special case of Lemma
10.11
.

For
x
as above, we can compute
\left[\frac{x}{p_{i}^{\sigma}}\right]_{n}
in terms of
f
and
\mathbf{X}
:

\displaystyle\left[\frac{x}{p_{0}^{\sigma}}\right]_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\left[\frac{p_{i}}{p_{0}^{\sigma}}\right]_{n}=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\left[\frac{p_{0}^{\sigma}}{p_{i}}\right]_{n}=\left[\frac{p_{0}^{\sigma}}{p_{0}}\right]_{n}-k\cdot\frac{1}{k}\cdot\left[\frac{p_{0}^{\sigma}}{p_{0}}\right]_{n}=0,

\displaystyle\left[\frac{x}{p_{i}^{\sigma}}\right]_{n}

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{p_{0}}{p_{i}^{\sigma}}\right]_{n}+\sum_{j}\left[\frac{p_{j}}{p_{i}^{\sigma}}\right]_{n}=\left[\frac{(p_{0})^{\sigma^{-1}}}{p_{i}}\right]_{n}^{\sigma}+\sum_{j}\left[\frac{p_{i}^{\sigma}}{p_{j}}\right]_{n}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{k}\cdot\left[\frac{(p_{0})^{\sigma^{-1}}}{p_{0}}\right]_{n}^{\sigma}+\sum_{j}X_{i,j}(\sigma)=-\frac{1}{k}f^{\dagger}(\sigma)+\sum_{j}X_{i,j}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{k}f(\sigma)+\sum_{j}X_{i,j}(\sigma),

for all
\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}
and
i=1,\ldots,k
. In the identities above we repeatedly used power-symbol reciprocity to infer that
\left[\frac{p_{j}}{p_{i}^{\sigma}}\right]_{n}=\left[\frac{p_{i}^{\sigma}}{p_{j}}\right]_{n}
for all
i,j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
(the obstruction
\prod_{w\in T}(p_{i}^{\sigma},p_{j})_{w,n}
to this identity vanishes for all pairs
i,j
, as when either
i
or
j
is
>0
then at least one among
p_{i}
or
p_{j}
is close to
1
at all places in
T
, while for
i=j=0
this is (PH1) for
p_{0}
).
We can also compute the half-spin symbols of
x
in terms of
(f,h)
and
\mathbf{X}
for all
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{hs}_{\tau}(x)

\displaystyle\equiv\operatorname{hs}_{\tau}(p_{0})+\sum_{i}\left[\frac{p_{i}}{p_{0}^{\tau}}\right]_{n}+\operatorname{hs}_{\tau}(p_{1}\cdots p_{k})

\displaystyle\bmod\,n_{\tau}

\displaystyle\equiv h(\tau)-f(\tau)+\sum_{i}\operatorname{hs}_{\tau}(p_{i})+\sum_{i<j}X_{i,j}(\tau)

\displaystyle\bmod\,n_{\tau}

\displaystyle\equiv-h(\tau)+\sum_{i}h_{i}(\tau)+\sum_{i<j}X_{i,j}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\bmod\,n_{\tau},

where the first congruence is obtained applying recursively point (ii) of Proposition
5.3
to the products
p_{0}(p_{1}\cdots p_{k}),p_{1}(p_{2}\cdots p_{k}),\ldots,p_{k-1}p_{k}
, and the third holds because
(f,h)
is an even augmented function.

In the following,
\Fun(\Gamma^{*},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1))
denotes the group of functions from
\Gamma^{*}
to
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)
.

Lemma 10.12
.

Let
(f,h),(\delta,r),f,\delta:\Gamma^{*}\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)
be even augmented functions. There exist functions
X_{i,j}\in\Fun(\Gamma^{*},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)),1\leq i<j\leq k
, such that letting

\mathbf{X}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}(X_{1,1},h_{1})&\cdots&X_{1,k}\\
\hline\cr\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
\hline\cr X_{k,1}&\cdots&(X_{k,k},h_{k})\\
\end{array}\right)

be the matrix defined by
X_{j,i}:=X_{i,j}^{\dagger}
for
i>j
, and
(X_{i,i},h_{i}):=(\delta,r)
for all
i
, the following hold:

(1)

k\cdot\sum_{j}X_{i,j}=f
for all
i
;

(2)

\sum_{i}h_{i}(\tau)+\sum_{i<j}X_{i,j}(\tau)\equiv h(\tau)\bmod n_{\tau}
for all
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
.

By the computations before the lemma, (1) and (2) give that for an
x=p_{0}p_{1}\cdots p_{k}
with associated matrix
\mathbf{X}
, all symbols
\left[\frac{x}{p_{i}^{\sigma}}\right]_{n}
and
\operatorname{hs}_{\tau}(x)
become trivial.

Proof of Lemma
10.12
.

For a matrix
M=(m_{i,j})_{1\leq i,j\leq k},\,m_{i,j}\in\Fun(\Gamma^{*},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1))
, we denote by
M^{\dagger}
the “adjoint” matrix
M^{\dagger}=(m_{j,i}^{{\dagger}})
. We say that
M
is
even
if
m_{j,i}=m_{i,j}^{\dagger}
for all
i,j
and
m_{i,i}
is even for all
i
.

We rewrite (2) as

(2
′
)

k\cdot r(\tau)+\sum_{i<j}X_{i,j}(\tau)\equiv{{h}}(\tau)\bmod n_{\tau}
for all
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
.

We are then looking for an even matrix
(X_{i,j})_{1\leq i,j\leq k}
with
X_{i,i}=\delta
for all
i
and that satisfies (1) and (2
′
). We restrict our search among matrices
(X_{i,j})
of the shape

(10.5)

\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc|ccc}\delta&0&\cdots&0&0&0&x_{1}\\
0&\delta&\cdots&0&0&0&x_{2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&\delta&0&0&x_{k-3}\\
\hline\cr 0&0&\cdots&0&\delta&a&b\\
0&0&\cdots&0&a^{\dagger}&\delta&c\\
x_{1}^{\dagger}&x_{2}^{\dagger}&\cdots&x_{k-3}^{\dagger}&b^{\dagger}&c^{\dagger}&\delta\end{array}\right),

where
a,b,c,x_{1},\ldots,x_{k-3}
indicate unknowns in
\Fun(\Gamma^{*},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1))
, for whose values we solve below. For matrices of this shape, (1) and (2
′
) reduce to the system:

\begin{cases}\delta+x_{1}=\frac{1}{k}\cdot f\\
\cdots\\
\delta+x_{k-3}=\frac{1}{k}\cdot f\\
\delta+a+b=\frac{1}{k}\cdot f\\
a^{\dagger}+\delta+c=\frac{1}{k}\cdot f\\
x_{1}^{\dagger}+\cdots+x_{k-3}^{\dagger}+b^{\dagger}+c^{\dagger}+\delta=\frac{1}{k}\cdot f\\
k\cdot r(\tau)+a(\tau)+b(\tau)+c(\tau)+\sum_{i\leq k-3}x_{i}(\tau)\equiv{{h}}(\tau)\bmod n_{\tau},&\text{ for all }\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}.\end{cases}

We use the first
k-2
equations to eliminate the unknowns
b,c
and
x_{i},i\leq k-3
by setting
x_{i}\coloneqq\frac{1}{k}\cdot f-\delta
for
i\leq k-3
,
b\coloneqq\frac{1}{k}\cdot f-\delta-a
, and
c\coloneqq\frac{1}{k}\cdot f-\delta-a^{\dagger}
. Substituting in the last two equations, and using
\delta^{\dagger}=\delta,f^{\dagger}=f
, the system reduces to:

\begin{cases}a+a^{\dagger}=(k-2)\cdot(\frac{1}{k}\cdot f-\delta)\\
a(\tau)\equiv-{{h}}(\tau)+k\cdot r(\tau)+(k-1)(\frac{1}{k}\cdot f(\tau)-\delta(\tau))\bmod n_{\tau},&\text{ for all }\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**},\end{cases}

or equivalently, using that
\delta(\tau)\equiv 2r(\tau)
and
f(\tau)\equiv 2{{h}}(\tau)
modulo
n_{\tau}
for all
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
(both
(f,{{h}})
and
(\delta,r)
are even augmented functions):

\begin{cases}a+a^{\dagger}=(k-2)\cdot(\frac{1}{k}\cdot f-\delta)\\
a(\tau)\equiv(k-2)\cdot(\frac{1}{k}\cdot{{h}}(\tau)-r(\tau))\bmod n_{\tau},&\text{ for all }\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}.\end{cases}

Linear combinations with coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
of augmented functions are also augmented functions, and thus such is the linear combination
(g,\eta):=(k-2)\cdot(\frac{1}{k}\cdot(f,{{h}})-(\delta,r))
. We may rewrite the system above as:

(10.6)

\begin{cases}a+a^{\dagger}=g\\
a(\tau)\equiv\eta(\tau)\bmod n_{\tau},&\text{ for all }\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}.\end{cases}

We observe that for an
a
that satisfies the first equation, the second already automatically holds modulo
n_{\tau}/2
as the first equation gives
(1+\chi(\tau))a(\tau)=g(\tau)
for all
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{*}
, and thus
2a(\tau)\equiv g(\tau)\equiv 2\eta(\tau)\bmod n_{\tau}
for
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{*}
.

We now define
a:\Gamma^{*}\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)
as follows:

a(\sigma):=\begin{cases}g(\sigma)&\text{ if }\sigma\in\Gamma_{+},\\
0&\text{ if }\sigma\in\Gamma_{+}^{-1},\\
\frac{g(\tau)}{1+\chi(\tau)}&\text{ if }\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{*},\end{cases}

where the fraction indicates any element of
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)
that multiplied by
(1+\chi(\tau))
is equal to
g(\tau)
. For each
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{*}
, at least one such value of the fraction exists because
g
is even. Any choice of value for the fractions solves the first equation of (
10.6
). Let us argue that we may further choose these values so that the second holds as well.

As noted above, we already have
a(\tau)\equiv\eta(\tau)\bmod n_{\tau}/2
for all
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
from the first equation. Now observe that
\frac{n_{\tau}}{2}(1+\chi(\tau))=0
in
\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
for all
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
(this product being
0
is equivalent to the definition of “
**
”), and so we may add
n_{\tau}/2
to the value of the fractions
\frac{g(\tau)}{1+\chi(\tau)}
, if necessary, and obtain the sought congruence.

Now the system is solved and (
10.5
) is the sought matrix
X_{i,j}
, concluding the proof.
∎

For each even augmented function
\boldsymbol{\delta}=(\delta,r),\,\delta:\Gamma^{*}\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)
, we fix a matrix
\mathbf{X}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
as given by Lemma
10.12
. We indicate its off-diagonal entries by
X_{i,j}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
.

To construct the sought grid, consider one copy of the following rectangular
(k\times M)
tile for each
\boldsymbol{\delta}
:

\begin{array}[]{|c|ccc|c|}\hline\cr&&\cdots&&\\
\hline\cr&&\cdots&&\\
\hline\cr\vdots&&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\hline\cr&&\cdots&&\\
\hline\cr\end{array}_{\ \boldsymbol{\delta}}

We shall use the indices
i,j,\ldots
to indicate the rows of the tiles, and the indices
a,b,c,\ldots
for the columns. We order the slots
(i,a)
of each tile lexicographically, i.e.
(i,a)<(j,b)
if
i<j
or if
i=j
and
a<b
. So whenever we say “first slot of a tile such that …”, we always refer to the first slot
in this lexicographic order.

We wish to fill at least one of the tiles with prime elements of
\mathcal{S}
whose associated even augmented functions are equal to the index of the tile, and such that, denoting by
A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}
the row vectors of this tile, the grid
p_{0}\cdot A_{1}\cdot\,\cdots\,\cdot A_{k}
satisfies (1)-(6).

To achieve this goal, we recursively fill the tiles lexicographically, i.e. whenever we place a new element we place it in the first free slot of some tile (but which tile we choose will depend on the element), with elements of
\mathcal{S}
, until one is completely filled.

At the first step, pick any
p\in\mathcal{S}
, and then place it in the top left corner of the tile with index
\boldsymbol{\delta}=\boldsymbol{\delta}(p):=(f_{p},h_{p})
.

At the recursive step, each tile is partially filled as in the following figure:

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr*&\cdots&*&*&\cdots&*\\
\hline\cr\vdots&&&&&\vdots\\
\hline\cr*&\cdots&*&*&\cdots&*\\
\hline\cr*&\cdots&*&&\cdots&\\
\hline\cr&&&&&\\
\hline\cr\vdots&&&&&\vdots\\
\hline\cr&\cdots&&&\cdots&\\
\hline\cr\end{array}_{\ \boldsymbol{\delta}},

where the asterisks denote prime elements in
\mathcal{S}
.
Now pick a prime element
p
of
\mathcal{O}_{E,T}
such that (the existence of such a
p
is argued right after the list of properties):

•

p
approximates
1
arbitrarily well for all finite places
w\in T
and its Minkowski direction approximates arbitrarily well that of
1
;

•

we have
\left[\frac{p_{0}^{\sigma}}{p}\right]_{n}=-\frac{1}{k}\cdot\left[\frac{p_{0}^{\sigma}}{p_{0}}\right]_{n}
for all
\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}
;

•

for any
\boldsymbol{\delta}
, letting
j\geq 0
be the integer such that exactly the first
j
rows
A_{1},\ldots,A_{j}
of the
\boldsymbol{\delta}
-tile are already filled, then
\left[\frac{p_{i}^{\sigma}}{p}\right]_{n}=X_{i,j+1}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\sigma)
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,j\},\,p_{i}\in A_{i},\,\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}
;

•

for any
\boldsymbol{\delta}
on whose corresponding tile the first and second rows are already completely filled, and whose first empty slot is of the form
(3,c+1),\,c\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}
(i.e. lies on the third row and is not on the first column), we have

(10.7)

[(p_{1,a+1}p_{1,a}^{-1})^{\sigma_{1}},(p_{2,b+1}p_{2,b}^{-1})^{\sigma_{2}},p\cdot p_{3,c}^{-1}]_{n}=g(a,b,c)(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},id),

for all
a,b\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}^{2},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Gamma^{*},\sigma_{1}\neq\sigma_{2}
, where
p_{i,\star}
denotes the element in the
(i,\star)
-position of the
\boldsymbol{\delta}
-tile;

•

we have
\left[\frac{q}{p}\right]_{n}=0
for all prime elements
q
already appearing on the partially filled tiles.

The existence of such a
p
is an application of Corollary
10.4
. More precisely, note that
\left[\frac{\alpha}{p}\right]_{n}=\chi_{\alpha}(\Frob_{p})
for any element
\alpha\in E^{*}/E^{*n}
coprime with
p
, and that, for
\alpha,\beta\in E^{*}/E^{*n}
and any prime element
p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}_{E,T}
such that
\alpha,\beta,p\cdot(p^{\prime})^{-1}
satisfies condition (
C^{\prime}
)
T
from Section
5
, we have

\displaystyle[\alpha,\beta,p\cdot(p^{\prime})^{-1}]_{n}

\displaystyle=\Frob_{p}(E(\sqrt[n]{\alpha},\sqrt[n]{\beta})_{2}/E)-\Frob_{p^{\prime}}(E(\sqrt[n]{\alpha},\sqrt[n]{\beta})_{2}/E)

\displaystyle=\psi_{\alpha,\beta}(\Frob_{p})-\psi_{\alpha,\beta}(\Frob_{p^{\prime}}),

where
E(\sqrt[n]{\alpha},\sqrt[n]{\beta})_{2}
denotes any Heisenberg extension of
E
as defined in Section
5
(see in particular Lemma
5.8
) and
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\chi_{\alpha}&\psi_{\alpha,\beta}\\
0&1&\chi_{\beta}\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}
denotes its associated Heisenberg representation.
So the conditions on
p
may be imposed by applying Corollary
10.4
to

•

the characters
\chi_{\pi^{\sigma}},
where
\pi
varies among
p_{0}
and the prime elements already appearing on the partially filled tiles and
\sigma
varies in
\Gamma
, and

•

the Heisenberg representations

\rho_{\boldsymbol{\delta},a,b,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}:=\begin{pmatrix}1&\chi_{(p_{1,a+1}p_{1,a}^{-1})^{\sigma_{1}}}&\psi_{\boldsymbol{\delta},a,b,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\\
0&1&\chi_{(p_{2,b+1}p_{2,b}^{-1})^{\sigma_{2}}}\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix},

associated to (a choice of) Heisenberg extensions

E\left(\sqrt[n]{(p_{1,a+1}p_{1,a}^{-1})^{\sigma_{1}}},\sqrt[n]{(p_{2,b+1}p_{2,b}^{-1})^{\sigma_{2}}}\right)_{2}/E

where
\boldsymbol{\delta}
ranges among the augmented even functions for which the first and second row of the
\boldsymbol{\delta}
-tile are already filled,
a
and
b
range among elements of
\{1,\ldots,M-1\}
, and
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
range among pairs in
\Gamma^{*}
with
\sigma_{1}\neq\sigma_{2}
.

We now place
p
on the tile with index
\boldsymbol{\delta}=\boldsymbol{\delta}(p):=(f_{p},h_{p})
. Note that that the first two conditions guarantee that
p
lies in
\mathcal{S}
.

By the pidgeonhole principle, after enough steps, one of the tiles is completely filled. Let
A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}
be its rows, and
\mathcal{M}:=p_{0}\cdot A_{1}\cdots A_{k}
. By construction,

\displaystyle\left[\frac{{p}_{i}^{\sigma}}{{p}_{j}}\right]_{n}=X_{i,j}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\sigma)\text{ for all }1\leq i<j\leq k,\ p_{i}\in A_{i},p_{j}\in A_{j},\text{ and }

\displaystyle(f_{p},h_{p})=(\delta,r)\text{ for all }p\in A:=A_{1}\cup\cdots\cup A_{k}.

By these two conditions, the augmented matrix
\mathbf{X}(x)
associated to every
x=p_{0}p_{1}\cdots p_{k}\in\mathcal{M}
coincides with the augmented matrix
\mathbf{X}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}

on the entries
(i,j)
with
i\leq j
. Thus
\mathbf{X}(x)=\mathbf{X}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
by the skew-symmetry (
10.3
), and (1)-(5) are satisfied as wished. Moreover, condition (
10.7
) ensures that (6) holds as well when
\sigma_{3}=\text{id}
, and the
\Gamma
-equivariance of Redéi symbols gives the case of a general
\sigma_{3}
. Finally, we have

\displaystyle\left[\frac{{p}_{i}}{{p}_{j}}\right]_{n}=0\text{ for all }0\leq i<j\leq k,\ p_{i}\in A_{i},p_{j}\in A_{j},\text{ with }A_{0}:=\{p_{0}\},

by the vanishing condition
\left[\frac{q}{p}\right]_{n}=0
that we imposed for all
q
appearing before
p
. Thus
\left[\frac{{p}_{i}}{{p}_{j}}\right]_{n}=0
also for
0\leq j<i\leq k
by power-symbol reciprocity, and condition (7) follows.
∎

11.
Lifting local points

Let
f:X\rightarrow Q
be a smooth proper fibration over a Galois quasi-trivial torus
Q=R_{E/K}\mathbb{G}_{m}
defined over a number field
K
, endowed with a multi-section
s:Q^{L,n,(1)}\rightarrow X
for some
n
such that
\mu_{n}\subset E^{*}
and some field extension
L/K
containing
E
, as in the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q^{L,n,(1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

In this section, we show how
for such an
f
one may employ the multi-section and the arithmetic results of Section
10
to produce parameters
q\in Q(K)
and adelic points
(P_{v}(q))\in X_{q}(\mathbb{A}_{K})^{(\Br X)_{+}}
.

Let
S\subset M_{K}
be a large enough finite set of places. More explicitly, let
S
be large enough so that:

•

it contains all archimedean places, all places dividing
2n
, and all places ramifying in
L
;

•

there exist a smooth proper
\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
-model
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}
for the morphism
f
(we still denote the model with
f
with an abuse of notation);

•

the map
\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
is surjective for all places
v\notin S
.

Combining routine spreading out results, with the addition of Lang–Weil estimates and Hensel’s lemma for the third condition, such a choice of
S
can always be made.

Definition
.

(1). A
K
-point
q\in Q(K)
is
(L,n)
-liftable
outside
S
if for all
v\notin S
,
q
lies in
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\cup\im({Q^{L,n,(1)}(K_{v})\rightarrow Q(K_{v})})
.

(2). A prime-variational grid
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
is
(L,n)
-liftable
outside
S
if all of its elements are
(L,n)
-liftable
and, for all
v\notin S
and
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
, the quotient
q^{\prime}q^{-1}
belongs to
\im(Q^{L,n}(K_{v})\rightarrow Q(K_{v}))
.

Definition
.

For a group
G
and a subset
\mathcal{S}\subset E^{*}
, a function
f:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow G
is
multiplicative
if it extends to a homomorphism
f:\langle\mathcal{S}\rangle\rightarrow G
, where
\langle\mathcal{S}\rangle
denotes the subgroup of
E^{*}
generated by
\mathcal{S}
.

The following lemma guarantees that, for prime-variational grids made of
(L,n)
-liftable elements, local lifts may be chosen multiplicatively.

Lemma 11.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
be a prime-variational grid all of whose elements are
(L,n)
-liftable outside
S
for some
n\geq 2
. Then, for every place
v\notin S
such that
\mathcal{M}\not\subset\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
, there exists an embedding
\iota:L\hookrightarrow K_{v}
and lifts
q^{L,n}\in{Q^{L,n,(1)}(K_{v})}_{\iota}
for all
q\in\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
such that the function
\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\rightarrow{Q^{L,n,(1)}(K_{v})}_{\iota}:q\mapsto q^{L,n}
is multiplicative.

Moreover, if
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
is
(L,n)
-liftable, then
(q^{\prime})^{L,n}(q^{L,n})^{-1}
belongs to
\im({Q^{L,n}(K_{v})}_{\iota}\rightarrow{Q^{L,n,(1)}(K_{v})}_{\iota})
for all
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
.

The multiplications in
{Q^{L,n,(1)}(K_{v})}_{\iota}
are meant with respect to the
L
-group scheme structure of
Q^{L,n,(1)}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m,L}^{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

Write

\mathcal{M}=q_{0}\cdot\{q_{1,1},\ldots,q_{1,M}\}\cdot\{q_{2,1},\ldots,q_{2,M}\}\cdots\{q_{k,1},\ldots,q_{k,M}\}\subset\mathcal{O}_{E,S}\setminus\{0\}.

and let
\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{1},\ldots,\Sigma_{r}
be the places dividing
q_{0},\prod_{j}q_{1,j},\ldots,\prod_{j}q_{k,j}
, respectively. The
\Sigma_{i}
are disjoint because
\mathcal{M}
is square-free (being prime-variational). The places
v
in the statement are precisely those in the union
\Sigma_{0}\sqcup\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\Sigma_{r}
. For such
v
we have

\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})=\begin{cases}\mathcal{M}&\text{if }v\in\Sigma_{0}\\
q_{0}\cdot\{q_{1,1},\ldots,q_{1,M}\}\cdots q_{i,j}\cdots\{q_{k,1},\ldots,q_{k,M}\}&\text{if }v\in\Sigma_{i},\,i\geq 1,\\
\end{cases}

where
q_{i,j}\in\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
is the prime element whose underlying prime in
K
is the place associated to
v
.
In both cases,
\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
is a grid, that we denote by
\mathcal{M}_{v}
. Now, for all
v
as above and all
q\in\mathcal{M}_{v}
, our hypothesis says that
q
lifts to
{Q^{L,n,(1)}(K_{v})}
. Equivalently, as in the proof of Theorem
10.7
(see page 51), there exists a
K
-embedding
\iota:L\hookrightarrow K_{v}
such that

(\star)
the image of
q^{\sigma}
under
\iota
is an
n
-th power for all
\sigma\neq\text{id}
.

Property
(\star)
clearly depends only on the restriction of
\iota
to
E
. Let
w
be the place of
E
associated to
\iota|_{E}
. We claim that
w
does not depend on
q
.

In fact, the places of
E
dividing
v
form a unique orbit under the
\Gamma
-action, and since
q\notin\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
, for at least one of these places
q
must have non-zero valuation. Since the grid
\mathcal{M}
is square-free, the valuation of
q
at each of these places is either
0
or
1
.
Since
(\star)
forces
w^{\sigma}(q)=w(q^{\sigma^{-1}})
to be a multiple of
n
for all
\sigma\neq\text{id}
, it must then be that
w(q)>0
and so
w(q)=1
. But, if
v\in\Sigma_{i},\,i\geq 1
(resp.
v\in\Sigma_{0}
), we have
w(q)=w(q_{i,j})
(resp.
w(q)=w(q_{0})
) and so
w
is the unique place associated to the prime
q_{i,j}
(resp. the unique place of
E
above
v
with
w(q_{0})=1
), proving the claim.

In particular, fixing an embedding
\iota:L\hookrightarrow K_{v}
whose restriction to
E
is associated to
w
, we can lift all
q\in\mathcal{M}_{v}
to
Q^{L,n(1)}(K_{v})_{\iota}
.

We rewrite now
\mathcal{M}_{v}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{v}

\displaystyle=q_{\text{base}}\cdot\Delta_{1}\cdots\Delta_{i-1}\cdot\Delta_{i+1}\cdots\Delta_{k},\text{ where }

\displaystyle q_{\text{base}}:

\displaystyle=q_{0}\cdot q_{1,1}\cdots q_{i-1,1}\cdot q_{i,j}\cdot q_{i+1,1}\cdots q_{k,1},

\displaystyle\Delta_{\alpha}:

\displaystyle=\{1,\delta_{\alpha,1},\ldots,\delta_{\alpha,M-1}\},\,\delta_{\alpha,\beta}:=q_{\alpha,\beta+1}q_{\alpha,1}^{-1}.

Let also
q_{\alpha}:=q_{\text{base}}\cdot\delta_{\alpha,\beta}\in\mathcal{M}_{v}
.
Choose now arbitrary lifts
q_{\text{base}}^{L,n}
and
q_{\alpha}^{L,n}
to
Q^{L,n(1)}(K_{v})_{\iota}
. Define
\delta_{\alpha,\beta}^{L,n}:=q_{\alpha,\beta}^{L,n}\cdot(q_{\text{base}}^{L,n})^{-1}
, and extend the lifts multiplicatively to all
\mathcal{M}_{v}
. Now condition (
M
) holds.

For the last statement, note that, since
Q^{L,n}/Q
is Galois, the inverse images of
q^{\prime}q^{-1}
in
Q^{L,n}(\overline{K}_{v})
are permuted transtitively by
\Gal(Q^{L,n}/Q)
, and in particular they all have the same definition field. Since
q^{\prime}q^{-1}\in\im(Q^{L,n}(K_{v})\rightarrow Q(K_{v}))
, the common field of definition is
K_{v}
. In particular, any point in
Q^{L,n}(\overline{K}_{v})
that is an inverse image of
(q^{\prime})^{L,n}(q^{L,n})^{-1}
is in fact defined over
K_{v}
, i.e. lies in
Q^{L,n}(K_{v})\subset Q^{L,n}(\overline{K}_{v})
. We conclude by observing that any inverse image of an element of
Q^{L,n,(1)}(K_{v})_{\iota}
in
Q^{L,n}(K_{v})
lies necessarily in
Q^{L,n}(K_{v})_{\iota}
.
∎

Warning.
The existence of even just one
(L,n)
-liftable element (with at least one prime divisor outside
S
) in
Q(K)
is not immediate at all, but is rather the point of Section
10
: Theorem
10.7
produces grids with
(L,n)
-liftable elements.

Given opens
U_{v}\subset X(K_{v}),\,v\in S\cap M_{K}^{\text{fin}},U_{\infty}\subset X(K_{\infty})_{dir}
and a prime-variational grid
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
that is contained in
f(U_{v})
for all finite
v\in S
, whose image in
Q(K_{\infty})_{dir}
is contained in
f(U_{\infty})
,
and all of whose elements are
(L,n)
-liftable for some pair
(L,n)
with
n\geq 2
, we use Lemma
11.1
to define adelic lifts as follows. For each
q\in\mathcal{M}
, we let
(P_{v}(q))_{v}\in X_{q}(\mathbb{A}_{K})
be defined by:

•

P_{v}(q)
lies in
U_{v}
for all
v\in S
;

•

P_{v}(q)
lies in
\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\subset X(K_{v})
for all
v
such that
q\in\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
;

•

P_{v}(q)=s(q_{L,n})
, where
q_{L,n}\in Q^{L,n(1)}(K_{v})
is as in Lemma
11.1
.

It is clear from our choice of
S
that such adelic lifts exist for every
q\in\mathcal{M}
.

11.1.
Brauer–Manin pairing with
(\Br X)_{+}

Recall that
(\Br X)_{+}:=(\Br X)_{+}(\mathfrak{A}\times_{Q}X)
from Subsection
7.3
.

Proposition 11.2
.

Let
q\in\mathcal{O}_{E,S}\subset Q(K)
be coprime in
\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
with its conjugates
q^{\tau}
for all
\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}
, and let
(P_{v})_{v\in M_{K}}\in X_{q}(\mathbb{A}_{K})
be such that
P_{v}\in s(Q^{L,n(1)}(K_{v}))
for
v\notin S
with
q\notin\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
. For
b\in(\Br X)_{+}
such that
s^{*}b=0
, we have

((P_{v})_{v},b)_{BM}=\sum_{v\in S}\inv_{v}(b(P_{v}))+\sum_{\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}}a_{\tau}\operatorname{hs}_{S,n_{\tau}}(q),

where
a_{\tau}\in(\mathbb{Z}/n_{\tau}\mathbb{Z})(-1)
is uniquely defined by
\partial_{Z^{\prime}_{\tau}}(b)=a_{\tau}\cdot\hat{y}\in H^{1}(k(Z^{\prime}_{\tau}),\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

Write:

(11.1)

((P_{v})_{v},b)_{BM}=\sum_{v\in S}\inv_{v}(b(P_{v}))+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\notin S,\\
q\in\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\end{subarray}}\inv_{v}(b(P_{v}))+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\notin S,\\
q\notin\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\end{subarray}}\inv_{v}(b(P_{v})).

The third sum is zero as
P_{v}\in s(Q^{L,n(1)}(K_{v}))
for the
v
appearing there and
s^{*}b=0
by assumption.
By Lemma
7.12
, the second sum is

(11.2)

\sum_{\tau\in\Gamma[2]^{**}}a_{\tau}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\notin S,\\
q\in\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\mid v\\
w\in M_{E^{\tau}}\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{q}{\mathcal{I}_{w}}\right]_{n_{\tau}}

By definition of half-spin symbols, we have

\operatorname{hs}_{S,n_{\tau}}(q)=\sum_{v\notin S}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\mid v\\
w\in M_{E^{\tau}}\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{q}{\mathcal{I}_{w}}\right]_{n_{\tau}}.

Thus to prove the statement, it suffices to prove that
\left[\frac{q}{\mathcal{I}_{w}}\right]_{n_{\tau}}=0
for all
w\in M_{E^{\tau}}
dividing a
v\notin S
such that
q\notin\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
.
For such
w
, the existence of
P_{v}\in X_{q}(K_{v})\cap s(Q^{L,n(1)}(K_{v}))
implies that
q\in\im(Q^{L,n,(1)}(K_{v})\rightarrow Q(K_{v}))
. I.e. there exists a
K
-embedding
\iota:L\rightarrow K_{v}
such that
\iota(q^{\sigma})\in K_{v}^{*n}
for all
\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}
. In particular,
v
splits completely in
E
and
q\in E_{w^{\prime}}^{*n}
for all places
w^{\prime}\mid v
of
E
that are
different from
the one place
w_{E}\in M_{E}
lying below the place
w_{L}
of
L
corresponding to
\iota
. At least one of the two places
w^{\prime}
of
E
dividing the place
w
of
E^{\tau}
is different from
w_{E}
, and thus
\left[\frac{q}{w^{\prime}}\right]_{n}=0
for this
w^{\prime}
. Writing
\left[\frac{q}{\mathcal{I}_{w}}\right]_{n_{\tau}}=w^{\prime}(q^{\sigma}-q)\cdot\left[\frac{q}{w}\right]_{n_{\tau}}=w^{\prime}(q^{\sigma}-q)\cdot\left[\frac{q}{w^{\prime}}\right]_{n_{\tau}}
, where the last identity holds because
w^{\prime}
has degree
1
over
w
, we conclude since
n_{\tau}\mid n
.
∎

Remark
.

For a real place
v
, the local Brauer pairing
X^{\sharp}(K_{v})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z},\,P_{v}\mapsto\inv_{v}(b(P_{v}))
might not factor through
\pi_{0}(X(K_{v}))
, but it rather only factors through
\pi_{0}(X^{\sharp}(K_{v}))
. The connected components of
X^{\sharp}(K_{v})
can be smaller than those of
X(K_{v})
, and this ultimately forces us to have some degree of archimedean approximation in our arguments. Luckily,
Q^{\sharp}(K_{v})\subset Q(K_{v})

has a “conic shape”, i.e. it is invariant under the action of
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, and the connected components of
X^{\sharp}(K_{v})
are just inverse images of (some) of those of
Q^{\sharp}(K_{v})
.

So to gain control over
\inv_{v}(b(P_{v}(q)))
at real places, the working solution that we adopted is to make sure that
q
approximates a given direction in
Q(K_{v})
in the sense of Section
2
(in fact, since it requires no further effort, we do even more, and approximate a simultaneous direction in
\prod_{v\mid\infty}Q(K_{v})
). This is our motivation behind Appendix
A
.

12.
Triple Variation theorem

The purpose of this section is to compute under certain technical hypotheses the triple variation over a grid of fibers of the Brauer–Manin pairing of the adelic points defined in Section
11
. (The “technical hypotheses” are satisfied by the grids produced by Theorem
10.7
.) See Theorem
12.1
below.

12.1.
Main result

Let
Q=R_{E/K}\mathbb{G}_{m}
be a Galois quasi-trivial torus defined over a number field
K
. Let
\Gamma=\Gal(E/K)
, and
n
be a natural number such that
\mu_{n}\subset E^{*}
.

Notation
.

For a fixed total ordering
<
of
\Gamma
, an element
\bar{\beta}\in H^{3}(Q\otimes_{K}\overline{K},\mu_{n})
may be written uniquely as

(12.1)

\bar{\beta}=\sum_{\sigma_{1}<\sigma_{2}<\sigma_{3}}a_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}(\bar{\beta})\cdot(y^{\sigma_{1}}\cup y^{\sigma_{2}}\cup y^{\sigma_{3}})_{n},\ \ a_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}(\bar{\beta})\in(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(-2).

For any three pairwise distinct elements
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\in\Gamma
, set
a_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}(\bar{\beta}):=\linebreak(-1)^{\sgn s}a_{s(\sigma_{1}),s(\sigma_{2}),s(\sigma_{3})}(\bar{\beta})
for
s\in S_{3}
a permutation such that
s(\sigma_{1})<s(\sigma_{2})<s(\sigma_{3})
, and set
a_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}(\bar{\beta}):=0
if two of the indices are repeated. Since the cup-product is alternating, the elements
a_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}(\bar{\beta})
just defined do not depend on the chosen ordering of
\Gamma
.

Acting by
{{g}}\in\Gamma
on (
12.1
) gives

(12.2)

a_{\sigma_{1}{{g}},\sigma_{2}{{g}},\sigma_{3}{{g}}}(\bar{\beta}^{{{g}}})=a_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}(\bar{\beta})^{{{g}}}
