In particular, since
\d\alpha\in\check{C}^{3}(\mathcal{V}/X,\mu_{n})
is the image of
\beta\in\check{Z}^{3}(\tilde{Q}/Q,\mu_{n})
under the upper horizontal map, the coborder of the pullback
\alpha_{sec}
of
\alpha
to
{\check{C}^{2}(\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{sec}/Q_{sec},\mu_{n})}
is the image of the pullback
\beta_{sec}
of
\beta
to
{\check{Z}^{3}(\tilde{Q}/Q_{sec},\mu_{n})}
. Bu
\beta_{sec}
is zero by our assumptions, and so
\alpha_{sec}
is a cocycle. We denote by
b_{\alpha}
its image in
\Br Q_{sec}
under the composition
{\check{Z}^{2}(\mathcal{V}_{sec}/Q_{sec},\mu_{n})}\rightarrow H^{2}(Q_{sec},\mu_{n})\rightarrow H^{2}(Q_{sec},\mathbb{G}_{m})
. Since, by our assumptions of compatibility with the multi-section, the morphism
(P_{v}(q),\overline{P_{v}(q)}):(\Spec K_{v},\Spec\overline{K}_{v})\rightarrow(X,\tilde{\mathcal{V}})
factors as

(\Spec K_{v},\Spec\overline{K}_{v})\xrightarrow{(q^{L,n},\tilde{q}^{L,n})}(Q_{sec},\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{sec})\rightarrow(X,\tilde{\mathcal{V}}),

we have

\inv_{v}(\alpha(P_{v}(q),\overline{P_{v}(q)}))=\inv_{v}(b_{\alpha}(q^{L,n})).

For each
q\in\mathcal{M}_{v}
, the point
q^{L,n}\in Q^{n,L,(1)}(K_{v})_{\iota}
corresponds to a point of the form
(\iota(q),(\sqrt[n]{\iota(q^{\sigma})})_{\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}})\in(K_{v}^{*})^{\Gamma}
, for some choice of
n
-th roots, under the identification
Q^{n,L,(1)}(K_{v})_{\iota}=(K_{v}^{*})^{\Gamma}
induced by the isomorphism
Q^{L,n,1}=\Spec L[y^{\pm 1},(y^{\sigma})^{\pm\frac{1}{n}}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m,L}^{\Gamma}
of
L
-schemes (see the proof of Lemma
10.10
). The valuation of
\iota(q)
is
1
, while the valuation of
\iota(q^{\sigma})
is
0
for all
\sigma\neq\text{id}
, so the quotient
(q^{\prime})^{L,n}(q^{L,n})^{-1}
lies in
(\mathcal{O}_{v}^{*})^{\Gamma}\subset(K_{v}^{*})^{\Gamma}
for all
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{v}
.

We need the following lemma.

Lemma 12.14
.

Let
d
be a natural number,
b\in\Br\mathbb{G}_{m,K_{v}}^{d}
be of order coprime with
Nv
, and
t_{1},t_{2},s_{1},s_{2}
be elements of
(K_{v}^{*})^{d}=\mathbb{G}_{m,K_{v}}^{d}(K_{v})
. If the quotients
t_{2}t_{1}^{-1},s_{2}s_{1}^{-1}
lie in
(\mathcal{O}_{v}^{*})^{d}\subset(K_{v}^{*})^{d}
, then

\sum_{i,j\in\{1,2\}}(-1)^{i+j}\inv_{v}(b(t_{i}s_{j}))=0

Proof.

Write
\mathbb{G}_{m,K_{v}}^{d}=\Spec K_{v}[y_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,y_{d}^{\pm 1}]
. If
b\in\Br_{1}\mathbb{G}_{m,K_{v}}^{d}
, write
b=b_{0}+b_{1}
with
b_{0}\in\im\Br K_{v}
,
b_{1}=\sum_{i}(\chi_{i},y_{i})
for characters
\chi_{i}:\Gamma_{K_{v}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
. Then the sum in the statement vanishes for both
b=b_{0}
, as the function
\mathbb{G}_{m,K_{v}}^{d}(K_{v})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}:P\mapsto\inv_{v}(b_{0}(P))
is constant, and for
b=b_{1}
, as the function
\mathbb{G}_{m,K_{v}}^{d}(K_{v})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}:P\mapsto\inv_{v}(b_{1}(P))
is linear.

It thus suffices to prove the lemma for
b=c\cdot(y_{a},y_{b})_{m},\,a<b
with
m
coprime with
Nv
such that
\mu_{m}\subset K_{v}
and
c\in(\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z})(-1)
, since the transcendental Brauer group of split tori is generated by such twisted cup-products
[
7
, Proposition 9.1.2]
. We assume
b=c\cdot(y_{1},y_{2})_{m}
after pemuting the coordinates, and
c=1
after fixing an
m
-th root of unity. Then
\inv_{v}(b((p_{1},\ldots,p_{d})))=[p_{1},p_{2}]_{v}
, where the latter denotes the additive variant of the Tate pairing (taken with values in
\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}
instead of
\mu_{m}
via the identification
\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}=\mu_{m}
). So, writing
t_{i}=(t_{1,i},\ldots,t_{d,i}),\,s_{i}=(s_{1,i},\ldots,s_{d,i})
, the sum in the statement becomes

\sum_{i,j\in\{1,2\}}(-1)^{i+j}[t_{1,i}s_{1,j},t_{2,i}s_{2,j}]_{v}=[t_{1,2}t_{1,1}^{-1},s_{2,2}s_{2,1}^{-1}]_{v}+[s_{1,2}s_{1,1}^{-1},t_{2,2}t_{2,1}^{-1}]_{v},

which is trivial bacause the coprime-to-the-residual-characteristic Tate pairing of
v
-adic units vanishes.
∎

As the rank of
\mathcal{M}_{v}
is at least
2
and the variations of the grid
\{q^{n,L}\}_{q\in\mathcal{M}_{v}}
are
v
-adically integral as proven above, Lemma
12.14
gives

\sum_{q\in\mathcal{M}_{v}}(-1)^{\sgn(q)}\inv_{v}(b_{\alpha}(q^{L,n}))=0.

I.e. the contributions of the
q\in\mathcal{M}_{v}
in
\inv_{v}\left(\Delta^{(3)}\alpha(P_{v}(q),\overline{P_{v}(q)})-\eta_{v}\right)
cancel each other out. On the other hand, when
q\in\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{v}=\mathcal{M}\cap\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
, the cochain
\alpha(P_{v}(q),\overline{P_{v}(q)})
is unramified, and so contributes trivially. We thus get
\inv_{v}\left(\Delta^{(3)}\alpha(P_{v}(q),\overline{P_{v}(q)})-\eta_{v}\right)=0
as wished.

12.3.4.
Second contribution

The cochain
\Delta^{(3)}\gamma(q)\in C^{2}(\overline{K}/K,\mathbb{G}_{m})
is a primitive for
\Delta^{(3)}\beta(q,\bar{q})
. If
R\in C^{2}(\overline{K}/K,\mathbb{G}_{m})
is any other primitive for
\Delta^{(3)}\beta(q,\bar{q})
, then
\sum_{v}\inv_{v}(R_{v}-\Delta^{(3)}\gamma(q)_{v})=0
by the Albert–Brauer–Hasse–Noether theorem, and so

(12.14)

\sum_{v}\inv_{v}(\Delta^{(3)}\gamma(q)_{v}-\eta_{v})=\sum_{v}\inv_{v}(R_{v}-\eta_{v}).

Recall that

\mathcal{M}=q_{b}\cdot\left\{1,\delta_{1}\right\}\cdot\left\{1,\delta_{2}\right\}\cdot\left\{1,\delta_{3}\right\},\,q_{b}:=q_{0}q_{1,1}q_{2,1}q_{3,1},\,\delta_{i}=q_{i,2}q_{i,1}^{-1}.

In order to proceed further with the computation, it is useful to consider the point
p_{\mathcal{M}}:=(q_{b},\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\delta_{3})\in Q^{4}(K)
.

Notation
.

For
k\geq 0
, and a vector
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k})\in\{0,1\}^{k}
, we let
m_{\mathbf{v}}:Q^{k+1}\rightarrow Q
denote the morphism
(q_{0},\ldots,q_{k})\mapsto q_{0}\cdot\prod_{i\geq 1}q_{i}^{v_{i}}
.

The maps just defined may be used to recover the elements of
\mathcal{M}
from
p_{\mathcal{M}}
. Namely, for
\mathbf{v}:=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})\in\{0,1\}^{3}
, we have

m_{\mathbf{v}}(p_{\mathcal{M}})=q_{b}\delta_{1}^{v_{1}}\delta_{2}^{v_{2}}\delta_{3}^{v_{3}}\in\mathcal{M}.

Proposition
12.15
below, proved in
12.3.5
, shall give us a suitable
R
for (
12.14
).
Before stating it, we need to introduce suitable coordinates for the powers of
Q
.

Notation
.

For a power
Q^{k+1},\,k\geq 0
of
Q
, and
i\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, we let
\{y_{i}^{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in\Gamma}
be the set of generating characters of the
i
-th factor of
Q^{k+1}
, so that

Q^{k+1}=\Spec\left(\overline{K}[(y_{i}^{\sigma})^{\pm 1}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma,0\leq i\leq k}\right)^{\Gamma_{K}}.

We also let

(Q^{k+1})^{ur}:=\Spec\overline{K}[(y_{i}^{\sigma})^{\pm\frac{1}{\omega}}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma,0\leq i\leq k},\quad\pi_{1}(Q^{k+1}):=\Gal((Q^{k+1})^{ur}/Q^{k+1})

be the universal cover of
Q^{k+1}
and its étale fundamental group, respectively.

For any intermediate cover
Q^{\prime}
between
(Q^{k+1})^{ur}
and
Q^{k+1}
we set, as before,
\pi_{1}(Q^{\prime}):=\Gal((Q^{k+1})^{ur}/Q^{\prime})
by convention.

In the following, we view
\beta
as an element of
Z^{3}(\pi_{1}(Q),\mu_{n})
via the identification
Z^{3}(Q^{ur}/Q,\mu_{n})=Z^{3}(\pi_{1}(Q),\mu_{n})
given by (
2.3
).

Proposition 12.15
.

We have

\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{3}}(-1)^{|\mathbf{v}|}m_{\mathbf{v}}^{*}\beta=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Gamma^{*},\sigma_{1}\neq\sigma_{2}}\cores_{E/K}(a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}(\beta)\cdot(y_{1}\cup y_{2}^{\sigma_{1}}\cup y_{3}^{\sigma_{2}})_{n})+\d\delta

in
C^{3}(\pi_{1}(Q^{4}),\mu_{n})
, where each cup-product
(y_{1}\cup y_{2}^{\sigma_{1}}\cup y_{3}^{\sigma_{2}})_{n}
is viewed as an element of
Z^{3}(\pi_{1}(Q^{4}_{E}),\mu_{n})
, and
\delta\in C^{2}(\pi_{1}(Q^{4}),\mu_{n})
is such that for each
i=1,2,3
, the restriction of
\delta
to

C^{2}(H_{i},\mu_{n}),\,H_{i}:=\pi_{1}(Q^{i}\times_{K}Q^{K,n}\times_{K}Q^{3-i})

is a coborder.

Warning.
Here
(y_{1}\cup y_{2}^{\sigma_{1}}\cup y_{3}^{\sigma_{2}})_{n}\in Z^{3}(\pi_{1}(Q_{E}),\mu_{n})
really denotes the cup-product as a
group cocycle
and not as a mere cohomology class. Analogously,
\cores_{E/K}
denotes the corestriction operator on
group cochains

[
36
, p.48]
.

To the lifts
\bar{q}_{b},\bar{\delta}_{1},\bar{\delta}_{2},\bar{\delta}_{3}
defined in
12.3.2
, we may associate the point
\bar{p}_{\mathcal{M}}\in\tilde{Q}^{\times_{F}4}(\overline{K})_{incl}
with
\tilde{Q}^{\times_{F}4}=\tilde{Q}\times_{F}\tilde{Q}\times_{F}\tilde{Q}\times_{F}\tilde{Q}
. We may lift this further to a
\overline{K}
-point
\bar{p}_{\mathcal{M}}\in(Q^{4})^{ur}(\overline{K})_{id}
along the morphism
(Q^{4})^{ur}\rightarrow Q^{\times_{F}4}
(viewed as a morphism of
F
-schemes). We then define, for each
q=q_{b}\delta_{1}^{v_{1}}\delta_{2}^{v_{2}}\delta_{3}^{v_{3}}\in\mathcal{M}
, a lift
\bar{q}\in Q^{ur}(\overline{K})_{id}
as
m_{\mathbf{v}}(\bar{p}_{\mathcal{M}})
, where
m_{\mathbf{v}}
denotes the morphism
m_{\mathbf{v}}:(Q^{4})^{ur}\rightarrow Q
with
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})
. By the mulitplicativity imposed on the lifts in
\tilde{Q}
in
12.3.2
, each
\bar{q}
projects to the lift
\bar{q}\in\tilde{Q}(\overline{K})_{incl}
defined there.

Observe how, for each
q\in\mathcal{M}
, the lift
\bar{q}\in Q^{ur}(\overline{K})
defines a representation
D_{q}:\Gamma_{K}\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Q)
defined by
D_{q}(\gamma)\cdot\bar{q}=\bar{q}^{\gamma}
.

Analogously, for the point
p_{\mathcal{M}}\in(Q^{4})(\overline{K})
, the lift
\bar{p}_{\mathcal{M}}\in(Q^{4})^{ur}(\overline{K})
defines a decomposition representation
D_{{\mathcal{M}}}:\Gamma_{K}\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Q^{4})
defined by
D_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\gamma)\cdot\bar{p}_{\mathcal{M}}=(\bar{p}_{\mathcal{M}})^{\gamma}.
This recovers the representations
D_{q},\,q\in\mathcal{M}
: for each
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})\in\{0,1\}^{3}
, the homomorphism
D_{q}:\Gamma_{K}\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Q)
coincides with the composition

\Gamma_{K}\xrightarrow{D_{{\mathcal{M}}}}\pi_{1}(Q^{4})\xrightarrow{m_{\mathbf{v}}}\pi_{1}(Q)

by the definition of our lifts
\bar{q},q\in\mathcal{M}
.

Pulling back the identity of Proposition
12.15
along
D_{{\mathcal{M}}}
and using that for all
q=q_{b}\delta_{1}^{v_{1}}\delta_{2}^{v_{2}}\delta_{3}^{v_{3}}
, we have
m_{\mathbf{v}}\circ D_{\mathcal{M}}=D_{q}
, we get

\sum_{q\in\mathcal{M}}(-1)^{\sgn(q)}D_{q}^{*}(\beta)=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Gamma^{*},\sigma_{1}\neq\sigma_{2}}\cores_{E/K}(a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\cdot(\delta_{1}\cup\delta_{2}^{\sigma_{1}}\cup\delta_{3}^{\sigma_{2}})_{n})+d(D_{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\delta))

in
C^{3}(K,\mu_{n})
. Via (
12.5
), the left hand side is equal to
\sum_{q\in\mathcal{M}}(-1)^{\sgn(q)}\beta(q,\bar{q})=\Delta^{(3)}\beta(q,\bar{q})
.

By Lemma
5.6
, there exists a primitive
R_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\in C^{2}(E,(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(3))
for the cocycle
(\delta_{1}\cup\delta_{2}^{\sigma_{1}}\cup\delta_{3}^{\sigma_{2}})_{n}\in Z^{3}(E,(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(3))
. So now

R:=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Gamma,\sigma_{1}\neq\sigma_{2}}\cores_{E/K}(a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\cdot R_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}})+D_{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\delta)

gives another primitive for
\Delta^{(3)}\beta(q,\bar{q})
. Letting
\eta^{\prime}_{v}:=D_{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\delta)_{v}+\eta_{v}
for each
v
, we get

\eqref{R}=\sum_{v}\inv_{v}\left(\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\nolimits^{*}(\cores_{E/K}(a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\cdot R_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}})|_{\Gamma_{v}}+\eta^{\prime}_{v}\right),

where we are abbreviating via
\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}^{*}:=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Gamma^{*},\sigma_{1}\neq\sigma_{2}}
.

The cochain
D_{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\delta)
is everywhere unramified. In fact, this is clear for
v\notin S
such that for which
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
. For
v\in S
and
v\notin S
such that
\mathcal{M}\cap\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\neq\emptyset
, the elements
\delta_{i}\in Q(K)=E^{*}
with
i\neq i_{v}
(recall that
i_{v}\in\{0,1,2,3\}
denotes the index such that
q_{i^{\prime},j}\in\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
for all
i^{\prime}\neq i_{v}
) lie in the image of
Q(K_{v})\xrightarrow{[n]}Q(K_{v})
: while for
v\in S
this is clear because all
\delta_{i}
’s are close to
1
(resp. close to
1
after scaling by a positive real factor) if
v
is non-archimedean (resp. archimedean), for
v\notin S
this holds because the quotients
q^{\prime}\cdot q^{-1},\,q,q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{v}
are assumed to lie in
Q^{L,n}(K_{v})\rightarrow Q(K_{v})
, which factors through
Q(K_{v})\xrightarrow{[n]}Q(K_{v})
.

Thus the composition
\Gamma_{v}\rightarrow\Gamma_{K}\xrightarrow{D_{\mathcal{M}}}\pi_{1}(Q^{4})
lands in
H_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,2,3\}\setminus\{i_{v}\}
. So the cochain
D_{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\delta)
is everywhere unramified as wished. Thus
\eta^{\prime}_{v}
is unramified for all
v
.

The cocycle
(\delta_{1}\cup\delta_{2}^{\sigma_{1}}\cup\delta_{3}^{\sigma_{2}})_{n}
is everywhere unramified (
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}^{\sigma_{1}},\delta_{3}^{\sigma_{2}}
satisfy condition (C)), so for each place
w
of
E
, there exists
\xi_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},w}\in C^{2}(\Gal(E_{w}^{ur}/E_{w}),(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(3))
with
\d\xi_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},w}=(\delta_{1}\cup\delta_{2}^{\sigma_{1}}\cup\delta_{3}^{\sigma_{2}})_{n}|_{\Gamma_{w}}
. We may also assume that
\xi_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},w}=0
when
w\mid S
, since
(\delta_{1}\cup\delta_{2}^{\sigma_{1}}\cup\delta_{3}^{\sigma_{2}})_{n}|_{\Gamma_{w}}=0
for such
w
as all conjugates of all
\delta_{i}
’s are
n
-th powers in
E_{w}
. So, rewriting (
12.14
) as

\displaystyle\sum_{v}\inv_{v}\left(\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\nolimits^{*}(\cores_{E/K}(a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\cdot R_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}})|_{\Gamma_{v}}+\eta^{\prime}_{v}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{v}\inv_{v}\left(\sum_{w\mid v}\left(\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\nolimits^{*}\cores_{E_{w}/K_{v}}(a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\cdot R_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}|_{\Gamma_{w}})\right)+\eta^{\prime}_{v}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{v}\inv_{v}\left(\sum_{w\mid v}\left(\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\nolimits^{*}\cores_{E_{w}/K_{v}}(a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\cdot R_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}|_{\Gamma_{w}}-a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\cdot\xi_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},w})\right)+\eta^{\prime\prime}_{v}\right)

with
\eta^{\prime\prime}_{v}:=\sum_{w\mid v}\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\nolimits^{*}\left(\cores_{E_{w}/K_{v}}(a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\cdot\xi_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},w})\right)+\eta^{\prime}_{v}
, this
\eta^{\prime\prime}_{v}
is unramified for all
v
as it is the corestriction of an unramified cochain via the unramified extension
E_{w}/K_{v}
for
v\notin S
, and it is
0
for
v\in S
. Since each
R_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}|_{\Gamma_{w}}-\xi_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},w}
is a cocycle for each
w
by construction, we get that
\eta^{\prime\prime}_{v}
is also a cocycle and
\inv_{v}(\eta^{\prime\prime}_{v})=0
for all
v
because it is unramified. Thus, we may rearrange the sum as:

(
12.14
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\nolimits^{*}\sum_{v}\inv_{v}\left(\sum_{w\mid v}\cores_{E_{w}/K_{v}}(a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\cdot(R_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}|_{\Gamma_{w}}-\xi_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},w}))\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\nolimits^{*}\sum_{w}\inv_{w}(a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\cdot(R_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}|_{\Gamma_{w}}-\xi_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},w}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\nolimits^{*}a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\cdot\sum_{w}\inv_{w}(R_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}|_{\Gamma_{w}}-\xi_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},w})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Gamma^{*},\sigma_{1}\neq\sigma_{2}}a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\cdot[\delta_{1},\delta_{2}^{\sigma_{1}},\delta_{3}^{\sigma_{2}}]_{n}=R_{{\bar{\partial}(b)}}[\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\delta_{3}],

concluding the proof.

12.3.5.
Proof of Proposition
12.15

We need some additional notation before going to the heart of the proof.

Let
\bar{Q}:=Q\otimes_{K}\overline{K}=\Spec\overline{K}[y_{\sigma}^{\pm 1}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma}
and
\pi_{1}(\bar{Q}):=\Gal(Q^{ur}/\bar{Q})\cong\widehat{}\mathbb{Z}[\Gamma](1)
be its fundamental group. Galois theory provides a split short exact sequence

1\rightarrow\pi_{1}(\bar{Q})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Q)\rightarrow\Gamma_{K}\rightarrow 1.

For
k\geq 0
, we let

G_{n}^{(k+1)}:=\pi_{1}(Q^{k+1})/n\pi_{1}(\bar{Q}^{k+1})\text{ and }\bar{G}_{n}^{(k+1)}:=\pi_{1}(\bar{Q}^{k+1})/n\pi_{1}(\bar{Q}^{k+1}).

These corresponds via Galois Theory to the Galois groups of

\Spec\overline{K}[(y_{i}^{\sigma})^{\pm\frac{1}{n}}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma,0\leq i\leq k}/Q^{k+1},\quad\Spec\overline{K}[(y_{i}^{\sigma})^{\pm\frac{1}{n}}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma,0\leq i\leq k}/\bar{Q}^{k+1},

respectively. By Galois theory, we have
G_{n}^{(k+1)}=\bar{G}_{n}^{(k+1)}\rtimes\Gamma_{K}
and
\bar{G}_{n}^{(k+1)}=\bar{G}_{n}^{[0]}\oplus\cdot\oplus\bar{G}_{n}^{[k]}
, where each
\bar{G}_{n}^{[j]}
denotes the Galois group of

\Spec\overline{K}[(y_{i}^{\sigma})^{\pm\frac{1}{n}}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma,0\leq i\leq k}/\Spec\overline{K}[y_{j}^{\pm 1},(y_{i}^{\sigma})^{\pm\frac{1}{n}}]_{\sigma\in\Gamma,0\leq i\leq k,\,i\neq j}.

For each subset
J\subset\{0,\ldots,k\}
, we let
\bar{G}_{n}^{[J]}:=\oplus_{j\in J}\bar{G}_{n}^{[j]}
and
G_{n}^{[J]}:=\bar{G}_{n}^{[J]}\rtimes\Gamma_{K}
.

Recall that we are assuming that
\beta
lies in the image of the embedding
Z^{3}(G_{n},\mu_{n})\rightarrow Z^{3}(\pi_{1}(Q),\mu_{n})
.
We shall prove a slightly stronger statement than Proposition
12.15
:

Proposition 12.16
.

Viewing
\beta
as an element of
Z^{3}(G_{n},\mu_{n})
, the identity

\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{3}}m_{\mathbf{v}}^{*}(\beta)=-\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Gamma}\cores_{E/K}(a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}(\beta)\cdot(y_{1}\cup y_{2}^{\sigma_{1}}\cup y_{3}^{\sigma_{2}})_{n})+\d\delta

holds in
Z^{3}(G_{n}^{(4)},\mu_{n})
for some
\delta\in C^{2}(G_{n}^{(4)},\mu_{n})
restricting trivially to
C^{2}(G_{n}^{(I)},\mu_{n})
for
I=\{0,1,2\},\{0,1,3\}
and
\{0,2,3\}
.

Proof.

We divide the proof in three steps: we first prove that the two sides reduce to the same class in
H^{3}(\bar{G}_{n}^{(4)},\mu_{n})
, then that they reduce to the same class in
H^{3}(G_{n}^{(4)},\mu_{n})
, and finally that we may find the primitive
\delta
for the difference satisfying the sought vanishings.

Step 1.
Choose a total ordering
<
of
\Gamma
. The image of
\beta
in

H^{3}(\bar{G}_{n},\mu_{n})
lies in
H^{3}(\bar{G}_{n},\mu_{n})_{cup}
, and so we may write it as

\sum_{\sigma_{1}<\sigma_{2}<\sigma_{3}}a_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}(\beta)\cdot(y^{\sigma_{1}}\cup y^{\sigma_{2}}\cup y^{\sigma_{3}})_{n}.

In this proof, all cup-products shall denote cup-products of order
n
, so we drop the corresponding subscript from notation.
For a fixed triple
\sigma_{1}<\sigma_{2}<\sigma_{3}
, the triple variation of
y^{\sigma_{1}}\cup y^{\sigma_{2}}\cup y^{\sigma_{3}}
in
H^{3}(\bar{G}_{n}^{(4)},\mu_{n})
is:

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{3}}(-1)^{|\mathbf{v}|}\cdot m_{\mathbf{v}}^{*}(y^{\sigma_{1}}\cup y^{\sigma_{2}}\cup y^{\sigma_{3}})=\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{3}}(-1)^{|\mathbf{v}|}\cdot(y_{0}^{\sigma_{1}}+v_{1}y_{1}^{\sigma_{1}}+v_{2}y_{2}^{\sigma_{1}}+v_{3}y_{3}^{\sigma_{1}})

\displaystyle\phantom{\sum_{a}}\cup(y_{0}^{\sigma_{2}}+v_{1}y_{1}^{\sigma_{2}}+v_{2}y_{2}^{\sigma_{2}}+v_{3}y_{3}^{\sigma_{2}})\cup(y_{0}^{\sigma_{3}}+v_{1}y_{1}^{\sigma_{3}}+v_{2}y_{2}^{\sigma_{3}}+v_{3}y_{3}^{\sigma_{3}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{3}}\sum_{{h}:\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\}\rightarrow\{0,\ldots,4\}}(-1)^{|\mathbf{v}|}\cdot v_{{h}(\sigma_{1})}v_{{h}(\sigma_{2})}v_{{h}(\sigma_{3})}\cdot y_{{h}(\sigma_{1})}^{\sigma_{1}}\cup y_{{h}(\sigma_{2})}^{\sigma_{2}}\cup y_{{h}(\sigma_{3})}^{\sigma_{3}},

where the last internal sum is over all functions
h
as in the index, and in the summand we adopt the convention
v_{0}:=1
. Swapping the sums we get:

\displaystyle=\sum_{{h}:\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\}\rightarrow\{0,\ldots,4\}}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{3}}(-1)^{|\mathbf{v}|}\cdot v_{{h}(\sigma_{1})}v_{{h}(\sigma_{2})}v_{{h}(\sigma_{3})}\right)\cdot y_{{h}(\sigma_{1})}^{\sigma_{1}}\cup y_{{h}(\sigma_{2})}^{\sigma_{2}}\cup y_{{h}(\sigma_{3})}^{\sigma_{3}},

Note that the internal sum vanishes if the image of
{h}
is different than
\{1,2,3\}
since when this is the case, there exists
i\in\{1,2,3\}\setminus\im{h}
and then the summands with
v_{i}=0
cancel out those with
v_{i}=1
. On the other hand, when the image of
h
is
\{1,2,3\}
, the internal sum becomes
-1
. Since the functions
{h}:\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\}\rightarrow\{0,\ldots,4\}
with image
\{1,2,3\}
are permuted on transitively and faithfully by the
S_{3}
-action on
\{1,2,3\}
, the sum above then becomes:

-\sum_{s\in S_{3}}y_{s(1)}^{\sigma_{1}}\cup y_{s(2)}^{\sigma_{2}}\cup y_{s(3)}^{\sigma_{3}}=-\sum_{s\in\operatorname{Sym}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})}(-1)^{\sgn(s)}\cdot y_{1}^{s(\sigma_{1})}\cup y_{2}^{s(\sigma_{2})}\cup y_{3}^{s(\sigma_{3})}.

The image of the triple variation
\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{3}}m_{\mathbf{v}}^{*}(\beta)
of
\beta
in
H^{3}(\bar{G}_{n}^{(4)},\mu_{n})
is then the weighted sum of the expression above over all triples
\sigma_{1}<\sigma_{2}<\sigma_{3}
, with weights
a_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}(\beta)
, which is just:

-\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\,\in\Gamma}a_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}(\beta)\cdot(y^{\sigma_{1}}\cup y^{\sigma_{2}}\cup y^{\sigma_{3}})_{n},

finishing the computation of image of the left hand side in
H^{3}(\bar{G}_{n},\mu_{n})
.

The opposite of the right hand side is

\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\cores_{E/K}(a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}(\beta)\cdot(y\cup y^{\sigma_{1}}\cup y^{\sigma_{2}})_{n})=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\rho}a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}(\beta)^{\rho}\cdot(y\cup y^{\sigma_{1}}\cup y^{\sigma_{2}})_{n}^{\rho}\\
=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\rho}a_{\rho,\sigma_{1}\rho,\sigma_{2}\rho}(\beta)\cdot(y^{\rho}\cup y^{\sigma_{1}\rho}\cup y^{\sigma_{2}\rho})_{n}=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}a_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}(\beta)\cdot(y^{\sigma_{1}}\cup y^{\sigma_{2}}\cup y^{\sigma_{3}})_{n}

and coincides with the left hand side as wished.

Step 2.
By the point above, the sum

\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{3}}m_{\mathbf{v}}^{*}(\beta)+\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Gamma}\cores_{E/K}(a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}(\beta)\cdot(y\cup y^{\sigma_{1}}\cup y^{\sigma_{2}})_{n})

lies in the kernel of
H^{3}(G_{n}^{(4)},\mu_{n})\rightarrow H^{3}(\bar{G}_{n}^{(4)},\mu_{n})
.
By Nakaoka’s theorem, this kernel decomposes as

\bigoplus_{i=0,1,2}H^{i}(\Gamma_{K},H^{2-i}(\bar{G}_{n}^{(4)},\mu_{n})).

Write
\bar{G}_{n}^{(4)}=\bar{G}_{n}^{[0]}\oplus\bar{G}_{n}^{[1]}\oplus\bar{G}_{n}^{[2]}\oplus\bar{G}_{n}^{[3]}
, where each
\bar{G}_{n}^{[i]}
is a copy of
\bar{G}_{n}
. Using Künneth’s formula, the sum above decomposes as

\bigoplus_{i+j_{1}+\ldots+j_{4}=2}H^{i}\left(\Gamma_{K},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(1)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}}\bigotimes_{k=0,1,2,3}H^{j_{k}}(\bar{G}_{n}^{[k]},\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})\right).

Since in the latter tensor product, for any fixed string
(i,j_{1},\ldots,j_{4})
, at most two factors appear, it is clear that any element that restricts to zero in
H^{3}(G_{n}^{[J]},\mu_{n})
for all subgroups
J\subset\{0,1,2,3\}
of cardinality
2
must be zero. Such are both
\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{3}}m_{\mathbf{v}}^{*}(\beta)
and
\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Gamma}\cores_{E/K}(a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}(\beta)\cdot(y\cup y^{\sigma_{1}}\cup y^{\sigma_{2}})_{n})
, and thus so is their sum.

Step 3.
Let
\delta
be any primitive of

(\star)=\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{3}}m_{\mathbf{v}}^{*}(\beta)+\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Gamma}\cores_{E/K}(a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}(\beta)\cdot(y\cup y^{\sigma_{1}}\cup y^{\sigma_{2}})_{n}).

For each subset
J\subset\{0,1,2,3\}
with
0\in J
, we define

\delta_{J}=pr_{J}^{*}(\delta)|_{G_{n}^{[J]}},

where
pr_{J}:G_{n}^{(4)}\rightarrow G_{n}^{[J]}
denotes the natural projection. Note that

\d\delta=\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{3}}m_{\mathbf{v}}^{*}(\beta)+\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Gamma}\cores_{E/K}(a_{\text{id},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}(\beta)\cdot(y\cup y^{\sigma_{1}}\cup y^{\sigma_{2}})_{n})

restricts trivially to
G^{[J]}_{n}
for all proper subgroups
J\subset\{0,1,2,3\}
with
0\in J
. Thus

\d\left(\sum_{J}(-1)^{\#J}\delta_{J}\right)=\d\delta_{\{0,1,2,3\}}=\d\delta

and so
\sum_{J}(-1)^{\#J}\delta_{J}
is also a primitive of
(\star)
. Replacing
\delta
with other primitive proves the statement, since this sum restricts trivially to
G_{I}
for all
I
as in the statement.
∎

13.
Proof of Main Theorem

We conclude here the proof of Theorem
3.1
, and thus also of Theorem
1.4
, by proving the following:

Theorem 13.1
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Q
be a smooth projective morphism with rationally connected fibers, with
Q
a quasi-trivial torus. Further assume that
(R_{1})-(R_{4})
hold.
Then

(
\star
)

X(K_{\Omega})_{dir}^{\Br_{ur}X}=\overline{\bigcup_{q\in Q(K)}X_{q}(\mathbb{A}_{K})_{\bullet}^{\Br X_{q}}}.

Let
B_{tr}\coloneqq\Coker(\Br X_{\eta}\xrightarrow{r}\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q))
. We start with the following:

Corollary 13.2

(of Theorems
10.8
and
12.1
)
.

In the setting of Theorem
13.1
, for a sufficiently large set of places
S
, the following holds. For every collection of local points
(P_{v})_{v\in S}\in X(K_{S})
, and every function
g:\{1,\ldots,M-1\}^{3}\rightarrow B_{tr}^{D}
, there exists a grid of parameters

\mathcal{M}=\{q_{0}\}\cdot\{q_{1,1},\cdots,q_{1,M}\}\cdot\{q_{2,1},\cdots,q_{2,M}\}\cdot\{q_{3,1},\cdots,q_{3,M}\}\subset Q(K)

and adelic points
(P_{v}(q))\in X_{q}(\mathbb{A}_{K})^{r(\Br X_{\eta})},q\in\mathcal{M}
, each approximating
(P_{v})_{v\in S}
arbitrarily well at finite places and lying on the same connected of
f^{-1}(Q^{\sharp})(K_{v})
as
P_{v}
for real
v
, such that

\Delta^{(3)}(-,(P_{v}(q)))_{BM}=g

as elements of
\Fun(\{1,\ldots,M-1\}^{3},B_{tr}^{D})
.

Proof.

We enlarge
S
and
(P_{v})_{v\in S}
via Harari’s formal lemma and assume that the local family
(P_{v})_{v\in S}
is orthogonal

We assume that
S
is large enough and that
(P_{v})_{v\in S}
has been modified through Harari’s Formal lemma, and that the multi-section
Q\leftarrow Q^{L,n,(1)}\xrightarrow{s}X
has been refined as in Proposition
11.2
. We also assume that
S
has been enlarged and
s
refined as in Theorem
12.1
.

The map
\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)\xrightarrow{\bar{\partial}}H^{3}(Q\otimes_{K}\overline{K},\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\Gamma_{K}}
has kernel
\im\Br X_{\eta}
by assumption
(R_{3})
, and so induces an injection

\iota:B_{tr}\hookrightarrow H^{3}(Q\otimes_{K}\overline{K},\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\Gamma_{K}}.

Following the discussion of subsection
12.1
, the homomorphism

B_{tr}\rightarrow\Fun_{\Gamma}(\Gamma^{3}\setminus\Delta_{3},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(-2)),\quad b\mapsto a_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\gamma}(\iota(b))

is also injective, where
\Delta_{3}\subset\Gamma^{3}
denotes the multi-diagonal. Identifying the dual of
\Fun_{\Gamma}(\Gamma^{3}\setminus\Delta_{3},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(-2))
with
\Fun_{\Gamma}(\Gamma^{3}\setminus\Delta_{3},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2))
, the dual of the map above is

\begin{matrix}&\Fun_{\Gamma}(\Gamma^{3}\setminus\Delta_{3},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2))&\rightarrow&B_{tr}^{D},\\
&c(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})&\mapsto&\left(b\mapsto\sum a_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}(\iota(b))c(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})\right),\end{matrix}

which is thus surjective. Let
f:\{1,\ldots,M-1\}^{3}\rightarrow\Fun_{\Gamma}(\Gamma^{3}\setminus\Delta_{3},(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})(2))
be a lift of
g
through this map.

By Theorem
10.8
there exists an
(L,n)
-liftable grid

\mathcal{M}=\{q_{0}\}\cdot\{q_{1,1},\cdots,q_{1,M}\}\cdot\{q_{2,1},\cdots,q_{2,M}\}\cdot\{q_{3,1},\cdots,q_{3,M}\}\subset Q(K),

whose elements approximate the collection
(f(P_{v}))_{v\in S}
, and on which the Redéi symbols
[\partial p,(\partial q)^{\sigma_{1}},(\partial r)^{\sigma_{2}}](i,j,k)
are equal to
g^{\prime}(i,j,k)(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
. Then by Theorem
12.1
, for a suitable choices of adelic points
(P_{v}(q))\in X_{q}(\mathbb{A}_{K})
, the triple variation of the function

\mathcal{M}\rightarrow B_{tr}^{D},\ \ q\mapsto(b,(P_{v}(q)))_{BM}

is
g
. All the adelic points
(P_{v}(q))_{v}
approximate
P_{v},v\in S
by construction.
∎

To conclude the argument we use the following lemma (this is Lemma
1.5
from the introduction, but we also added an explicit expression for
M_{0}(A)
), a baby-case of work of Alexander Smith:

Lemma 13.3
.

Let
A
be a finite abelian group of cardinality
a
. For every natural number
M>k\cdot a\log a
, there exists
g:\{1,\ldots,M-1\}^{3}\rightarrow A
such that every
f:\{1,\ldots,M\}^{k}\rightarrow A
with
\Delta^{(k)}(f)=g
is surjective.

Proof.

Consider the operator

\Delta^{(k)}_{ns}:\Fun_{\text{non-surj}}(\{1,\ldots,M\}^{k},A)\rightarrow\Fun(\{1,\ldots,M-1\}^{k},A),\ f\mapsto\Delta^{(k)}(f),

where the domain denotes the set of non-surjective functions
\{1,\ldots,M\}^{k}\rightarrow A
.
A function
g
satisfies the desired condition in the statement if and only if it does not lie in the image of
\Delta^{(k)}_{ns}
. The cardinality of the domain is
\leq a\cdot(a-1)^{M^{k}}
, while that of the codomain is
a^{(M-1)^{k}}
. An exercise in calculus shows that
a^{(M-1)^{k}}>a\cdot(a-1)^{M^{k}}
when
M>k\cdot a\log a
, and so the sought
g
exists.
∎

Proof of Theorem
13.1
.

Let
g
be as in Smith’s lemma. By Corollary
13.2
, we may find a grid
\mathcal{M}
for which the triple variation of the function

\mathcal{M}\rightarrow B_{tr}^{D},\ \ q\mapsto(b,(P_{v}(q)))_{BM}

is equal to
g
. So, this function attains a
0
on some
q\in\mathcal{M}
by the lemma. The corresponding adelic point then lies in
X_{q}(\mathbb{A}_{K})^{\Br_{\text{hor}}(X/Q)}=X_{q}(\mathbb{A}_{K})^{\Br X_{q}}
, and approximates the point
(P_{v})_{v\in S}
as wished.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Combine Theorem
13.1
and Proposition
9.1
.
∎

Appendix A
Combining Chebotarev and Hecke equidistribution

Let
E
be a number field.
Recall that the reciprocity map
rec:C_{E}\rightarrow\Gamma_{E}^{ab}
identifies
\Gamma_{E}^{ab}
with the profinite completion of
C_{E}
, and via this map to every finite quotient
A
of
C_{E}
, we can associate a finite abelian extension
L/E
such that
\Gal(L/E)\cong A
, and the quotient map
rec_{L/E}:C_{E}\rightarrow\Gal(L/E)
is the Artin reciprocity map for
L/E
.

For a prime
\mathcal{P}
of
\mathcal{O}_{E}
, we denote by
[\mathcal{P}]
an idele
(i_{v})_{v}\in\mathbb{I}_{E}
such that
i_{v}\in E_{v}^{*}
is a local uniformizer when
v
is the valuation defined by
\mathcal{P}
, and such that
i_{v}=1
for all other
v
. (
[\mathcal{P}]
is not uniquely defined, but this ambiguity is irrelevant for our purposes.)

Theorem A.1

(Chebotarev-Hecke density)
.

Let
L/E
be a finite Galois extension with group
G
, and let
L\cap E^{ab}
be its maximal abelian subextension.
Let
q:C_{E}\rightarrow B
be a compact quotient. Assume that
B
is a real Lie group,
and that the abelian extension corresponding to the projection
C_{E}\rightarrow\pi_{0}(B)
is
L\cap E^{ab}
. Let
\mu
be the Haar measure of
B
normalized so that its connected components have mass
1
. Let
c
be a conjugacy class in
\Gal(L/E)
, and
U\subseteq B
be a non-empty open that lies entirely in the connected component of
B
corresponding to the projection of
c
in
\pi_{0}(B)=\Gal(L\cap E^{ab}/E)
. Then the primes
\mathcal{P}
of
E
such that
q([\mathcal{P}])
lies in
U
and their Frobenius class in
\Gal(L/E)
is
c
have density
\frac{|c|}{|G|}\cdot\mu(U)
when ordered by norm.

For a subset
W
of
\Spec\mathcal{O}_{E}
, recall that its
density
is the limit (if it exists):

\delta_{E}(W)\coloneqq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\text{ Number of }\mathfrak{p}\in W\text{ with }\mathbf{N}p\leqq n}{\text{ Number of }\mathfrak{p}\in\Spec\mathcal{O}_{E}\text{ with }\mathbf{N}\mathfrak{p}\leqq n}.

Equivalently, by Landau’s prime number theorem, it is equal to the limit:

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\text{ Number of }\mathfrak{p}\in W\text{ with }\mathbf{N}p\leqq n}{n/\log n}.

Proof.

We follow the lines of a simple argument due to Duering as presented in
[
30
, p.169]
, but using
[
30
, p.317, Theorem 6]
(which combines Dirichlet and Hecke equidistribution) instead of Dirichlet’s theorem.
When
L/E
is abelian, the statement follows directly from
[
30
, p.317, Theorem 6]
.

We go to the general case. Let
\Delta_{L/E}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{E}
be the discriminant of the extension
L/E
. Let
\sigma
be a representative of
c
,
f
be its order and
Z=L^{\sigma}
. The extension
L/Z
is cyclic of group
\Gal(L/Z)\cong\langle\sigma\rangle
, and so induces a character
\chi:C_{Z}\rightarrow\Gal(L/Z)\cong\mathbb{Z}/f\mathbb{Z}
.

Let
W
be the set of primes
\mathcal{P}
as in the statement. Define

W_{Z}:=\left\{\mathfrak{p}\in\Spec\mathcal{O}_{Z}:\begin{gathered}\mathfrak{p}\text{ has degree $1$ over }E,\ (\mathfrak{p},\Delta_{L/E})=1,\\
\Frob_{\mathfrak{p}}(L/Z)=\sigma,\ q([\mathfrak{p}\cap E])\in U\end{gathered}\right\}.

The map
\mathfrak{p}\mapsto\mathcal{P}=\mathfrak{p}\cap E
defines a finite-to-one map
W_{Z}\rightarrow W
. The fibers of this map have constant cardinality
\frac{|G|}{|c|f}
. In fact, for a fixed prime
\mathcal{P}
of
E
of Frobenius class
c
, the number of primes
\beta
above it in
L
is
|G|/|G_{\beta}|=|G|/f
, where
G_{\beta}
denotes the decomposition group of
\beta
.
The Frobenii elements
\Frob_{\beta}(L/K),\,\beta|\mathcal{P}
are uniformely distributed in the conjugacy class
c
, so exactly a proportion
1/|c|
of the primes
\beta
have
\Frob_{\beta}(L/E)=\sigma
. I.e. there are
|G|/(|c|f)
such
\beta
. The association
\beta\mapsto\mathfrak{p}=\beta\cap E
gives a bijection between these
\beta
and the
\mathfrak{p}
in the fiber of
W_{Z}\rightarrow W
above
\mathcal{P}
, showing that the fibers have the desired cardinality.

Note that
\mathbf{N}(\mathfrak{p})=\mathbf{N}(\mathcal{P})
for
\mathfrak{p}\in W_{Z}
. Thus
\delta_{E}(W)=\frac{|c|f}{|G|}\delta_{Z}(W_{Z})
. We now compute
\delta_{Z}(W_{Z})
.
The quotient map
q:C_{E}\rightarrow B
induces by pullback a homomorphism
q_{Z}:C_{Z}\xrightarrow{N_{Z/E}}C_{E}\rightarrow B
. The cokernel of
C_{Z}\xrightarrow{N_{Z/E}}C_{E}
is isomorphic to
\Gal(Z\cap E^{ab}/E)
by the norm limitation theorem, and is thus finite.
Hence the image of the homomorphism
q^{\prime}_{Z}:C_{Z}\xrightarrow{(q_{Z},(-,L/Z))}B\times_{\pi_{0}(B)}\Gal(L/Z)
is a subgroup of finite index of the real Lie group
B\times_{\pi_{0}(B)}\Gal(L/Z)
and so contains its connected component of the identity. Moreover, the image of
q^{\prime}_{Z}
surjects onto the second factor
\Gal(L/Z)
, which parametrizes the connected components of the fibered product
B\times_{\pi_{0}(B)}\Gal(L/Z)
, and we deduce that
q^{\prime}_{Z}
is surjective.

The condition
q([\mathfrak{p}\cap E])\in U
in the definition on
W_{Z}
is equivalent to
q_{Z}([\mathfrak{p}])\in U
(since
N_{Z/E}([\mathfrak{p}])=[\mathfrak{p}\cap E]
as
\mathfrak{p}
has degree
1
over
E
). Since only the primes that have degree
1
over
\mathbb{Q}
contribute to the density, the condition “
\mathfrak{p}\text{ has degree $1$ over }E
” may be removed when computing the density of
W_{Z}
, and this density becomes the density of
\mathfrak{p}
such that
q_{Z}([\mathfrak{p}])\in U
and
\Frob_{\mathfrak{p}}(L/Z)=\sigma
. This is
\frac{1}{f}\mu(U)
by the abelian (or even cyclic) case applied to
q^{\prime}_{Z}
, which yields the statement.
∎

In the cyclic strong approximation Theorem
10.9
, we use the following consequence of Chebotarev-Hecke density about approximating
v
-adic numbers,
v\in S
with prime elements of
\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
. Recall that the
direction
of a non-zero element of a real vector space
V
is its image in
(V\setminus\{0\})/\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

Theorem A.2

(Theorem
10.1
)
.

Let
L/E
be a Galois field extension, and
S
a finite set of places of
E
containing the archimedean ones and those where
L/E
ramifies. Let, for each place
v\in S
,
x_{v}\in E_{v}^{*}
. Assume that the idele
(\tilde{x}_{v})_{v}\in\mathbb{I}_{E},\tilde{x}_{v}\coloneqq x_{v}
if
v\in S
and
\tilde{x}_{v}\coloneqq 1
if
v\notin S
, is in the kernel of the reciprocity map

rec_{L\cap E^{ab}/E}:\mathbb{I}_{E}\rightarrow C_{E}\rightarrow\Gal(L\cap E^{ab}/E)

associated to the maximal abelian subextension
L\cap E^{ab}/E
of
L/E
. Let
c\subset\Gal(L/E)
be a conjugacy class which projects to the identity of
\Gal(L\cap E^{ab}/E)
.
Then there exist prime elements
x\in\mathcal{O}_{E,S}
whose Frobenius class in
\Gal(L/E)
is
c
, approximate
x_{v}
arbitrarily well for all finite
v\in S
, and whose direction in the real vector space
E\otimes\mathbb{R}
approximates the direction of
(x_{v})_{v\in M_{E}^{\infty}}\in E\otimes\mathbb{R}
arbitrarily well.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{m}
be the conductor of the abelian extension
L\cap E^{ab}/E
. Recall that this is the minimal modulus of
E
such that
L\cap E^{ab}
is contained in the ray class field
E_{\mathfrak{m}}
associated to
\mathfrak{m}
. Let
F
be the compositum
LE_{\mathfrak{m}}
. By Galois theory, we may identify
\Gal(F/E)
with the group of pairs
(\gamma,\delta)\in\Gal(E_{\mathfrak{m}}/E)\times\Gal(L/E)
such that the images of
\gamma
and
\delta
in
\Gal(L\cap E_{\mathfrak{m}}/E)=\Gal(L\cap E^{ab}/E)
coincide. Recall (see e.g. the sentence after
[
21
, Definition 15.22]
) that the reciprocity map
rec_{E_{\mathfrak{m}}/E}
gives an identification

\Gal(E_{\mathfrak{m}}/E)=E^{*}\backslash\mathbb{I}_{E}/U_{\mathfrak{m}}.

Let
\gamma\in\Gal(E_{\mathfrak{m}}/E)
be the image of
(\tilde{x}_{v})_{v}
in this quotient. Note that
c^{\prime}\coloneqq\{\gamma\}\times c
is a conjugacy class in
\Gal(F/E)
.

Let
U^{0}_{\mathfrak{m}}
be the subgroup of
U_{\mathfrak{m}}\coloneqq\prod_{v}U_{v}
(recall the notation from Section
2
) of ideles with archimedean components equal to
1
.
We apply Chebotarev-Hecke density (Theorem
A.1
) to

\pi:C_{E}\rightarrow C_{E}/U^{0}_{\mathfrak{m}}\mathbb{R}_{>0}=E^{*}\backslash\mathbb{I}_{E}/U^{0}_{\mathfrak{m}}\mathbb{R}_{>0}

(note that this is a compact real Lie group) and the Galois extension
F/E
to infer that there are primes
\mathcal{P}
of
E
with Frobenius class
c^{\prime}
in
\Gal(F/E)
such that
[\mathcal{P}]\in E^{*}\backslash\mathbb{I}_{E}/U_{\mathfrak{m}}\mathbb{R}_{>0}
approximates the image of
(\tilde{x}_{v})_{v}
. The compatibility assumption of Theorem
A.1
here is satisfied because
c^{\prime}
and
(\tilde{x}_{v})_{v}
map to the same element of

\Gal(E_{\mathfrak{m}}/E)=E^{*}\backslash\mathbb{I}_{E}/U_{\mathfrak{m}}=\pi_{0}(E^{*}\backslash\mathbb{I}_{E}/U^{0}_{\mathfrak{m}}\mathbb{R}_{>0}).

by construction of
c^{\prime}
. (See the sentence after
[
21
, Definition 15.22]
for the first identity.)

By definition of quotient topology there exists then
x\cdot\mathfrak{a}\cdot t\in E^{*}U^{0}_{\mathfrak{m}}\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
[\mathcal{P}]\cdot x^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{a}^{-1}\cdot t^{-1}\in\mathbb{I}_{E}
approximates
(\tilde{x}_{v})_{v}
. For such
[\mathcal{P}],x,\mathfrak{a},t
, we have
x\cdot\mathcal{O}_{E,S}=\mathcal{P}
,
x\cdot x_{v}^{-1}
is congruent to
1\bmod\mathfrak{m}
for every finite
v\in S
, and
tx
approximates
(x_{v})_{v\in M_{E}^{\infty}}\in\prod_{v\in M_{E}^{\infty}}E_{v}^{*}
. The last condition shows that
x
approximates the direction of
(x_{v})_{v\in M_{E}^{\infty}}
as wished. Refining
\mathfrak{m}
,
x
approximates
x_{v}
at
S
arbitrarily well, completing the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Relative cohomology

Relative (étale) cohomology was defined by Friedlander in
[
12
, Ch. 14]
in the general context of simplicial schemes. In this appendix we give an alternative definition that avoids the use of simplicial schemes. At the end of this appendix we discuss how to represent relative étale cohomology classes via Čech cocycles in an analog way as one does for usual (non-relative) cohomology.

Definition B.1
.

A
relative abelian (pre)sheaf
of a morphism of schemes
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a triple
(F,G,\alpha)
, with
F\in\operatorname{(P)Sh}_{Y},\,G\in\operatorname{(P)Sh}_{X},\,\alpha:F\rightarrow f_{*}G
.

Notation
.

We denote by
\operatorname{(P)Sh}_{X/Y}
the category of relative abelian (pre)sheaves of
f
.
For
(F,G,\alpha)\in\Sh_{X/Y}
, we denote by
\alpha^{\dagger}:f^{-1}F\rightarrow G
the left adjunct of
\alpha
.

The category
\operatorname{(P)Sh}_{X/Y}
is the comma category
\left(\operatorname{Id}_{\operatorname{(P)Sh}(Y)}\downarrow f_{*}\right)
. The comma category
(U\downarrow V)
of a diagram of functors
\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{U}\mathcal{C}\xleftarrow{V}\mathcal{B}
is the category whose objets are triples
(A\in\mathscr{A},B\in\mathscr{B},\vartheta:UA\rightarrow VB)

[
33
, II.6]
. Comma categories are abelian if
U
is right exact and
V
is left exact
[
1
, Prop. 5.1]
, so in particular
\Sh_{X/Y}
is abelian. (One may also easily prove this abelianity directly.)

The following lemma proves, in particular, that
\Sh_{X/Y}
and
\Psh_{X/Y}
have enough injectives:

Lemma B.2
.

Let
V:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
be a left exact functor between abelian categories, and
\mathfrak{C}:=(\text{id}\downarrow V)
be the comma category of
\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\text{id}}\mathcal{A}\xleftarrow{V}\mathcal{B}
. The injectives objects of
\mathfrak{C}
are those of the form
(I\oplus V(J),J,I\oplus V(J)\rightarrow V(J))
, with
I
and
J
injective in
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
, respectively. Moreover, if
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
have enough injectives, then
\mathfrak{C}
does too.

Proof.

See
[
11
, p.16]
. ∎

We give the following definition, borrowing Friedlander’s notation.

Definition B.3

(Relative cohomology)
.

Let
H^{k}_{X}(Y,-),\,k\geq 0
be the
k
-th derived functor of

\Gamma_{Y,X}:\Sh_{X/Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}b,\ \ (F,G,\alpha)\mapsto\Ker(\alpha:F(Y)\rightarrow G(X)).

Below, we shall argue that, for
M^{\bullet}=(F^{\bullet},G^{\bullet},\alpha)\in\mathcal{D}^{+}(\Sh_{X/Y})
, the total derived functor
R\Gamma_{Y,X}(M^{\bullet})
is, in a sense, naturally isomorphic to the cone of the natural transformation
R\Gamma_{Y}(F^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\alpha}R\Gamma_{X}(G^{\bullet})
. A slight problem arises: to give precise statements we need to talk about cones in derived categories, which notoriously cannot be defined functorially in general
[
45
, Prop. 1.2.13]
. In the following paragraph, we present a way to circumvent this lack of functoriality by defining the cone functor on the derived category of the morphism category instead of on the morphism category of the derived category.
5
5

5

Other ways would be to pass to dg-enhancements of the categories in question or use the theory of derivators, but for the sake of self-containement of the paper I preferred to avoid both.

Derived category of the morphism category.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category, and
\Mor(\mathcal{A})
be its category of morphisms. This is still an abelian category
[
17
]
.

Since
\Ch(\Mor(\mathcal{A}))=\Mor(\Ch(\mathcal{A}))
, we shall represent elements of
\Ch(\Mor(\mathcal{A}))
as morphisms
A^{\bullet}\rightarrow B^{\bullet}
of chain complexes. (The same identity does not pass to the derived category! Namely,
\mathcal{D}(\Mor(\mathcal{A}))
and
\Mor(\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}))
are different categories in general: in view of
[
45
, Prop. 1.2.13]
, this follows for instance from the proposition below.)

Proposition B.4
.

There is a functor

\Cone(-):\mathcal{D}(\Mor(\mathcal{A}))\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}),\

and natural exact triangles

A^{\bullet}\rightarrow B^{\bullet}\rightarrow\Cone(A^{\bullet}\rightarrow B^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{+1}\ \ \text{ in }\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})

for
(A^{\bullet}\rightarrow B^{\bullet})\in\mathcal{D}(\Mor(\mathcal{A}))
.

Proof.

The usual mapping cone functor
\Ch(\Mor(\mathcal{A}))\rightarrow\Ch(\mathcal{A}),\,(A^{\bullet}\rightarrow B^{\bullet})\mapsto\linebreak\Cone(A^{\bullet}\rightarrow B^{\bullet})
defined on the category of chain complexes sends a quasi-isomorphism
(A_{1}^{\bullet}\rightarrow B_{1}^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\text{q.i.}}(A_{2}^{\bullet}\rightarrow B_{2}^{\bullet})
to a quasi-isomorphism of cones
\Cone(A_{1}^{\bullet}\rightarrow B_{1}^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\text{q.i.}}\Cone(A_{2}^{\bullet}\rightarrow B_{2}^{\bullet})
, and so passes to the derived category.
∎

We observe that, if
\mathcal{A}
has enough injectives, then
\Mor(\mathcal{A})
does as well, as follows from Lemma
B.2
, since the morphism category is just the comma category
(\text{id}\downarrow\text{id})
. So the statement of the following lemma is well-posed.

Lemma B.5
.

The functor
\Ker:\Mor(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
sending a morphism to its kernel is left exact. Moreover, if
\mathcal{A}
has enough injectives, then the total right derived functor
R\Ker:\mathcal{D}^{+}(\Mor(\mathcal{A}))\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathcal{A})
is the shifted cone
\Cone(-)[-1]
.

Proof.

The first part is clear from the snake lemma.
For the second part, let
\mathcal{I}\subset\Mor(\mathcal{A})
be the full subcategory of injective objects of
\Mor(\mathcal{A})
. By Lemma
B.2
, any morphism
A\rightarrow B
that is injective as an object of
\Mor(\mathcal{A})
is an epimorphism. In particular,
A^{n}\rightarrow B^{n}
is surjective for all
n
and all
(A^{\bullet}\rightarrow B^{\bullet})\in\Ch(\mathcal{I})
.

We denote by
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{I})\subset\mathcal{D}(\Mor(\mathcal{A}))
the full subcategory of complexes with entries in
\mathcal{I}
. We have
R\Ker((A^{\bullet}\rightarrow B^{\bullet}))=\Ker(A^{\bullet}\rightarrow B^{\bullet})
for
(A^{\bullet}\rightarrow B^{\bullet})\in\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathcal{I})
. By the surjectivity of the maps
A^{n}\rightarrow B^{n}
, the natural chain map

\Ker(A^{\bullet}\rightarrow B^{\bullet})\rightarrow\Cone(A^{\bullet}\rightarrow B^{\bullet})[-1],

defined degree-wise as the composition
\Ker(A^{n}\rightarrow B^{n})\rightarrow A^{n}\hookrightarrow A^{n}\oplus B^{n-1}
, is a quasi-isomorphism. So
R\Ker\cong\Cone(-)[-1]
on the full subcategory
\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathcal{I})\subset\mathcal{D}^{+}(\Mor(\mathcal{A}))
. Since
\Mor(\mathcal{A})
has enough injectives, the inclusion
\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathcal{I})\subset\mathcal{D}^{+}(\Mor(\mathcal{A}))
is essentially surjective, and so the two functors are naturally isomorphic on the whole
\mathcal{D}^{+}(\Mor(\mathcal{A}))
.
∎

A long exact sequence.
We express the functor
\Gamma_{Y,X}
as the composition

\displaystyle\Gamma_{Y,X}:\Sh_{X/Y}\xrightarrow{\Gamma_{X/Y}}\Mor(\mathcal{A}b)\xrightarrow{\Ker}\mathcal{A}b,\quad\text{where}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{X/Y}(F,G,\alpha):=(F(Y)\xrightarrow{\alpha(Y)}G(X)).

The functor
\Gamma_{X/Y}
is left exact and has an exact left adjoint
\Mor(\mathcal{A}b)\rightarrow\Sh_{X/Y}
sending a morphism
A\xrightarrow{\alpha}B
to the constant relative sheaf
(A_{Y},B_{X},A_{Y}\rightarrow f_{*}B_{X})
, where
A_{Y}
and
B_{X}
denote the constant sheaves associated to
A
and
B
on
X
and
Y
, and the morphism
A_{Y}\rightarrow f_{*}B_{X}
is adjoint to the morphism
f^{-1}A_{Y}=A_{X}\xrightarrow{\alpha}B_{X}
. In particular,
\Gamma_{X/Y}
sends injectives to injectives, so Grothendieck’s theorem on the composition of derived functors gives
R\Gamma_{Y,X}=\Cone[-1]\circ R\Gamma_{X/Y}
.

For a diagram
\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\text{id}}\mathcal{A}\xleftarrow{V}\mathcal{B}
with
V
left exact, we denote by
pr_{1}:(\text{id}\downarrow V)\rightarrow\mathcal{A},\,pr_{2}:(\text{id}\downarrow V)\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
the two “projection” functors
(A,B,\vartheta)\mapsto A
and
(A,B,\vartheta)\mapsto B
. These are exact functors, so
R(pr_{i})=pr_{i}
for
i=1,2
. Moreover, if
V
sends injectives to injectives then they also both send injectives to injectives by Lemma
B.2
(if
V
does not send injectives to injectives, then only the second projection does).

By these general observations on projections in comma categories, we get
pr_{1}\circ R\Gamma_{X/Y}\cong R(pr_{1}\circ\Gamma_{X/Y})\cong R(\Gamma_{Y}\circ pr_{1})\cong R\Gamma_{Y}\circ pr_{1}
. So
pr_{1}\circ R\Gamma_{X/Y}\cong R\Gamma_{Y}\circ pr_{1}
and, analogously,
pr_{2}\circ R\Gamma_{X/Y}\cong R\Gamma_{X}\circ pr_{2}
.

For
M^{\bullet}=(F^{\bullet},G^{\bullet},\alpha^{\bullet})\in\mathcal{D}^{+}(\Sh_{X/Y})
, we may consider
R\Gamma_{X/Y}(M^{\bullet})\in\mathcal{D}^{+}(\Mor(\mathcal{A}b))
. The discussion above gives natural derived isomorphisms

(B.1)

pr_{1}(R\Gamma_{X/Y}(M^{\bullet}))\cong R\Gamma_{Y}(F^{\bullet}),\,pr_{2}(R\Gamma_{X/Y}(M^{\bullet}))\cong R\Gamma_{X}(G^{\bullet}),

and the image of
R\Gamma_{X/Y}(M^{\bullet})\in\mathcal{D}^{+}(\Mor(\mathcal{A}b))
in
\Mor(\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathcal{A}b))
thus defines a map
R\Gamma_{Y}(F^{\bullet})\rightarrow R\Gamma_{X}(G^{\bullet})
. We already have a natural map between the two: the composition
R\Gamma_{Y}(F^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{f^{*}}R\Gamma_{X}(f^{-1}F^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\dagger}}R\Gamma_{X}(G^{\bullet}).

As one would expect, these two coincide:

Lemma
.

The image under
\mathcal{D}^{+}(\Mor(\mathcal{A}b))\rightarrow\Mor(\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathcal{A}b))
of
R\Gamma_{X/Y}(M^{\bullet})
corresponds to the composition
R\Gamma_{Y}(F^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{f^{*}}R\Gamma_{X}(f^{-1}F^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\dagger}}R\Gamma_{X}(G^{\bullet})
under the natural isomorphisms (
B.1
).

Proof.

The statement is equivalent to the commutativity of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr_{1}\circ R\Gamma_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr_{2}\circ R\Gamma_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma_{Y}\circ pr_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma_{X}\circ f^{-1}\circ pr_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma_{X}\circ pr_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\circ pr_{1}}

of natural transformations
\mathcal{D}^{+}(\Sh_{X/Y})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathcal{A}b)
(the upper row specializes to
[R\alpha^{\bullet}]
and the lower row to
\alpha^{\dagger}\circ f^{*}
). But this commutes as it is the “derived” of the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr_{1}\circ\Gamma_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr_{2}\circ\Gamma_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{Y}\circ pr_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{X}\circ f^{-1}\circ pr_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{X}\circ pr_{2}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\circ pr_{1}}

∎

The lemma above gives a natural triangle

R\Gamma_{Y}(F^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\dagger}\circ f^{*}}R\Gamma_{X}(F^{\bullet})\rightarrow\Cone(R\Gamma_{X/Y}(M^{\bullet}))\xrightarrow{+1},

and, after shifting:

R\Gamma_{Y,X}(M^{\bullet})\rightarrow R\Gamma_{Y}(F^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\dagger}\circ f^{*}}R\Gamma_{X}(F^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{+1},

Specializing at
M=(F,G,\alpha)\in\Sh_{X/Y}
and taking cohomology, we get a natural long exact sequence:

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow

\displaystyle H_{X}^{0}(Y,M)\rightarrow H^{0}(Y,F)\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\dagger}\circ f^{*}}H^{0}(X,G)

(B.2)

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle H_{X}^{1}(Y,M)\rightarrow H^{1}(Y,F)\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\dagger}\circ f^{*}}H^{1}(X,G)\rightarrow\cdots.

Proposition B.6
.

When
F=f_{*}G
, we have natural isomorphisms

(B.3)

H_{X}^{k}(Y,M)\cong H^{k-1}(Y,\tau_{\geq 1}Rf_{*}G)

for all
k\geq 0
(with the convention that
H^{-1}(Y,\tau_{\geq 1}Rf_{*}G)=0
).

Proof.

We first express the functor
\Gamma_{Y,X}
as the composition

\displaystyle\Gamma_{Y,X}:\Sh_{X/Y}\xrightarrow{p}\Mor(\Sh_{Y})\xrightarrow{\Ker}\Sh_{Y}\xrightarrow{\Gamma_{Y}}\mathcal{A}b,

where
{p}((F,G,\alpha:F\rightarrow f_{*}G)):=\alpha
. All functors appearing are left-exact, so
R\Gamma_{Y,X}\cong R\Gamma_{Y}\circ\Cone\circ R{p}
, and we get a natural isomorphism

R\Gamma_{Y,X}(M^{\bullet})\cong R\Gamma_{Y}(\Cone(R{p}(M^{\bullet})))

for
M^{\bullet}\in\mathcal{D}^{+}(\Sh_{X/Y})
.

Analogously as in our discussion on
R\Gamma_{X/Y}
, we have natural derived isomorphisms
pr_{1}(R{p}(M^{\bullet}))\cong F^{\bullet}
and
pr_{2}(R{p}(M^{\bullet}))\cong Rf_{*}(G^{\bullet})
. In this case, the image of
[R{p}(M^{\bullet})]\in\mathcal{D}^{+}(\Mor(\Sh_{Y}))
in
\Mor(\mathcal{D}^{+}(\Sh_{Y}))
is the composition
F^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\bullet}}f_{*}G^{\bullet}\rightarrow Rf_{*}G^{\bullet}
.
So we get a natural triangle

F^{\bullet}\rightarrow Rf_{*}G^{\bullet}\rightarrow\Cone(R{p}(M^{\bullet}))\xrightarrow{+1}

in
\mathcal{D}^{+}(\Sh_{Y})
. Specializing at
M=(f_{*}G,G,id)
(with everything supported in degree
0
), this triangle becomes

f_{*}G\rightarrow Rf_{*}G\rightarrow\Cone(R{p}(M))\xrightarrow{+1}.

Comparing it with the triangle
f_{*}G\rightarrow Rf_{*}G\rightarrow\tau_{\geq 1}Rf_{*}G\xrightarrow{+1}
, we get an isomorphism

\Cone(R{p}(M))\cong\tau_{\geq 1}Rf_{*}G.

Because
\tau_{\geq 1}Rf_{*}G
is supported in degree
\geq 1
and
f_{*}G
is supported in degree
0
, we have
\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(f_{*}G,\tau_{\geq 1}Rf_{*}G[-1])=0
, so this isomorphism is uniquely defined
[
14
, IV.1.5]
and thus natural.
Thus we get natural isomorphisms

R\Gamma_{Y,X}(M)\cong R\Gamma_{Y}(\tau_{\geq 1}Rf_{*}G)[1],

and taking cohomology we get (
B.3
).
∎

Relative coverings and relative Čech cochains.

Definition B.7
.

A
relative covering
of a morphism of schemes
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a triple
(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U},\psi)
, where
\mathcal{U}
(resp.
\mathcal{V}
) is an étale covering of
Y
(resp.
X
), and
\psi
is a refinement
\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}\times_{Y}X
, where we view
\mathcal{U}\times_{Y}X
as a covering of
Y
.

For a morphism
f
, we define the
relative Čech complex
of a relative sheaf
(F,G,\alpha)\in\Sh_{X/Y}
with respect to a relative covering
(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U},\psi)
as

(B.4)

\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U};F,G)\coloneqq\Cone(\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{U}/Y,F)\rightarrow\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V}/X,G)),

where
\Cone
denotes the
mapping cone
in the category of chain complexes, and the map
\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{U},F)\rightarrow\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V},G)
is
\psi^{*}\circ\alpha^{\dagger}\circ f^{*}
(this is just the natural pullback). We denote the cohomology of this complex by
\check{H}^{k}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U};F,G),k\geq 0
. Taking hypercohomology of the exact triangle

\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{U}/Y,F)\rightarrow\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V}/X,G)\rightarrow\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U};F,G)\xrightarrow{+1},

we obtain a natural long exact sequence

(B.5)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\rightarrow\check{H}^{0}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U};F,G)\rightarrow\check{H}^{0}(\mathcal{U}/Y,F)\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\dagger}\circ\psi^{*}\circ f^{*}}\check{H}^{0}(\mathcal{V}/X,G)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\check{H}^{1}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U};F,G)\rightarrow\check{H}^{1}(\mathcal{U}/Y,F)\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\dagger}\circ\psi^{*}\circ f^{*}}\check{H}^{1}(\mathcal{V}/X,G)\rightarrow\cdots.

A
refinement
map between two relative coverings
(\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{U}_{1},\psi_{1})
and
(\mathcal{V}_{2},\mathcal{U}_{2},\psi_{2})
of a morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a pair
\vartheta=(\vartheta_{X},\vartheta_{Y})
of refinements
\vartheta_{X}:\mathcal{V}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{2},\,\vartheta_{Y}:\mathcal{U}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}_{2}
commuting with
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
as in the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}_{1}\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}_{2}\times_{Y}X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{Y}\times_{Y}X}

Clearly, a refinement
\vartheta
induces by pullback a refinement map
\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V}_{2},\mathcal{U}_{2};F,G)\xrightarrow{\vartheta^{*}}\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{U}_{1};F,G)
on Čech complexes, which in turn induces a refinement map on cohomology.
The following lemma shows that, just as for the non-relative case, the pullback map induced on relative cohomology among two relative coverings do not depend on the choice of refinement.

Lemma B.8
.

Let
\vartheta,\vartheta^{\prime}:(\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{U}_{1},\psi_{1})\rightarrow(\mathcal{V}_{2},\mathcal{U}_{2},\psi_{2})
be two refinements between relative coverings of a morphism
f
. The pullbacks
\vartheta^{*},(\vartheta^{\prime})^{*}:\check{H}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U};F,G)\rightarrow\check{H}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V}^{\prime},\mathcal{U}^{\prime};F,G)
coincide.

Proof.

Write
\vartheta=(\vartheta_{X},\vartheta_{Y}),\,\vartheta^{\prime}=(\vartheta^{\prime}_{X},\vartheta^{\prime}_{Y}).
From the well-known analogue statement in the usual “non-relative” setting (see (
2.1
)), we have homotopies
K_{\vartheta_{X},\vartheta^{\prime}_{X}}:{\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{U}/Y,F)}\rightarrow{\check{C}^{\bullet-1}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime}/Y,F)}
and
K_{\vartheta_{Y},\vartheta^{\prime}_{Y}}:{\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V}/X,G)}\rightarrow{\check{C}^{\bullet-1}(\mathcal{V}^{\prime}/X,G)}
satisfying

\d K_{\vartheta_{X},\vartheta^{\prime}_{X}}+K_{\vartheta_{X},\vartheta^{\prime}_{X}}\d=\vartheta_{2,Y}^{*}-\vartheta_{1,Y}^{*},\quad\d K_{\vartheta_{Y},\vartheta^{\prime}_{Y}}+K_{\vartheta_{Y},\vartheta^{\prime}_{Y}}\d=\vartheta_{2,X}^{*}-\vartheta_{1,X}^{*}.

By the naturality of the homotopies
K_{\star,\star}
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{U}/Y,F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V}/X,G)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\check{C}^{\bullet-1}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\check{C}^{\bullet-1}(\mathcal{V}^{\prime}/X,G)}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{\vartheta_{X},\vartheta^{\prime}_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{*}\circ f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{\vartheta_{Y},\vartheta^{\prime}_{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{*}\circ f^{*}}

commutes. Taking cones of the upper and lower rows, the pair
(K_{\vartheta_{X},\vartheta^{\prime}_{X}},K_{\vartheta_{Y},\vartheta^{\prime}_{Y}})
defines a chain map
K:C^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U};F,G)\rightarrow C^{\bullet-1}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U};F,G)
, and by construction
\d K+K\d=\vartheta^{*}-(\vartheta^{\prime})^{*}
.
∎

Just as in the non-relative case, we have maps from relative Čech cohomology to relative étale cohomology:

Proposition B.9
.

For
M=(F,G,\alpha)\in\Sh_{X/Y}
, there exist natural maps

\check{H}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U};F,G)\rightarrow H^{\bullet}_{X}(Y;F,G).

For fixed
X,Y,\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}
, these form a natural transformation of
\delta
-functors. Moreover, these morphisms are compatible with the long exact sequences (
B.2
) and (
B.5
).

Before proving the proposition, let us recall how these maps are constructed in the non-relative setting. For an étale covering
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\rightarrow Y\}_{i\in I}
of a scheme
Y
, we may factor the functor
\Gamma_{Y}:\Sh_{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}b
as

\Sh_{Y}\xhookrightarrow{\iota}\Psh_{Y}\xrightarrow{\check{H}^{0}(\mathcal{U},-)}\mathcal{A}b,

with
\check{H}^{0}(\mathcal{U},P):=\Ker\left(\prod_{i\in I}P(U_{i})\rightrightarrows\prod_{i,j}P(U_{i}\times_{Y}U_{j})\right)
. Since
\iota
sends injectives to injectives, we have
R\Gamma_{Y}=R\check{H}^{0}(\mathcal{U},-)\circ R\iota
. The derived functor
R\check{H}^{0}(\mathcal{U},-)
is the functor
\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{U},-)
associating to a complex its total Čech complex
[
34
, Ch. III.§2]
,
and so
R\Gamma_{Y}=\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{U},-)\circ R\iota
. The natural transformation
\iota\rightarrow R\iota
then defines a natural transformation
\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{U},-)\circ\iota\rightarrow R\Gamma_{Y}
of functors
\Ch^{+}(\Sh_{Y})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathcal{A}b)
. Equivalently, it gives natural derived maps

\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{U},F^{\bullet})\rightarrow R\Gamma_{Y}(F^{\bullet})

for
(F^{\bullet})\in\Ch^{+}(\Sh_{Y})
. Taking cohomology and specializing to the case where
F^{\bullet}=F
is supported in degree
0
, we get natural maps:

\check{H}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{U},F)\rightarrow H^{\bullet}(Y,F).

Proof of Proposition
B.9
.

Just as the derived functor
R\check{H}^{0}(\mathcal{U},-)
is the functor
\check{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{U},-)
associating to a complex its total Čech complex, the content of
[
34
, Ch. III.§2]
also shows that the derived functor
R\check{H}^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};-)
is the functor associating to a complex of relative sheaves its total relative Čech complex defined via (
B.4
).

The same argument as for the non-relative case works when applied to the composition

\Sh_{X/Y}\xhookrightarrow{\iota}\Psh_{X/Y}\xrightarrow{\check{H}^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};-)}\Mor(\mathcal{A}b),

with
\check{H}^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};F,G,\alpha):=(\check{H}^{0}(\mathcal{U},F)\xrightarrow{\check{\alpha}}\check{H^{0}}(\mathcal{V},G)),\,\check{\alpha}:=\alpha^{\dagger}\circ\psi^{*}\circ f^{*}
, using that the derived functor
R\check{H}^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};-)
is the functor associating to a complex of relative sheaves its total relative Čech complex defined via (
B.4
).
∎

Appendix C
Nakaoka’s theorem

The following theorem is essentially due to Nakaoka
[
35
]
(see also Leary’s subsequent work
[
31
, Theorem 2.1]
), but we present it with a slightly modified statement, with profinite groups instead of standard ones, and with more general coefficients. We give a simple full proof below, freely adapted from the original argument of Nakaoka.

Theorem C.1

(Nakaoka)
.

Let
G
be a profinite group, and
R
be a constant commutative ring. Let
Y
be a finite set on which
G
acts continuously by permutations, and
H
be a profinite group such that
H^{\bullet}(H,R)
is
R
-projective. Then for any
R[G]
-module
M
that is flat over
R
, the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence

H^{p}(G,H^{q}(H^{Y},M))\Rightarrow H^{p+q}(H^{Y}\rtimes G,M)

degenerates at the second page and the additive extensions in the reconstruction of the cohomology from the
E_{\infty}
-page are all trivial. Moreover, for fixed
H
and
R
, the additive extensions can be reconstructed in a way that is natural in
Y,G
and
M
.

Lemma C.2
.

Let
H\rtimes G
be a semi-direct product of profinite groups, and
M
be a
G
-module. Let
M\rightarrow M^{\bullet}
be a resolution in acyclic
H
-modules (where we are endowing
M
with the trivial
H
-action) that is endowed with a
G
-action. Assume further that, for all
p\geq 0
, the surjection

Z^{p}((M^{\bullet})^{H})\rightarrow H^{p}((M^{\bullet})^{H})=H^{p}(H,M)

splits as a map of
G
-modules. Then the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence

H^{p}(G,H^{q}(H,M))\Rightarrow H^{p+q}(H\rtimes G,M)

degenerates at the second page and the additive extensions in the reconstruction of the cohomology from the
E_{\infty}
-page are all trivial. Moreover, the additive extensions can be reconstructed in a way that is natural in all the data (
H,G,M,
the resolution
M\rightarrow M^{\bullet}
and the splittings of
Z^{p}((M^{\bullet})^{H})\rightarrow H^{p}((M^{\bullet})^{H})
).

Proof.

The Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence for
H\rtimes G
is the Grothendieck spectral sequence associated to the composition

(H\rtimes G)-\operatorname{Mod}\xrightarrow{F_{H}}G-\operatorname{Mod}\xrightarrow{F_{G}}\mathcal{A}b,

where the first functor is
F_{H}:M\mapsto M^{H}
, and the second is
F_{G}:M\mapsto M^{G}
.

The resolution
M\rightarrow M^{\bullet}
is a right resolution of
M
in
(H\rtimes G)
-modules that are
H
-acyclic, so it computes
RF_{H}
and we have
RF_{H}(M)=((M^{\bullet})^{H})
. The splittings
s_{p}:H^{p}(H,M)=H^{p}((M^{\bullet})^{H})\rightarrow Z^{p}((M^{\bullet})^{H}),\,p\geq 0
given by our hypothesis provide a chain map of complexes of
G
-modules

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{p-1}((M^{\bullet})^{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{p}((M^{\bullet})^{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{p+1}((M^{\bullet})^{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{p-1}((M^{\bullet})^{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{p}((M^{\bullet})^{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{p+1}((M^{\bullet})^{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{p-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{p+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

that is a quasi-isomorphism by construction, and so in fact
RF_{H}(M)\cong(H^{\bullet}(H,M))
, where the complex
H^{\bullet}(H,M)
has trivial differentials. The Grothendieck spectral sequence under consideration coincides with the hypercohomology spectral sequence
{H}^{p}(G,H^{q}(RF_{H}(M)))\Rightarrow{H}^{p+q}(G,RF_{H}(M))
, and the hypercohomology spectral sequence of a complex with trivial differentials degenerates at the second page and gives trivial extensions at the
E_{\infty}
-page.
∎

Lemma C.3
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring,
G_{1},G_{2}
be profinite groups, and, for
i=1,2
,
M_{i}
be
R[G_{i}]
-modules. If
M_{2}
and
(M_{1})^{G_{1}}
are
R
-flat, then
(M_{1}\otimes_{R}M_{2})^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}=M_{1}^{G_{1}}\otimes_{R}M_{2}^{G_{2}}
.
If, additionally,
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are
G_{1}
- and
G_{2}
-acyclic, respectively, then
M_{1}\otimes_{R}M_{2}
is
G_{1}\times G_{2}
-acylic.

Proof.

In general, if
G
is a profinite group,
M
an
R[G]
-module, and
N
is an
R
-flat constant module, then
H^{n}(G,M\otimes_{R}N)=H^{n}(G,M)\otimes_{R}N
for all
n\geq 0
(as follows by tensoring by
N
the complex
C^{\bullet}(G,M)
computing the cohomology of
M
). The case
n=0
gives
(M\otimes_{R}N)^{G}=M^{G}\otimes_{R}N
. So
(M_{1}\otimes_{R}M_{2})^{G_{1}}=M_{1}^{G_{1}}\otimes_{R}M_{2}
and
(M_{1}^{G_{1}}\otimes_{R}M_{2})^{G_{2}}=M_{1}^{G_{1}}\otimes_{R}M_{2}^{G_{2}}
, which combine to the first statement.

For the second part, consider the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence
H^{p}(G_{2},\linebreak H^{q}(G_{1},M_{1}\otimes_{R}M_{2}))\Rightarrow H^{p+q}(G_{1}\times G_{2},M_{1}\otimes_{R}M_{2})
. If
q>0
, we have
H^{q}(G_{1},M_{1}\otimes_{R}M_{2})=H^{q}(G_{1},M_{1})\otimes_{R}M_{2}=0
. If
q=0
and
p>0
, we have
H^{p}(G_{2},H^{q}(G_{1},M_{1}\otimes_{R}M_{2}))=H^{p}(G_{2},(M_{1})^{G_{1}}\otimes_{R}M_{2})=(M_{1})^{G_{1}}\otimes_{R}H^{p}(G_{2},M_{2})=0
. So
H^{p}(G_{2},H^{q}(G_{1},\linebreak M_{1}\otimes_{R}M_{2}))=0
unless
p=q=0
, and the spectral sequence gives the statement.
∎

Proof of Theorem
C.1
.

The proof of Theorem
C.1
is an application of Lemma
C.2
.

Let
R\rightarrow C^{\bullet}
be any resolution into
H
-acyclic flat
R
-modules such that
(C^{n})^{H}
is
R
-flat for every
n\geq 0
. (For instance, take the standard resolution
R\rightarrow X^{\bullet}(H,R)
.) Then
R\rightarrow D^{\bullet}:=(C^{\bullet})^{\otimes Y}
is a resolution in flat
R
-modules, and is
H^{Y}
-acylic by Lemma
C.3
.
Let
\mathbf{Z}(C^{\bullet}):=\oplus_{p}Z^{p}((C^{\bullet})^{H})
and
\mathbf{H}(C^{\bullet}):=\oplus_{p}H^{p}((C^{\bullet})^{H})
. Define analogously
\mathbf{Z}(D^{\bullet})
and
\mathbf{H}(D^{\bullet})
. The resolutions
C^{\bullet}
and
D^{\bullet}
are, respectively,
H
- and
H^{Y}
-acyclic, so
\mathbf{H}(C^{\bullet})=\oplus_{p\geq 0}H^{p}(H,R)
and
\mathbf{H}(D^{\bullet})=\oplus_{p\geq 0}H^{p}(H^{Y},R)
.

By assumption, all the graded pieces of
\mathbf{H}(C^{\bullet})
are
R
-projective, hence the natural surjection
\mathbf{Z}(C^{\bullet})\rightarrow\mathbf{H}(C^{\bullet})
admits a graded section
s:\mathbf{H}(C^{\bullet})\rightarrow\mathbf{Z}(C^{\bullet})
.
Consider now the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{H}(C^{\bullet})^{\otimes Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Z}(C^{\bullet})^{\otimes Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{H}(D^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Z}(D^{\bullet})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\otimes Y}}

where the vertical maps are cross-products. The left one is an isomorphism by Künneth’s theorem
[
49
, Theorem 3.6.3]
, which applies since the
R
-module
\mathbf{H}(C^{\bullet})
is
R
-projective and hence
R
-flat. The bottom composition is now a graded section of
\mathbf{Z}(D^{\bullet})\rightarrow\mathbf{H}(D^{\bullet})
that is equivariant under permutations of
Y
, and so in particular under the action of
G
.

So the resolution
R\rightarrow D^{\bullet}
is a resolution in
H^{Y}
-acyclics with a
G
-action, and the surjection
\mathbf{Z}(D^{\bullet})\rightarrow\mathbf{H}(D^{\bullet})
admits a
G
-equivariant graded section. Applying the exact functor
\otimes_{R}M
to the resolution
R\rightarrow D^{\bullet}
, we obtain a resolution of
M\rightarrow D^{\bullet}\otimes_{R}M
with the same properties, and we conclude by Lemma
C.2
.

The naturality holds since, for fixed
H
and
R
, both
C^{\bullet}
and
s
can be fixed, and the rest of the proof is natural in all the data.
∎
